The Limiting Absorption Principle for Massless Dirac Operators, Properties of Spectral Shift Functions, and an Application to the Witten Index of Non-Fredholm Operators Thanks: A.C., G.L., and F.S. gratefully acknowledge financial support from the Australian Research Council.
Alan Carey, Fritz Gesztesy, Galina Levitina, Roger Nichols, Fedor Sukochev, Dmitriy Zanin

Abstract
. Applying the theory of strongly smooth operators, we derive a limiting absorption principle (LAP) on any compact interval in ℝ \ { 0 } {\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\} for the free massless Dirac operator, H 0 = α ⋅ ( − i ∇ ) H_{0}=\alpha\cdot(-i\nabla) in [ L 2 ​ ( ℝ n ) ] N [L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N} , n ∈ ℕ n\in{\mathbb{N}} , n ⩾ 2 n\geqslant 2 , N = 2 ⌊ ( n + 1 ) / 2 ⌋ N=2^{\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor} . We then use this to demonstrate the absence of singular continuous spectrum of interacting massless Dirac operators H = H 0 + V H=H_{0}+V , where the entries of the (essentially bounded) matrix-valued potential V V decay like O ⁡ ( | x | − 1 − ε ) O\big(|x|^{-1-\varepsilon}\big) as | x | → ∞ |x|\to\infty for some ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . This includes the special case of electromagnetic potentials decaying at the same rate. In addition, we derive a one-to-one correspondence between embedded eigenvalues of H H in ℝ \ { 0 } {\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\} and the eigenvalue − 1 -1 of the (normal boundary values of the) Birman–Schwinger-type operator V 2 ​ ( H 0 − ( λ 0 ± i ​ 0 ) ​ I [ L 2 ​ ( ℝ n ) ] N ) − 1 ​ V 1 ∗ ¯ . \overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(\lambda_{0}\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}. Upon expressing ξ ( ⋅ ; H , H 0 ) \xi(\,\cdot\,;H,H_{0}) as normal boundary values of regularized Fredholm determinants to the real axis, we then prove that in the concrete case ( H , H 0 ) (H,H_{0}) , under appropriate hypotheses on V V (implying the decay of V V like O ⁡ ( | x | − n − 1 − ε ) O\big(|x|^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big) as | x | → ∞ |x|\to\infty ), the associated spectral shift function satisfies ξ ( ⋅ ; H , H 0 ) ∈ C ( ( − ∞ , 0 ) ∪ ( 0 , ∞ ) ) \xi(\,\cdot\,;H,H_{0})\in C((-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)) , and that the left and right limits at zero, ξ ⁡ ( 0 ± , H , H 0 ) = lim ε ↓ 0 ξ ⁡ ( ± ε , H , H 0 ) \xi(0_{\pm};H,H_{0})=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\xi(\pm\varepsilon;H,H_{0}) , exist. This fact is then used to express the resolvent regularized Witten index of the non-Fredholm operator 𝑫 𝑨 {\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}} in L 2 ​ ( ℝ , [ L 2 ​ ( ℝ n ) ] N ) L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big) given by 𝑫 𝑨 = d d ​ t + 𝑨 , dom ⁡ ( 𝑫 𝑨 ) = W 1 , 2 ​ ( ℝ , [ L 2 ​ ( ℝ n ) ] N ) ∩ dom ⁡ ( 𝑨 − ) , {\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}=\frac{d}{dt}+{\bm{A}},\quad\dom({\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}})=W^{1,2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\cap\dom({\bm{A}}_{-}), where 𝑨 = 𝑨 − + 𝑩 , dom ⁡ ( 𝑨 ) = dom ⁡ ( 𝑨 − ) . {\bm{A}}={\bm{A}}_{-}+{\bm{B}},\quad\dom({\bm{A}})=\dom({\bm{A}}_{-}). Here 𝑨 {\bm{A}} , 𝑨 − {\bm{A}}_{-} , 𝑨 + {\bm{A}}_{+} , 𝑩 {\bm{B}} , and 𝑩 + {\bm{B}}_{+} in L 2 ​ ( ℝ , [ L 2 ​ ( ℝ n ) ] N ) L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big) are generated with the help of the Dirac-type operators H , H 0 H,H_{0} and potential matrices V V , via A ⁡ ( t ) = A − + B ⁡ ( t ) , t ∈ ℝ , A − = H 0 , A + = A − + B + = H , \displaystyle A(t)=A_{-}+B(t),\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},\quad A_{-}=H_{0},\quad A_{+}=A_{-}+B_{+}=H, B ⁡ ( t ) = b ⁡ ( t ) ​ B + , t ∈ ℝ , B + = V , \displaystyle B(t)=b(t)B_{+},\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},\quad B_{+}=V, in [ L 2 ​ ( ℝ n ) ] N [L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N} , assuming b ( k ) ∈ C ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) ∩ L ∞ ​ ( ℝ , d ​ t ) , k ∈ ℕ 0 , b ′ ∈ L 1 ​ ( ℝ , d ​ t ) , \displaystyle b^{(k)}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}};dt),\;k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0},\quad b^{\prime}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};dt), lim t → ∞ b ⁡ ( t ) = 1 , lim t → − ∞ b ⁡ ( t ) = 0 . \displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}b(t)=1,\quad\lim_{t\to-\infty}b(t)=0. In particular, A ± A_{\pm} are the asymptotes of the family A ⁡ ( t ) A(t) , t ∈ ℝ t\in{\mathbb{R}} , as t → ± ∞ t\to\pm\infty in the norm resolvent sense. ( Here L 2 ​ ( ℝ , ℋ ) = ∫ ℝ ⊕ d ​ t ​ ℋ L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}})=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{\oplus}dt\,{\mathcal{H}} and 𝑻 = ∫ ℝ ⊕ d ​ t ​ T ​ ( t ) {\bm{T}}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{\oplus}dt\,T(t) represent direct integrals of Hilbert spaces and operators. ) Introducing the nonnegative, self-adjoint operators 𝑯 1 = 𝑫 𝑨 ∗ ​ 𝑫 𝑨 , 𝑯 2 = 𝑫 𝑨 ​ 𝑫 𝑨 ∗ . {\bm{H}}_{1}={\bm{D}}_{{\bm{A}}}^{*}{\bm{D}}_{{\bm{A}}},\quad{\bm{H}}_{2}={\bm{D}}_{{\bm{A}}}{\bm{D}}_{{\bm{A}}}^{*}. in L 2 ​ ( ℝ , [ L 2 ​ ( ℝ n ) ] N ) L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big) , one of the principal results proved in this manuscript expresses the resolvent regularized Witten index W k , r ​ ( 𝑫 𝑨 ) W_{k,r}({\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}) of 𝑫 𝑨 {\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}} in terms of spectral shift functions via W k , r ​ ( 𝑫 𝑨 ) = ξ L ​ ( 0 + , 𝑯 2 , 𝑯 1 ) \displaystyle W_{k,r}({\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}})=\xi_{L}(0_{+};{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1}) = [ ξ ⁡ ( 0 + , H , H 0 ) + ξ ⁡ ( 0 − , H , H 0 ) ] / 2 , \displaystyle=[\xi(0_{+};H,H_{0})+\xi(0_{-};H,H_{0})]/2, k ∈ ℕ , k ⩾ ⌈ n / 2 ⌉ . \displaystyle\hskip 65.44142ptk\in{\mathbb{N}},\;k\geqslant\lceil n/2\rceil. Here the notation ξ L ​ ( 0 + , 𝑯 2 , 𝑯 1 ) \xi_{L}(0_{+};{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1}) indicates that 0 0 is a right Lebesgue point for ξ ( ⋅ ; 𝑯 2 , 𝑯 1 ) \xi(\,\cdot\,;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1}) , and W k , r ​ ( T ) W_{k,r}(T) represents the k k th resolvent regularized Witten index of the densely defined, closed operator T T in the complex, separable Hilbert space 𝒦 {\mathcal{K}} , defined by W k , r ​ ( T ) = lim λ ↑ 0 ( − λ ) k ​ tr 𝒦 ⁡ ( ( T ∗ ​ T − λ ​ I 𝒦 ) − k − ( T ​ T ∗ − λ ​ I 𝒦 ) − k ) , W_{k,r}(T)=\lim_{\lambda\uparrow 0}(-\lambda)^{k}\tr_{{\mathcal{K}}}\big((T^{*}T-\lambda I_{{\mathcal{K}}})^{-k}-(TT^{*}-\lambda I_{{\mathcal{K}}})^{-k}\big), whenever the limit exists for some k ∈ ℕ k\in{\mathbb{N}} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 35P25, 35Q40, 81Q10; Secondary: 47A10, 47A40.

1.
Introduction

The primary motivation writing this manuscript was to express the Witten index of a certain class
of non-Fredholm operators, generated from multi-dimensional, massless Dirac operators, in terms of appropriate underlying spectral shift functions. This goal necessitated a detailed control over continuity properties (more precisely, the existence of Lebesgue points) for the spectral shift functions involved, and hence the bulk of this manuscript is devoted to an exhaustive investigation of the spectral properties of multi-dimensional, massless Dirac operators.

To set the stage, let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
n\geqslant 2
,
N=2^{\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor}
, and denote by
\alpha_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
,

\alpha_{n+1}:=\beta
,
n+1
anti-commuting Hermitian
N\times N
matrices with squares equal to
I_{N}
, that is,

\alpha_{j}^{*}=\alpha_{j},\quad\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}+\alpha_{k}\alpha_{j}=2\delta_{j,k}I_{N},\quad 1\leqslant j,k\leqslant n+1,

(1.1)

and introduce in
[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
the free massless Dirac operator

H_{0}=\alpha\cdot(-i\nabla)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}(-i\partial_{j}),\quad\dom(H_{0})=[W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},

(1.2)

where
\partial_{j}=\partial/\partial x_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
. Introducing the self-adjoint matrix-valued potential

V=\{V_{\ell,m}\}_{1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N}
satisfying for some fixed
\rho\in(1,\infty)
,
C\in(0,\infty)
,

V\in[L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N\times N},\quad|V_{\ell,m}(x)|\leqslant C\langle x\rangle^{-\rho}\,\text{ for a.e.~$x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}$, $1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N$,}

(1.3)

then permits one to introduce the massless Dirac operator
H
in
[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
via

H=H_{0}+V,\quad\dom(H)=\dom(H_{0})=[W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}.

(1.4)

In this context we recall our convention

[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n};{\mathbb{C}}^{N}),\quad[W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}=W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n};{\mathbb{C}}^{N}),\,\text{ etc.,}

(1.5)

to be used throughout.

Then
H_{0}
and
H
are self-adjoint in
[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
,
with essential spectrum covering the
entire real line,

\sigma_{ess}(H)=\sigma_{ess}(H_{0})=\sigma(H_{0})={\mathbb{R}},

(1.6)

In addition,

\sigma_{ac}(H_{0})={\mathbb{R}},\quad\sigma_{p}(H_{0})=\sigma_{sc}(H_{0})=\emptyset.

(1.7)

Relying on the theory of (local) Kato-smoothness (see, e.g.,

[
140
, Sect. XIII.7]
,
[
183
, Ch. 4]
, and
[
185
, Chs. 0–2]
) and its variant, strong (local) Kato-smoothness (see, e.g.,
[
183
, Ch. 4]
,
[
185
, Chs. 0–2]
), then yields under the stated hypotheses on
V
that actually,

\displaystyle\sigma_{ess}(H)=\sigma_{ac}(H)={\mathbb{R}},

(1.8)

\displaystyle\sigma_{sc}(H)=\emptyset,

(1.9)

\displaystyle\sigma_{s}(H)\cap({\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\})=\sigma_{p}(H)\cap({\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}),

(1.10)

with the only possible accumulation points of
\sigma_{p}(H)
being
0
and
\pm\infty
. Relations
(
1.8
)–(
1.10
) describe only the tip of the proverbial iceberg in connection with Sections
2
and
3
. In fact, leading up to (
1.10
) we establish a limiting absorption principle (LAP) on any compact interval in
{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
for the free (i.e., non-interacting) massless Dirac operator

H_{0}
, prove the absence of singular continuous spectrum of
H=H_{0}+V
for matrix elements

V_{\ell,m}
,
1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N
, of
V
decaying like
O\big(|x|^{-1-\varepsilon}\big)
as
|x|\to\infty
for some
\varepsilon>0
, derive Hölder continuity of the boundary values

(H_{0}-(\lambda\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}
in appropriate weighted
L^{2}
-spaces for
\lambda
varying in compact subintervals of
{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
, and derive Hölder continuity of the boundary values
(H-(\lambda\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}
in appropriate weighted
L^{2}
-spaces for

\lambda
varying in compact subintervals of
{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
away from the possibly embedded eigenvalues of
H
. In particular, factoring
V
into
V=V_{1}^{*}V_{2}
, we derive a
1-1
-correspondence between embedded eigenvalues of
H
in
{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
and the eigenvalue
-1
of the (normal boundary values of the) Birman–Schwinger-type operator
\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(\lambda_{0}\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}
.

This leaves open the existence of eigenvalues embedded in the essential spectrum, and particularly, the existence of an eigenvalue
0
. To deal with these situations one follows
[
103
, Theorems 2.3]
and assumes in addition that

V:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
is Lebesgue measurable and self-adjoint
a.e. on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, and that

for some
R>0
,
V\in\big[C^{1}(E_{R})\big]^{N\times N}
, where

E_{R}=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,|\,|x|>R\}
,

(1.11)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|x|^{1/2}V_{\ell,m}(x)\underset{|x|\to\infty}{=}o(1),\quad(x\cdot\nabla V_{\ell,m})(x)\underset{|x|\to\infty}{=}o(1),\quad 1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N,\\
&\quad\text{uniformly with respect to directions.}\end{split}

(1.12)

Under all these conditions on
V
one then obtains

\sigma_{p}(H)\subseteq\{0\}.

(1.13)

This still leaves open the possibility of an eigenvalue
0
. To exclude that as well

[
103
, Theorems 2.1]
assume in addition that

\esssup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|x|\|V(x)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}\leqslant C\,\text{ for some $C\in(0,(n-1)/2)$,}

(1.14)

with
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}
denoting the operator norm of an
N\times N
matrix in
{\mathbb{C}}^{N}
. Then finally,

\sigma_{p}(H)=\emptyset,

(1.15)

and hence
H
and
H_{0}
are unitarily equivalent under these conditions on
V
. The facts (
1.13
) and (
1.15
) are discussed in detail Section
4
.

We emphasize, however, that in the bulk of this manuscript we will not assume (
1.14
) as we explicitly intend to include situations with
0
an eigenvalue (and/or a threshold resonance) of
H
.

Section
5
provides a detailed study of the Green’s function (matrix) of the free Dirac operator
H_{0}
, that is, the integral kernel of the resolvent
(H-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}
, in terms of the Hankel function of order
1
and half integer index
(n-2)/2
and
n/2
,

\displaystyle G_{0}(z;x,y):=(H_{0}-zI)^{-1}(x,y)

\displaystyle\quad=i4^{-1}(2\pi)^{(2-n)/2}|x-y|^{2-n}z\,[z|x-y|]^{(n-2)/2}H_{(n-2)/2}^{(1)}(z|x-y|)I_{N}

(1.16)

\displaystyle\qquad-4^{-1}(2\pi)^{(2-n)/2}|x-y|^{1-n}[z|x-y|]^{n/2}H_{n/2}^{(1)}(z|x-y|)\,\alpha\cdot\frac{(x-y)}{|x-y|}.

The Green’s function
G_{0}(z;\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
of
H_{0}
continuously extends to
z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}
, in particular, the limit
z\to 0
exists,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\to 0,\\
z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\backslash\{0\}\end{subarray}}G_{0}(z;x,y):=G_{0}(0+i\,0;x,y)\\
&\quad=i2^{-1}\pi^{-n/2}\Gamma(n/2)\,\alpha\cdot\frac{(x-y)}{|x-y|^{n}},\quad x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\;x\neq y,\;n\in{\mathbb{N}},\;n\geqslant 2,\end{split}

(1.17)

and no blow up occurs for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
n\geqslant 2
.

This section ends with various boundedness properties of integral operators
R_{0,\delta}
and

R_{0,\delta}(z)
in
[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
,
n\geqslant 2
, associated with integral kernels that are bounded entrywise by

|R_{0,\delta}(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)_{j,k}|\leqslant C\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\delta}|G_{0}(0;\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)_{j,k}|\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\delta},\quad\delta\geqslant 1/2,\;1\leqslant j,k\leqslant N,

(1.18)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}|R_{0,\delta}(z;\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)_{j,k}|\leqslant C\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\delta}|G_{0}(z;\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)_{j,k}|\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\delta},&\\
\delta\geqslant(n+1)/4,\;z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}},\;1\leqslant j,k\leqslant N,\end{split}

(1.19)

for some
C\in(0,\infty)
. In particular, we prove that

\displaystyle R_{0,\delta}\in{\mathcal{B}}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad\delta\geqslant 1/2,

(1.20)

\displaystyle R_{0,\delta}(z)\in{\mathcal{B}}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad\delta>(n+1)/4,\;z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}.

(1.21)

Section
6
takes the boundedness property of
R_{0,\delta}
and
R_{0,\delta}(z)
a step further by proving trace ideal properties. In fact, employing interpolation techniques for trace ideals, we prove, among a variety of related results, that for
n\geqslant 2
and
\delta>(n+1)/4
,

R_{0,\delta}(z)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad p>n,\;z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}.

(1.22)

Since we are dealing with
n
-dimensional Dirac operators,
n\geqslant 2
, the study of resolvents alone is insufficient and certain
n
-dependent powers of the resolvent of
H_{0}
and
H
naturally enter the analysis. As a result, in Section
7
we prove that for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
k\geqslant n
,

\big[(H-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N})})^{-k}-(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N})})^{-k}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(1.23)

as well as for all
\varepsilon>0
,

V\big(H_{0}^{2}+I_{[L^{2}[{\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N})}\big)^{-(n/2)-\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),

(1.24)

now assuming additional decay of
V
of the type, for some
\varepsilon>0
,

V\in[L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N\times N},\quad|V_{\ell,m}(x)|\leqslant C\langle x\rangle^{-n-\varepsilon}\,\text{ for a.e.~$x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}$, $1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N$.}

(1.25)

The next two sections, Sections
8
and
9
, are devoted to the notion of the spectral shift function for a pair of self-adjoint operators
(S,S_{0})
in
{\mathcal{H}}
, particularly building on work of Yafaev
[
184
]
: We start by introducing the class of functions
{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}({\mathbb{R}})
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, by

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}({\mathbb{R}}):=\big\{f\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})\,\big|\,f^{(\ell)}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}});\text{ there exists }\varepsilon>0\text{ and }f_{0}=f_{0}(f)\in{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{ such that }\big(d^{\ell}/d\lambda^{\ell}\big)\big[f(\lambda)-f_{0}\lambda^{-m}\big]\underset{|\lambda|\to\infty}{=}O\big(|\lambda|^{-\ell-m-\varepsilon}\big),\;\ell=0,1,2\big\}

(1.26)

(it is implied that
f_{0}=f_{0}(f)
is the same as
\lambda\to\pm\infty
); one observes that

C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathfrak{F}_{m}(\mathbb{R})
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Assuming that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\dom(S)=\dom(S_{0}),\quad(S-S_{0})\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}),\\
&\text{for some $0<\varepsilon<1/2$, }\,(S-S_{0})(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-(m/2)-\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),\end{split}

(1.27)

the following are then the principal results of Section
8
: Let
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then

[f(S)-f(S_{0})]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad\in{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}({\mathbb{R}}),

(1.28)

and there exists a function

\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})\in L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\lambda|)^{-m-1}\,d\lambda\big)

(1.29)

such that the following trace formula holds,

\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}(f(S)-f(S_{0}))=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})f^{\prime}(\lambda),\quad f\in{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}({\mathbb{R}}).

(1.30)

In particular, one has

\big[(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}-(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(1.31)

and

\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\big((S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}-(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}\big)=-m\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})d\lambda}{(\lambda-z)^{m+1}},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

(1.32)

The following Section
9
then derives an explicit representation for the spectral shift function

\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})
in terms of normal boundary values to the real axis of regularized Fredholm determinants as follows: Slightly extending our set of hypotheses and now assuming that

S_{0}
and
S
are self-adjoint operators in
{\mathcal{H}}
with
(S-S_{0})\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
, we suppose in addition the following two conditions:

(i)
If
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
is odd, assume that

\big[(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}-(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(1.33)

and

(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/j}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad j\in{\mathbb{N}},\;1\leqslant j\leqslant m+1.

(1.34)

(ii)
If
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
is even, assume that for some
0<\varepsilon<1/2
,

(S-S_{0})\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-(m/2)-\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(1.35)

(In this case one can show that (
1.34
) holds as well).

Introducing

F_{S,S_{0}}(z):=\text{\rm ln}\big(\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},m+1}\big((S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big)\big),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(1.36)

where
(\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},m+1}(\,\cdot\,)
denotes the
(m+1)
st regularized Fredholm determinant, and
introducing the analytic function
G_{S,S_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)
in
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\displaystyle\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}G_{S,S_{0}}(z)

\displaystyle\quad=\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\Bigg(\frac{d^{m-1}}{dz^{m-1}}\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(-1)^{m-j}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big[(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big]^{m-j}\Bigg),

\displaystyle\hskip 270.30118ptz\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(1.37)

the main result of Section
9
then reads as follows: If
F_{S,S_{0}}
and
G_{S,S_{0}}
have normal (or nontangential) boundary values on
{\mathbb{R}}
, then for a.e.
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})=\pi^{-1}\text{\rm Im}(F_{S,S_{0}}(\lambda+i0))-\pi^{-1}\text{\rm Im}(G_{S,S_{0}}(\lambda+i0))+P_{m-1}(\lambda)\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}$},

(1.38)

where
P_{m-1}
is a polynomial of degree less than or equal to
m-1
.

The subsequent two sections then analyze (
1.36
) and (
1
) and their normal boundary
values to the real axis in the concrete case where
S=H
and
S_{0}=H_{0}
.

More precisely, Section
10

establishes continuity properties of
\text{\rm Im}(F_{H,H_{0}}(\lambda+i0))
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, by invoking a lengthy study of threshold spectral properties of
H
, following an approach by Jensen and Nenciu
[
98
]
, and, especially, by Erdoğan, Goldberg, and Green
[
59
]
,
[
60
]
,
[
64
]
,
[
65
]
. In particular, we recall an exhaustive study of eigenvalues
0
and /or resonances at
0
and finally prove that
under assumptions (
1.11
), (
1.12
), and (
1.25
),

F_{H,H_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)
, has normal boundary values on
{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
. In addition, the boundary values to
{\mathbb{R}}

of the function
\text{\rm Im}(F_{H,H_{0}}(z))
,
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, are continuous on
(-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)
,

\text{\rm Im}(F_{H,H_{0}}(\lambda+i0))\in C((-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)),

(1.39)

and the left and right limits at zero,

\text{\rm Im}(F_{H,H_{0}}(0_{\pm}+i0))=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\text{\rm Im}(F_{H,H_{0}}(\pm\varepsilon+i0)),

(1.40)

exist. In particular, if
0
is a regular point for
H
(i.e., in the absence of any zero energy eigenvalue and resonance of
H
), then

\text{\rm Im}(F_{H,H_{0}}(\lambda+i0))\in C({\mathbb{R}}).

(1.41)

Under the following strengthened decay assumption on
V
, for some
\varepsilon>0
,

V\in[L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N\times N},\quad|V_{\ell,m}(x)|\leqslant C\langle x\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\,\text{ for a.e.~$x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}$, $1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N$,}

(1.42)

an unrelenting barrage of estimates finally proves in Section
11
that if

n\in{\mathbb{N}}
is odd,
n\geqslant 3
, then
\frac{d^{n}}{dz^{n}}G_{H,H_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)
is analytic in
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and
continuous in
\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}
. If
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
is even, then
\frac{d^{n}}{dz^{n}}G_{H,H_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)
is analytic in

{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, continuous in
\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\backslash\{0\}
. Moreover, if
n\geqslant 4
, then

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{d^{n}}{dz^{n}}G_{H,H_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)\bigg\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}z\to 0,\\
z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\backslash\{0\}\end{subarray}}{=}O\big(|z|^{-[n-(n/(n-1))]}\big),

(1.43)

and if
n=2
, then for any
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{d^{2}}{dz^{2}}G_{H,H_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)\bigg\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{2})}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}z\to 0,\\
z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\backslash\{0\}\end{subarray}}{=}O\big(|z|^{-(1+\delta)}\big).

(1.44)

Thus, combining (
1.38
)–(
1.41
), (
1.43
), (
1.44
) finally yields the first principal
result of Section
12
in the following form:
Under the hypotheses (
1.11
), (
1.12
), and (
1.42
),

\xi(\,\cdot\,;H,H_{0})\in C((-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)),

(1.45)

and the left and right limits at zero,

\xi(0_{\pm};H,H_{0})=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\xi(\pm\varepsilon;H,H_{0}),

(1.46)

exist. In particular, if
0
is a regular point for
H
, then

\xi(\,\cdot\,;H,H_{0})\in C({\mathbb{R}}).

(1.47)

This represents the main spectral theoretic result derived in this manuscript. The remainder of Section
12
then describes our application to the (resolvent regularized) Witten index of a particular class of
non-Fredholm operators in the space
L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
in connection with multi-dimensional, massless Dirac operators.

This requires some preparations to which we turn next. We recall a bit of notation: Linear operators in the Hilbert space
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};dt;{\mathcal{H}})
, in short,
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}})
, will be denoted by calligraphic boldface symbols of the type
{\bm{T}}
, to distinguish them from operators
T
in
{\mathcal{H}}
. In particular, operators denoted by
{\bm{T}}
in the Hilbert space
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}})
represent operators associated with a
family of operators
\{T(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
in
{\mathcal{H}}
, defined by

\displaystyle({\bm{T}}f)(t)=T(t)f(t)\,\text{ for a.e.\ $t\in{\mathbb{R}}$,}

\displaystyle f\in\dom({\bm{T}})=\bigg\{g\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}})\,\bigg|\,g(t)\in\dom(T(t))\text{ for a.e.\ }t\in{\mathbb{R}};

(1.48)

\displaystyle\quad t\mapsto T(t)g(t)\text{ is (weakly) measurable;}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}dt\,\|T(t)g(t)\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}<\infty\bigg\}.

In the special case, where
\{T(t)\}
is a family of bounded operators on
{\mathcal{H}}
with

\sup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\|T(t)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}<\infty
, the associated operator
{\bm{T}}
is a bounded operator on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}})
with
\|{\bm{T}}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}}))}=\sup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\|T(t)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}
.

For brevity we will abbreviate
{\bm{I}}:=I_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}})}
in the following and note that in the concrete situation of
n
-dimensional, massless Dirac operators at hand,
{\mathcal{H}}=[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
.

Denoting

A_{-}=H_{0},\quad B_{+}=V,\quad A_{+}=A_{-}+B_{+}=H,

(1.49)

we introduce two families of operators in

[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&B(t)=b(t)B_{+},\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}},\\
&b^{(k)}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}};dt),\;k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0},\quad b^{\prime}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};dt),\\
&\lim_{t\to\infty}b(t)=1,\quad\lim_{t\to-\infty}b(t)=0,\\
&A(t)=A_{-}+B(t),\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.\end{split}

(1.50)

Next, following the general setups described in
[
38
]
,
[
41
]
–
[
44
]
,

[
77
]
,
[
136
]
, the operators

{\bm{A}}
,
{\bm{B}},{\bm{A}}^{\prime}={\bm{B}}^{\prime}
are now given in terms of the families
A(t)
,
B(t)
, and
B^{\prime}(t)
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, as in (
1.48
). In addition,
{\bm{A}}_{-}
in
L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
represents
the self-adjoint (constant fiber) operator defined by

\displaystyle({\bm{A}}_{-}f)(t)=A_{-}f(t)\,\text{ for a.e.\ $t\in{\mathbb{R}}$,}

\displaystyle f\in\dom({\bm{A}}_{-})=\bigg\{g\in L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\,\bigg|\,g(t)\in\dom(A_{-})\text{ for a.e.\ }t\in{\mathbb{R}},

\displaystyle\quad t\mapsto A_{-}g(t)\text{ is (weakly) measurable,}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}dt\,\|A_{-}g(t)\|_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}^{2}<\infty\bigg\}.

(1.51)

At this point one can introduce the fundamental operator
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
in

L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
by

{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}=\frac{d}{dt}+{\bm{A}},\quad\dom({\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}})=W^{1,2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\cap\dom({\bm{A}}_{-}),

(1.52)

where

\displaystyle{\bm{A}}={\bm{A}}_{-}+{\bm{B}},\quad\dom({\bm{A}})=\dom({\bm{A}}_{-}),

(1.53)

\displaystyle{\bm{B}}\in{\mathcal{B}}\big(L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\big).

(1.54)

Here the operator
d/dt
in
L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
is defined by

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{d}{dt}f\bigg)(t)=f^{\prime}(t)\,\text{ for a.e.\ $t\in{\mathbb{R}}$,}\quad f\in\dom(d/dt)=W^{1,2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big).

(1.55)

Since
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
is densely defined and closed in
L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
, one can introduce the nonnegative, self-adjoint operators

{\bm{H}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, in
L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
by

{\bm{H}}_{1}={\bm{D}}_{{\bm{A}}}^{*}{\bm{D}}_{{\bm{A}}},\quad{\bm{H}}_{2}={\bm{D}}_{{\bm{A}}}{\bm{D}}_{{\bm{A}}}^{*}.

(1.56)

Introducing the operator
{\bm{H}}_{0}
in
L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
by

{\bm{H}}_{0}=-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+{\bm{A}}_{-}^{2},\quad\dom({\bm{H}}_{0})=W^{2,2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\cap\dom\big({\bm{A}}_{-}^{2}\big),

(1.57)

then
{\bm{H}}_{0}
is self-adjoint and one obtains the following decomposition of the operators
{\bm{H}}_{j}
,

j=1,2
,

\displaystyle{\bm{H}}_{j}=-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+{\bm{A}}^{2}+(-1)^{j}{\bm{A}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 15.649pt={\bm{H}}_{0}+{\bm{B}}{\bm{A}}_{-}+{\bm{A}}_{-}{\bm{B}}+{\bm{B}}^{2}+(-1)^{j}{\bm{B}}^{\prime},

(1.58)

\displaystyle\,\dom({\bm{H}}_{j})=\dom({\bm{H}}_{0}),\quad j=1,2.

Next, we turn to a canonical approximation procedure: Consider the characteristic function for the interval
[-\ell,\ell]\subset{\mathbb{R}}
,

\chi_{\ell}(\nu)=\chi_{[-\ell,\ell]}(\nu),\quad\nu\in{\mathbb{R}},\;\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(1.59)

and hence
\slim_{\ell\to\infty}\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})=I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}
.
Introducing

\displaystyle\begin{split}&A_{\ell}(t)=A_{-}+\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B(t)\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})=A_{-}+B_{\ell}(t),\\
&\dom(A_{\ell}(t))=\dom(A_{-}),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},\\
&B_{\ell}(t)=\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B(t)\chi_{\ell}(A_{-}),\quad\dom(B_{\ell}(t))=[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},\\
&A_{+,\ell}=A_{-}+\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B_{+}\chi_{\ell}(A_{-}),\quad\dom(A_{+,\ell})=\dom(A_{-}),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\\
\end{split}

(1.60)

one concludes that

\displaystyle A_{+,\ell}-A_{-}=\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B_{+}\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(1.61)

\displaystyle A_{\ell}^{\prime}(t)=B_{\ell}^{\prime}(t)=\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B^{\prime}(t)\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(1.62)

As a consequence of (
1.61
), the spectral shift functions

\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, exist and are uniquely determined by

\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};d\nu),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(1.63)

We also note the analogous decompositions,

\displaystyle{\bm{H}}_{j,\ell}=-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+{\bm{A}}_{\ell}^{2}+(-1)^{j}{\bm{A}}_{\ell}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 19.06332pt={\bm{H}}_{0}+{\bm{B}}_{\ell}{\bm{A}}_{-}+{\bm{A}}_{-}{\bm{B}}_{\ell}+{\bm{B}}_{\ell}^{2}+(-1)^{j}{\bm{B}}_{\ell}^{\prime},

(1.64)

\displaystyle\,\dom({\bm{H}}_{j,\ell})=\dom({\bm{H}}_{0})=W^{2,2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;j=1,2,

with

{\bm{B}}_{\ell}=\chi_{\ell}({\bm{A}}_{-}){\bm{B}}\chi_{\ell}({\bm{A}}_{-}),\quad{\bm{B}}_{\ell}^{\prime}=\chi_{\ell}({\bm{A}}_{-}){\bm{B}}^{\prime}\chi_{\ell}({\bm{A}}_{-}),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(1.65)

Under hypotheses (
1.11
), (
1.12
), (
1.42
), and

V_{\ell,m}\in W^{4n,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}),\quad 1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N,

(1.66)

it is proven in
[
44
]
that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&[({\bm{H}}_{2}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}-({\bm{H}}_{1}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}\big],\,\big[({\bm{H}}_{2,\ell}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}-({\bm{H}}_{1,\ell}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}\big]\\
&\quad\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big(L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\big),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;r\in{\mathbb{N}},\;r\geqslant\lceil n/2\rceil,\end{split}

(1.67)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\big\|\big[({\bm{H}}_{2,\ell}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}-({\bm{H}}_{1,\ell}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}\big]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-[({\bm{H}}_{2}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}-({\bm{H}}_{1}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}\big]\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}))}=0,

(1.68)

\displaystyle\hskip 234.73523ptz\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash[0,\infty).

Relations (
1.67
) together with the fact that
{\bm{H}}_{j}\geqslant 0
,
{\bm{H}}_{j,\ell}\geqslant 0
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
j=1,2
, implies the existence and uniqueness of spectral shift functions

\xi(\,\cdot\,;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})
and

\xi(\,\cdot\,;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})
for the pair of operators
({\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})
and

({\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, respectively, employing the normalization

\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})=0,\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})=0,\quad\lambda<0,\;\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}

(1.69)

(cf.
[
183
, Sect. 8.9]
). Moreover,

\xi(\,\cdot\,;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})\in L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\lambda|)^{-q-1}d\lambda\big).

(1.70)

Since

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}dt\,\|A_{\ell}^{\prime}(t)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}<\infty,\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(1.71)

employing
b^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,)\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};dt)
, one obtains (cf.
[
77
]
,
[
136
]
)

[({\bm{H}}_{2,\ell}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-1}-({\bm{H}}_{1,\ell}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-1}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big(L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\big),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(1.72)

and hence

\xi(\,\cdot\,;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};d\lambda),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(1.73)

In addition, (
12.26
), (
1.69
), and (
1.71
) imply the approximate trace formula,

\int_{[0,\infty)}\frac{\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})\,d\lambda}{(\lambda-z)^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{\xi(\nu;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})\,d\nu}{(\nu^{2}-z)^{3/2}},\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash[0,\infty),

(1.74)

which in turn implies

\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\lambda^{1/2}}^{\lambda^{1/2}}\frac{\xi(\nu;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})\,d\nu}{(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{1/2}},&\text{for a.e.~$\lambda>0$,}\\
0,&\lambda<0,\end{cases}\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(1.75)

via a Stieltjes inversion argument.

Given hypothesis (
1.25
), we will prove in Theorem
7.4
that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big[(A_{+}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-r_{0}}-(A_{-}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-r_{0}}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),&\\
r_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}},\;r_{0}\geqslant 2\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1,\;z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.&\end{split}

(1.76)

Since
2\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1
is always odd,
[
184
, Theorem 2.2]
yields the existence
of a spectral shift function
\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})
for the pair
(A_{+},A_{-})
satisfying

\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})\in L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\nu|)^{-q_{0}-1}d\nu\big).

(1.77)

While (
1.77
) does not determine
\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})
uniquely, one can show (following
[
39
, Theorem 4.7]
) that there exists a unique spectral shift function
\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})

given by the limiting relation

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})=\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})\,\text{ in $L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\nu|)^{-q_{0}-1}d\nu\big)$,}

(1.78)

and hence we will always choose this particular spectral shift function in (
1.78
) for the pair
(A_{+},A_{-})
in the following.

At this point one can entertain the limit
\ell\to\infty
in (
1.75
): Indeed, (
1.75
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})f^{\prime}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,f^{\prime}(\lambda)\int_{-\lambda^{1/2}}^{\lambda^{1/2}}\frac{\xi(\nu;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})\,d\nu}{(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\nu\,\xi(\nu;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})F^{\prime}(\nu),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(1.79)

where
F^{\prime}
is defined by

F^{\prime}(\nu)=\int_{\nu^{2}}^{\infty}d\lambda\,f^{\prime}(\lambda)(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{-1/2},\quad\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(1.80)

The limit
\ell\to\infty
on left-hand side of (
1.79
) is controlled via (
1.68
), and, since
F^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}})
, the right-hand side of (
1.79
) is controlled via (
1.78
), implying

\displaystyle\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})f^{\prime}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\nu\,\xi(\nu;A_{+},A_{-})F^{\prime}(\nu)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\lambda\,f^{\prime}(\lambda)\int_{-\lambda^{1/2}}^{\lambda^{1/2}}\frac{\xi(\nu;A_{+},A_{-})\,d\nu}{(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{1/2}}\chi_{[0,\infty)}(\lambda),\quad f\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}),

(1.81)

and hence

\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\lambda^{1/2}}^{\lambda^{1/2}}\frac{\xi(\nu;A_{+},A_{-})\,d\nu}{(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{1/2}}\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda>0$,}

(1.82)

due to our normalization in (
1.69
). This establishes the limiting relation
\ell\to\infty
of (
1.75
).

Having established (
1.82
), we turn to the resolvent regularized Witten index of the operator

{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
. Since
\sigma(A_{\pm})={\mathbb{R}}
, in particular,
0\notin\rho(A_{+})\cap\rho(A_{-})
,

{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
is a non-Fredholm operator. Even though
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
is a non-Fredholm operator, its Witten index is well-defined and expressible in terms of the spectral shift functions for the pair of operators

({\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})
and
(A_{+},A_{-})
as will be shown below.

To introduce an appropriately (resolvent regularized) Witten index of
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
, we consider a densely defined, closed operator
T
in the complex, separable Hilbert space
{\mathcal{K}}
and assume that for some

k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and all
\lambda<0

\big[(T^{*}T-\lambda I_{{\mathcal{K}}})^{-k}-(TT^{*}-\lambda I_{{\mathcal{K}}})^{-k}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{K}}).

(1.83)

Then the
k
th resolvent regularized Witten index of
T
is defined by

W_{k,r}(T)=\lim_{\lambda\uparrow 0}(-\lambda)^{k}\tr_{{\mathcal{K}}}\big((T^{*}T-\lambda I_{{\mathcal{K}}})^{-k}-(TT^{*}-\lambda I_{{\mathcal{K}}})^{-k}\big),

(1.84)

whenever the limit exists. The analogous semigroup regularized definition reads,

W_{s}(T)=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\tr_{{\mathcal{K}}}\big(e^{-tT^{*}T}-e^{-tTT^{*}}\big),

(1.85)

but in this manuscript it suffices to employ (
1.84
).

The second main result of Section
12
, and at the same time the main result of this manuscript, the characterization of the Witten index of
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
in terms of spectral shift functions, can thus be summarized as follows:

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume hypotheses (
1.11
), (
1.12
), (
1.42
), and (
1.66
).
Then
0
is a right Lebesgue point of
\xi(\,\cdot\,;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})
, denoted by

\xi_{L}(0_{+};{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})
, and

\xi_{L}(0_{+};{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})=[\xi(0_{+};A_{+},A_{-})+\xi(0_{-};A_{+},A_{-})]/2.

(1.86)

In addition, the resolvent regularized Witten index
W_{k,r}({\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}})
of
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}

exists for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
k\geqslant\lceil n/2\rceil
, and
equals

\displaystyle\begin{split}W_{k,r}({\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}})=\xi_{L}(0_{+};{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})&=[\xi(0_{+};A_{+},A_{-})+\xi(0_{-};A_{+},A_{-})]/2\\
&=[\xi(0_{+};H,H_{0})+\xi(0_{-};H,H_{0})]/2.\end{split}

(1.87)

This is the first result of this kind applicable to non-Fredholm operators in a partial differential operator setting involving multi-dimensional massless Dirac operators.
In a sense, a project that started with Pushnitski in 2008, was considerably extended in scope in

[
77
]
, and further developed with the help of
[
38
]
–
[
44
]
,
[
82
]
,
[
83
]
, finally comes full circle.

Appendix
A
collects some useful results on block matrix operators, Appendix
B
is devoted to asymptotic results for Hankel functions, Appendic
C
presents low-energy expansions and estimates for the free Dirac Green’s function in the massless case, Appendix
D
recalls a product formula for modified (regularized) Fredholm determinants, and finally, Appendix
E
collects some of the notational conventions used throughout this manuscript.

2.
Some Background on (Locally) Smooth Operators

In this section we first recall a few basic facts on the notion of (local) Kato-smoothness (see, e.g.,

[
140
, Sect. XIII.7]
,
[
183
, Ch. 4]
, and
[
185
, Chs. 0–2]
) and then recall a variant, strong (local) Kato-smoothness (see, e.g.,
[
183
, Ch. 4]
,
[
185
, Chs. 0–2]
), as these
concepts will be useful in subsequent sections.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
S
be self-adjoint in
{\mathcal{H}}
and
T\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{K}})
with
\dom(S)\subseteq dom(T)

and fix
\varepsilon_{0}>0
. Then
T
is called
S
-Kato-smooth
(
in short,
S
-smooth in the following
)
if for each
f\in{\mathcal{H}}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|T\|_{S}^{2}=\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}),\;\|f\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}=1}\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\lambda\,\Big[\big\|T(S-(\lambda+i\varepsilon)I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}f\big\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\\
&\hskip 147.95424pt+\big\|T(S-(\lambda-i\varepsilon)I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}f\big\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\Big]<\infty.\end{split}

(2.1)

It suffices to require (
2.1
) for a dense set of
f\in{\mathcal{H}}
as
T
is closed. If
T
is
S
-smooth, then
T
is infinitesimally bounded with respect to
S
.

In terms of unitary groups,
T

is
S
-smooth if and only if for all
f\in{\mathcal{H}}
,
e^{-itS}f\in\dom(T)
for a.e.
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}dt\,\big\|Te^{-itS}f\big\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\leqslant C_{0}\|f\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}}

(2.2)

for some constant
C_{0}\in(0,\infty)
(
C_{0}
can be chosen to be
\|T\|^{2}_{S}
, but not
smaller).

An immediate consequence regarding the absence of singular spectrum derives from the fact
that if
T
is
S
-smooth then

\overline{\ran(T^{*})}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}_{ac}(S).

(2.3)

In particular,

if, in addition,
\ker(T)=\{0\}
, then
{\mathcal{H}}_{ac}(S)={\mathcal{H}}
,

(2.4)

and hence the spectrum of
S
is purely absolutely continuous,

\sigma(S)=\sigma_{ac}(S),\quad\sigma_{p}(S)=\sigma_{sc}(S)=\emptyset.

(2.5)

Here
{\mathcal{H}}_{ac}(S)
denotes the absolutely continuous subspace associated with
S
.

Moreover, as long as
B\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{L}})
(with
{\mathcal{L}}
another complex, separable Hilbert space),
BT
is
S
-smooth whenever
T
is
S
-smooth.

Finally, if
T\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{K}})
and for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
,

T(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}T^{*}
has a bounded closure in
{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying for some fixed

\varepsilon_{0}>0
,

C_{1}=\sup_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}},\;\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})}\big\|\overline{T(S-(\lambda+i\varepsilon)I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}T^{*}}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}<\infty,

(2.6)

then
T
is
S
-smooth with
\|T\|_{S}\leqslant C_{1}/\pi
.

While Definition
2.1
describes a global condition, a local version can be introduced as follows:

Definition 2.2
.

Let
S
be self-adjoint in
{\mathcal{H}}
and
T\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{K}})
with
\dom(S)\subseteq dom(T)
.

T
is called
S
-Kato-smooth on a Borel set
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}

(
in short,
S
-smooth on
\Lambda
in the following
)
if
TE_{S}(\Lambda)
is
S
-smooth.

Again, if
B\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{L}})
, then
BT
is
S
-smooth on
\Lambda_{0}
whenever
T
is.

For
TE_{S}(\Lambda)
to be well-defined it suffices that

E_{S}(\Lambda){\mathcal{H}}\cap\dom(S)\subseteq\dom(T)
.

If
T
is
S
-smooth on
\Lambda
then

\overline{\ran\big((TE_{S}(\Lambda))^{*}\big)}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}_{ac}(S),

(2.7)

in particular,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\text{ if, in addition, $\ker(T)=\{0\}$, then}\\
&\quad\sigma(S)\cap\Lambda=\sigma_{ac}(S)\cap\Lambda,\quad\sigma_{p}(S)\cap\Lambda=\sigma_{sc}(S)\cap\Lambda=\emptyset.\end{split}

(2.8)

If
T\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{K}})
and for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
,

T(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}T^{*}
has a bounded closure in
{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying for some fixed

\varepsilon_{0}>0
,

\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda,\;\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})}\big\|\overline{T(S-(\lambda+i\varepsilon)I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}T^{*}}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}<\infty,

(2.9)

or

\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda,\;\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})}\varepsilon\big\|\overline{T(S-(\lambda\pm i\varepsilon)I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}<\infty,

(2.10)

then
T
is
S
-smooth on
\overline{\Lambda}
.

Next, following
[
185
, Sect. 4.4]
, we turn to the concept of strongly smooth operators on
a compact interval
\Lambda_{0}=[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
,
\lambda_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
j=1,2
,

\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
(tailored toward certain applications to differential operators).
This requires some preparations: Given a separable complex
Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
, one considers the (nonseparable) Banach space of
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
-valued
Hölder continuous functions of order
\tau\in(0,1]
, denoted by

C^{\tau}(\Lambda_{0};{\mathcal{H}}_{0})
, with norm

\|f\|_{C^{\tau}(\Lambda_{0};{\mathcal{H}}_{0})}=\sup_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{0}}\bigg(\|f(\lambda)\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{0}}+\frac{\|f(\lambda)-f(\lambda^{\prime})\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{0}}}{|\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}|^{\tau}}\bigg),\quad f\in C^{\tau}(\Lambda_{0};{\mathcal{H}}_{0}).

(2.11)

Suppose the self-adjoint operator
S
in
{\mathcal{H}}
has purely absolutely continuous spectrum
on
\Lambda_{0}
, that is,

\sigma(S)\cap\Lambda_{0}=\sigma_{ac}(S)\cap\Lambda_{0},\quad\sigma_{p}(S)\cap\Lambda_{0}=\sigma_{sc}(S)\cap\Lambda_{0}=\emptyset,

(2.12)

of constant multiplicity
m_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
on
\Lambda_{0}
, with
\dim({\mathcal{H}}_{0})=m_{0}
.
In addition, let

{\mathcal{F}}_{0}:\begin{cases}E_{S}(\Lambda_{0}){\mathcal{H}}\to L^{2}(\Lambda_{0};d\lambda;{\mathcal{H}}_{0}),\\
f\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}_{0}f:=\widetilde{f},\end{cases}\text{be unitary,}

(2.13)

and “diagonalizing”
S
, that is, turning
SE_{S}(\Lambda_{0})
into a multiplication operator.
More precisely,
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
generates a spectral representation of
S
via,

({\mathcal{F}}_{0}E_{S}(\Omega)f)(\lambda)=\chi_{\Omega\cap\Lambda_{0}}(\lambda)\widetilde{f}(\lambda),\quad f\in E_{S}(\Lambda_{0}){\mathcal{H}}.

(2.14)

With these preparations in place, we are now in position to define the notion of strongly smooth operators (cf.
[
183
, Sect. 4.4]
, where a more general concept is introduced):

Definition 2.3
.

Let
S
be self-adjoint in
{\mathcal{H}}
with purely absolutely continuous spectrum
of constant
(
possibly, infinite
)
multiplicity on
\Lambda_{0}
and suppose that
T\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{K}})
with
\dom(S)\subseteq dom(T)
. Then
T
is called strongly
S
-Kato-smooth on
\Lambda_{0}

(
in short, strongly
S
-smooth
on
\Lambda_{0}
in the following
)
, with exponent
\tau\in(0,1]
,
if
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(TE_{S}(\Lambda))^{*}:{\mathcal{K}}\to C^{\tau}(\Lambda_{0};{\mathcal{H}}_{0})
is continuous, that is,
for
f=(TE_{S}(\Lambda_{0}))^{*}\xi
,
\xi\in{\mathcal{K}}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big\|\widetilde{f}(\lambda)\big\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{0}}=\|({\mathcal{F}}_{0}(TE_{S}(\Lambda_{0}))^{*}\xi)(\lambda)\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{0}}\leqslant C\|\xi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}},\\
&\big\|\widetilde{f}(\lambda)-\widetilde{f}(\lambda^{\prime})\big\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{0}}\leqslant C|\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}|^{\tau}\|\xi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}},\end{split}

(2.15)

with
C\in(0,\infty)
independent of
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{0}
and
\xi\in{\mathcal{K}}
.

Not surprisingly, the terminology chosen is consistent with the fact that

if
T
is strongly
S
-smooth on
\Lambda_{0}
, then it is
S
-smooth on
\Lambda_{0}
.

(2.16)

Moreover, as long as
B\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{L}})
(with
{\mathcal{L}}
another complex, separable Hilbert space)
and
T
is strongly
S
-smooth with exponent
\tau\in(0,1]
on
\Lambda_{0}
, then
BT
is
strongly
S
-smooth on
\Lambda_{0}
with the same exponent
\tau\in(0,1]
.

Next, we recall a perturbation approach in which
S
corresponds to the “sum” of an unperturbed
self-adjoint operator
S_{0}
in
{\mathcal{H}}
and a perturbation
V
in
{\mathcal{H}}
that can be factorized into a product
V_{1}^{*}V_{2}
as follows:
Suppose
V_{j}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{K}})
,
j=1,2
, with

V_{j}(|S_{0}|+I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1/2}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{K}}),\quad j=1,2,

(2.17)

and the symmetry condition,

(V_{1}f,V_{2}g)_{{\mathcal{K}}}=(V_{2}f,V_{1}g)_{{\mathcal{K}}},\quad f,g\in\dom\big(|S_{0}|^{1/2}\big).

(2.18)

In addition, suppose that for some (and hence for all)
z\in\rho(S_{0})
,

V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}
has a bounded extension in
{\mathcal{K}}
, which is then given by
its closure

\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}=V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1/2}\big[V_{1}(S_{0}-\overline{z}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1/2}\big]^{*}.

(2.19)

Here the operator
\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}
represents an abstract
Birman–Schwinger-type operator.

Finally, we assume that

\big[I_{{\mathcal{K}}}+\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-z_{0}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})\,\text{ for some $z_{0}\in\rho(S_{0})$.}

(2.20)

Then the equation

\displaystyle R(z)

\displaystyle=(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad-\big[V_{1}(S_{0}-\overline{z}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big]^{*}\big[I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]^{-1}V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1},

\displaystyle\hskip 241.84842ptz\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(2.21)

defines the resolvent of a self-adjoint operator
S
in
{\mathcal{H}}
, that is,

R(z)=(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(2.22)

with
S\supseteq S_{0}+V_{1}^{*}V_{2}
(the latter defined on
\dom(S_{0})\cap\dom(V_{1}^{*}V_{2})
, which may consist of
\{0\}
only); for details we refer to
[
106
]
(see also
[
78
]
,

[
183
, Sect. 1.9]
).

One also has

\displaystyle(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}-(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\big[V_{1}(S-\overline{z}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big]^{*}V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\big[V_{1}(S_{0}-\overline{z}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big]^{*}V_{2}(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1},

\displaystyle\hskip 123.76965ptz\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(2.23)

and

\displaystyle(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad-\big[V_{1}(S-\overline{z}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big]^{*}\big[I_{{\mathcal{H}}}-\overline{V_{2}(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]^{-1}V_{2}(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1},

\displaystyle\hskip 213.39566ptz\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(2.24)

as well as,

\displaystyle\overline{V_{1}(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\overline{V_{1}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}

(2.25)

\displaystyle\quad-\big[\overline{V_{1}(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]\big[\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big],\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

implying

\displaystyle\begin{split}\overline{V_{1}(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}=\overline{V_{1}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big[I_{{\mathcal{K}}}+\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]^{-1},\\
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.\end{split}

(2.26)

Similarly,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\overline{V_{2}(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}=I_{{\mathcal{H}}}-\big[I_{{\mathcal{K}}}+\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]^{-1},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.\end{split}

(2.27)

The remaining results in this section are all taken from
[
185
, Sects. 4.4–4.7]
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Assuming the hypotheses on
S_{0}
,
V_{j}
,
j=1,2
, employed in (
2.17
)–(
2.26
),
suppose the following additional conditions hold:

(i)

V_{2}
is
S_{0}
-smooth on
\Lambda_{0}=[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
,
\lambda_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

j=1,2
,
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
.

(ii)
The analytic, operator-valued functions
\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}
,

\overline{V_{1}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}
on
\text{\rm Re}(z)\in(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
,
\text{\rm Im}(z)\neq 0
, are continuous in
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
-norm up to and including the two rims of the “cut” along
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
.

(iii)
For some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\big[\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]^{k}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}({\mathcal{K}})
,

\text{\rm Im}(z)\neq 0
.

Define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{\pm}=\big\{\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}\,\big|\,\text{there exists $0\neq f\in{\mathcal{K}}$ s.t.}\,-f=\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-(\lambda\pm i0)I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}f\big\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}={\mathcal{N}}_{-}\cup{\mathcal{N}}_{+}.

(2.28)

Then
{\mathcal{N}}_{\pm},{\mathcal{N}}
are closed and of Lebesgue measure zero. Moreover, the analytic, operator-valued function
\overline{V_{1}(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}
on
\text{\rm Re}(z)\in(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
,

\text{\rm Im}(z)\neq 0
, is continuous in
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
-norm up to and including the two rims of the “cut” along

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\backslash{\mathcal{N}}
. If, in addition,
\ker(V_{1})=\{0\}
, then

S
has purely absolutely continuous spectrum on
\Lambda_{0}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}
, that is,

\sigma(S)\cap(\Lambda_{0}\backslash{\mathcal{N}})=\sigma_{ac}(S)\cap(\Lambda_{0}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}),\quad\sigma_{p}(S)\cap(\Lambda_{0}\backslash{\mathcal{N}})=\sigma_{sc}(S)\cap(\Lambda_{0}\backslash{\mathcal{N}})=\emptyset.

(2.29)

As detailed in
[
183
, Remark 4.6.3]
, condition
(iii)
in Theorem
2.4
can be
replaced by the following one:

(iii^{\prime})
Suppose there exist
z_{\pm}\in\rho(S_{0})
,
\pm\text{\rm Im}(z_{\pm})>0
, such that

\big[I_{{\mathcal{K}}}+\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-z_{\pm}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}),

(2.30)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}-\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-z^{\prime}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\\
&\quad=(z-z^{\prime})\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}(S_{0}-z^{\prime}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}({\mathcal{K}}),\quad z,z^{\prime}\in\rho(S_{0}).\end{split}

(2.31)

Next we strengthen the hypotheses in Theorem
2.4
by invoking the notion of strong

S_{0}
-smoothness:

Theorem 2.5
.

Assuming the hypotheses on
S_{0}
,
V_{j}
,
j=1,2
, employed in (
2.17
)–(
2.26
),
suppose in addition the following conditions hold:

(i)

S_{0}
has purely absolutely continuous spectrum of constant multiplicity

m_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
on
\Lambda_{0}=[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
,
\lambda_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

j=1,2
,
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
.

(ii)

V_{j}
are strongly
S_{0}
-smooth on any compact subinterval of
\Lambda_{0}
with exponents
\tau_{j}>0
,
j=1,2
.

(iii)
For some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\big[\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]^{k}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}({\mathcal{K}})
,
\text{\rm Im}(z)\neq 0
.

Then the analytic, operator-valued functions

\overline{V_{1}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}},\,\overline{V_{2}(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}},\;\text{ $\big($resp., $\overline{V_{1}(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}$$\big)$}

(2.32)

on
\text{\rm Re}(z)\in(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
,
\text{\rm Im}(z)\neq 0
, are Hölder continuous in
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
-norm with
exponent
\min\{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\}
up to and including the two rims of the “cut” along

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

(
resp.,
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\backslash{\mathcal{N}}
)
.

Moreover, the local wave operators

W_{\pm}(S,S_{0};\Lambda_{0})=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}e^{itS}e^{-itS_{0}}P_{S_{0},ac}(\Lambda_{0}),

(2.33)

with
P_{S_{0},ac}(\Lambda_{0})=E_{S_{0}}(\Lambda_{0})E_{S_{0},ac}
, and
E_{S_{0},ac}
the projection onto the absolutely continuous subspace of
S_{0}
, exist and are complete, that is,

\ker(W_{\pm}(S,S_{0};\Lambda_{0}))={\mathcal{H}}\ominus E_{S_{0},ac}(\Lambda_{0}){\mathcal{H}},\quad\ran(W_{\pm}(S,S_{0};\Lambda_{0}))=P_{S,ac}(\Lambda_{0}){\mathcal{H}},

(2.34)

with
P_{S,ac}(\Lambda_{0})=E_{S}(\Lambda_{0})E_{S,ac}
, and
E_{S,ac}
the projection onto the absolutely continuous subspace of
S
.

For the remainder of this theorem suppose in addition that
\tau_{1}>1/2
. Then

{\mathcal{N}}_{\pm}={\mathcal{N}}=\sigma_{p}(S)\cap\Lambda_{0}

(2.35)

and the
(
geometric
)
multiplicities of the eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}\in\Lambda_{0}
of
S
and the
eigenvalue
-1
of
\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-(\lambda_{0}\pm i0)I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}
coincide.
If in addition,
\ker(V_{1})=\{0\}
, then

S
has no singularly continuous spectrum on
\Lambda_{0}
, that is,

\sigma(S)\cap\Lambda_{0}=\sigma_{ac}(S)\cap\Lambda_{0},\quad\sigma_{sc}(S)\cap\Lambda_{0}=\emptyset,

(2.36)

and the singular spectrum of
S
on the interior,
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, of
\Lambda_{0}

consists only of eigenvalues of finite multiplicity with no accumulation point in

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, in particular,

\sigma_{s}(S)\cap(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\sigma_{p}(S)\cap(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}).

(2.37)

Again, condition
(iii)
in Theorem
2.5
can be replaced by condition
(iii^{\prime})
above.

To make the transition from local to global wave operators we also recall the following result.

Theorem 2.6
.

Assuming the hypotheses on
S_{0}
,
V_{j}
,
j=1,2
, employed in (
2.17
)–(
2.26
),
suppose in addition the following conditions hold:

(i)

S_{0}
has purely absolutely continuous spectrum of constant multiplicity

m_{0,k}\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
on a system of intervals

\Lambda_{0,\ell}=[\lambda_{1,\ell},\lambda_{2,\ell}]
,
\lambda_{j,\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

j=1,2
,
\lambda_{1,\ell}<\lambda_{2,\ell}
,
\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}
an appropriate index
set, such that

\sigma(S_{0})\bigg\backslash\bigcup_{\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}}\Lambda_{0,\ell}\,\text{ has Lebesgue
measure zero.}

(2.38)

(ii)

V_{j}
are strongly
S_{0}
-smooth on any compact subinterval of
\Lambda_{0,\ell}
with
exponents
\tau_{j,\ell}>0
,
j=1,2
,
\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}
.

(iii)
For some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\big[\overline{V_{2}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]^{k}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}({\mathcal{K}})
,
\text{\rm Im}(z)\neq 0
.

Then the
(
global
)
wave operators

W_{\pm}(S,S_{0})=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}e^{itS}e^{-itS_{0}},

(2.39)

exist and are complete, that is,

\ker(W_{\pm}(S,S_{0}))=\{0\},\quad\ran(W_{\pm}(S,S_{0}))=E_{S,ac}{\mathcal{H}},

(2.40)

with
E_{S,ac}
the projection onto the absolutely continuous subspace of
S
.

For additional references in the context of smooth operator theory, limiting absorption principles,
and completeness of wave operators, see, for instance,
[
6
]
,
[
11
]
,

[
35
]
,
[
19
, Ch. 17]
,
[
20
]
,
[
74
]
,

[
86
]
,
[
106
]
,
[
111
]
,
[
117
]
,

[
140
, Sect. XIII.7]
,
[
141
]
,
[
183
, Ch. 4]
,
[
185
, Chs. 0–2]
.

3.
A Limiting Absorption Principle for Interacting, Massless Dirac Operators

In this section, following
[
185
, Sects. 1.11, 2.1, 2.2]
, we apply the abstract framework of strongly smooth operators of the preceding section to the concrete case of massless Dirac operators with electromagnetic potentials.

To rigorously define the free massless
n
-dimensional Dirac operators to be studied in the sequel, we now introduce the following set of basic hypotheses assumed for the remainder of this manuscript (these hypotheses will have to be strengthened later on).

Hypothesis 3.1
.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
n\geqslant 2
.

(i)
Set
N=2^{\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor}
and let
\alpha_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
,

\alpha_{n+1}:=\beta
, denote
n+1
anti-commuting Hermitian
N\times N
matrices with squares equal to
I_{N}
, that is,

\alpha_{j}^{*}=\alpha_{j},\quad\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}+\alpha_{k}\alpha_{j}=2\delta_{j,k}I_{N},\quad 1\leqslant j,k\leqslant n+1.

(3.1)

(ii)
Introduce
in
[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
the free massless Dirac operator

H_{0}=\alpha\cdot(-i\nabla)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}(-i\partial_{j}),\quad\dom(H_{0})=[W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},

(3.2)

where
\partial_{j}=\partial/\partial x_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
.

(iii)
Next, consider the self-adjoint matrix-valued potential

V=\{V_{\ell,m}\}_{1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N}
satisfying for some fixed
\rho\in(1,\infty)
,
C\in(0,\infty)
,

V\in[L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N\times N},\quad|V_{\ell,m}(x)|\leqslant C\langle x\rangle^{-\rho}\,\text{ for a.e.~$x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}$, $1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N$.}

(3.3)

Under these assumptions on
V
, the massless Dirac operator
H
in
[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
is defined via

H=H_{0}+V,\quad\dom(H)=\dom(H_{0})=[W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}.

(3.4)

Then
H_{0}
and
H
are self-adjoint in
[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
,
with essential spectrum covering the
entire real line,

\sigma_{ess}(H)=\sigma_{ess}(H_{0})=\sigma(H_{0})={\mathbb{R}},

(3.5)

a consequence of relative compactness of
V
with respect to
H_{0}
. In addition,

\sigma_{ac}(H_{0})={\mathbb{R}},\quad\sigma_{p}(H_{0})=\sigma_{sc}(H_{0})=\emptyset.

(3.6)

On occasion (cf. Section
7
) we will drop the self-adjointness hypothesis on the
N\times N
matrix
V
and
still define a closed operator
H
in
[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
as in (
3.4
).

For completeness we also recall that the massive free Dirac operator in
[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
associated
with the mass parameter
m>0
then would be of the form

H_{0}(m)=H_{0}+m\beta,\quad\dom(H_{0}(m))=[W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},\;m>0,\;\beta=\alpha_{n+1},

(3.7)

but we will primarily study the massless case
m=0
in this manuscript.

In the special one-dimensional case
n=1
, one can choose for
\alpha_{1}
either a real constant or one of the three Pauli matrices. Similarly, in the massive case,
\beta
would typically be a second Pauli matrix (different from
\alpha_{1}
). For simplicity we confine ourselves to
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

n\geqslant 2
, in the following.

Let
S({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
denote the Schwartz space of rapidly decreasing functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and

S^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
the space of tempered distributions. In addition, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we also introduce the scale of weighted
L^{2}
-spaces,

L^{2}_{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})=\{f\in S^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\,|\,\|f\|_{L^{2}_{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}:=\|\langle x\rangle^{s}f\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}<\infty\},\quad s\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(3.8)

Defining
Q_{j}
as the operator of multiplication by
x_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
, in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, and
introducing
Q=(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{n})
, one notes that

\dom(\langle Q\rangle^{s})=L^{2}_{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}),\quad s\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(3.9)

Employing the relations (
3.1
), one observes that

H_{0}(m)^{2}=I_{N}\big[-\Delta+m^{2}I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}\big],\quad\dom\big(H_{0}(m)^{2}\big)=[W^{2,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},\;m\geqslant 0.

(3.10)

Remark 3.2
.

Since we permit a (sufficiently decaying) matrix-valued potential
V
in
H
, this includes, in particular, the case of electromagnetic interactions introduced via minimal coupling, that is,
V

describes also special cases of the form,

H(q,A):=\alpha\cdot(-i\nabla-A)+qI_{N}=H_{0}+[qI_{N}-\alpha\cdot A],\quad\dom(H(q,A))=[W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},

(3.11)

where
(q,A)
represent the electromagnetic potentials on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, with

q:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
q\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
,
A=(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})
,
A_{j}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

A_{j}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
, and for some fixed
\rho>1
,
C\in(0,\infty)
,

|q(x)|+|A_{j}(x)|\leqslant C\langle x\rangle^{-\rho},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\;1\leqslant j\leqslant n.

(3.12)

\diamond

To analyze the spectral properties of
H
we first turn to the spectral representation of

H_{0}=\alpha\cdot(-i\nabla)
(see also Thaller
[
164
, Sect. 5.6]
and
Yafaev
[
185
, Sect. 2.4]
).
Introducing the unitary Fourier transform in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
via

{\mathcal{F}}:\begin{cases}L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n};d^{n}x)\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n};d^{n}p)\\
f\mapsto({\mathcal{F}}f)(p):=f^{\wedge}(p)=\slim_{R\to\infty}(2\pi)^{-n/2}\int_{|x|\leqslant R}d^{n}x\,e^{-ip\cdot x}f(x),\end{cases}

(3.13)

with
\slim
abbreviating the limit in the topology of
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, one obtains

H_{0}={\mathcal{F}}^{-1}|p|(\alpha\cdot\omega){\mathcal{F}},

(3.14)

employing polar coordinates in Fourier space,
p=|p|\,\omega
,
\omega\in S^{n-1}
. Since
by (
3.1
) (see also (
3.10
))

(\alpha\cdot\omega)^{2}=I_{N},\quad\omega\in S^{n-1},

(3.15)

the self-adjoint matrix
\alpha\cdot\omega
has eigenvalues

\pm 1
of multiplicity
N/2
with associated spectral projection matrices of rank
N/2

denoted by
\Pi_{\pm}(\omega)
,

\alpha\cdot\omega=\Pi_{+}(\omega)-\Pi_{-}(\omega),\quad\omega\in S^{n-1}.

(3.16)

Introducing

T(\omega)=2^{-1/2}(\alpha_{n+1}+\alpha\cdot\omega),\quad\omega\in S^{n-1},

(3.17)

one infers that
T(\omega)\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N\times N}
is Hermitian symmetric for each
\omega\in S^{n-1}
. In addition, the anti-commutation property in (
3.1
) implies

\displaystyle T(\omega)T(\omega)^{*}

\displaystyle=2^{-1}\big[\alpha_{n+1}^{2}+(\alpha\cdot\omega)\alpha_{n+1}+\alpha_{n+1}(\alpha\cdot\omega)+(\alpha\cdot\omega)^{2}\big]

(3.18)

\displaystyle=I_{N},\quad\omega\in S^{n-1},

so that
T(\omega)
is actually unitary for each
\omega\in S^{n-1}
. The reason for introducing the unitary matrix
T(\omega)
,
\omega\in S^{n-1}
, is that it can be used to diagonalize the matrix
\alpha\cdot p
. Indeed, writing
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
in polar coordinates as
p=|p|\omega
with
\omega\in S^{n-1}
, one obtains

\displaystyle T(\omega)|p|\alpha_{n+1}T(\omega)^{*}

\displaystyle=2^{-1}|p|\big[\alpha_{n+1}+2\alpha\cdot\omega-(\alpha\cdot\omega)^{2}\alpha_{n+1}\big]

\displaystyle=|p|(\alpha\cdot\omega)

\displaystyle=\alpha\cdot p,

(3.19)

so that
\alpha\cdot p
is unitarily equivalent to
|p|\alpha_{n+1}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{N}
. Of course,

\alpha_{n+1}
is Hermitian symmetric, so it may be diagonalized by conjugating with a fixed
(i.e.,
p
-independent) unitary matrix
U\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N\times N}
. We may assume without loss
that the columns of
U
are arranged so that

\alpha_{n+1}=U\begin{pmatrix}-I_{N/2}&0\\
0&I_{N/2}\end{pmatrix}U^{*},

(3.20)

where
0
denotes the zero matrix in
{\mathbb{C}}^{(N/2)\times(N/2)}
. The facts (
3.19
) and (
3.20
) combine to yield

\alpha\cdot p=\widetilde{T}(\omega)|p|\begin{pmatrix}-I_{N/2}&0\\
0&I_{N/2}\end{pmatrix}\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*},\quad p=|p|\omega\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(3.21)

where

\widetilde{T}(\omega):=T(\omega)U,\quad\omega\in S^{n-1},

(3.22)

and then (
3.14
) implies

H_{0}={\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widetilde{T}(\omega)|p|\begin{pmatrix}-I_{N/2}&0\\
0&I_{N/2}\end{pmatrix}\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*}{\mathcal{F}}.

(3.23)

A simple manipulation in (
3.23
) yields

\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*}{\mathcal{F}}H_{0}=|p|\begin{pmatrix}-I_{N/2}&0\\
0&I_{N/2}\end{pmatrix}\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*}{\mathcal{F}}.

(3.24)

To “diagonalize”
H_{0}
, we introduce the notation

P_{-}:=\begin{pmatrix}I_{N/2}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad P_{+}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&I_{N/2}\end{pmatrix},

(3.25)

and define the transformation

{\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}:[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};d\lambda;[L^{2}(S^{n-1})]^{N})

(3.26)

according to

\displaystyle\begin{split}({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}f)(\lambda,\omega)=\begin{cases}|\lambda|^{(n-1)/2}P_{-}\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*}f^{\wedge}(|\lambda|\omega),&\lambda<0,\\
|\lambda|^{(n-1)/2}P_{+}\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*}f^{\wedge}(|\lambda|\omega),&\lambda\geqslant 0,\end{cases}&\\
\omega\in S^{n-1},\;f\in[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}.&\end{split}

(3.27)

The transformation
{\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}
is unitary. In fact,

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}f\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};d\lambda;[L^{2}(S^{n-1})]^{N})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,|\lambda|^{n-1}\int_{S^{n-1}}d^{n-1}\omega\,\Big\{\big\|P_{-}\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*}f^{\wedge}(|\lambda|\omega)\big\|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{N}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\|P_{+}\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*}f^{\wedge}(|\lambda|\omega)\big\|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{N}}^{2}\Big\},\quad f\in[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}.

(3.28)

Since
(P_{-}\xi,P_{+}\eta)_{{\mathbb{C}}^{N}}=0
for all
\xi,\eta\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N}
, an application of the Pythagorean theorem in (
3.28
) yields

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}f\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};d\lambda;[L^{2}(S^{n-1})]^{N})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,|\lambda|^{n-1}\int_{S^{n-1}}d^{n-1}\omega\,\big\|\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*}f^{\wedge}(|\lambda|\omega)\big\|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{N}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big\|f^{\wedge}\big\|_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big\|f\big\|_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}^{2},\quad f\in[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}.

(3.29)

To check that
{\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}
correctly diagonalizes
H_{0}
in the sense that

({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}H_{0}f)(\lambda,\,\cdot\,)=\lambda({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}f)(\lambda,\,\cdot\,)\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}$},\;f\in[W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},

(3.30)

one considers separately the cases
\lambda<0
and
\lambda\geqslant 0
. Indeed, for a fixed
f\in[W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
, one applies (
3.24
) to obtain

\displaystyle({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}H_{0}f)(\lambda,\omega)

\displaystyle=|\lambda|^{(n-1)/2}P_{-}\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*}(H_{0}f)^{\wedge}(|\lambda|\omega)

\displaystyle=-|\lambda|P_{-}\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*}f^{\wedge}(|\lambda|\omega)

\displaystyle=\lambda({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}f)(\lambda,\omega),\quad\lambda<0,\;\omega\in S^{n-1},

(3.31)

and, similarly,

\displaystyle({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}H_{0}f)(\lambda,\omega)

\displaystyle=|\lambda|^{(n-1)/2}P_{+}\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*}(H_{0}f)^{\wedge}(|\lambda|\omega)

\displaystyle=|\lambda|P_{+}\widetilde{T}(\omega)^{*}f^{\wedge}(|\lambda|\omega)

\displaystyle=\lambda({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}f)(\lambda,\omega),\quad\lambda\geqslant 0,\;\omega\in S^{n-1}.

(3.32)

Equations (
3.31
) and (
3.32
) combine to yield (
3.30
). Of course, (
3.30
) generalizes to

({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}\psi(H_{0})f)(\lambda,\,\cdot\,)=\psi(\lambda)({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}f)(\lambda,\,\cdot\,)\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}$},\;f\in\dom(\psi(H_{0})),

(3.33)

for any measurable function
\psi
on
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Consequently,
[
185
, Proposition 2.4.1]
applies to
H_{0}
, resulting in the following facts:

Proposition 3.3
.

Suppose Hypothesis
3.1

(i)
,
(ii)
and let
\gamma>1/2
. Then
\langle Q\rangle^{-\gamma}
is strongly
H_{0}
-smooth on compact subintervals of

{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
with exponent
\tau>0
given by

\tau=\begin{cases}\gamma-(1/2),&\gamma\in((1/2),(3/2)),\\
1-\varepsilon,&\gamma=3/2,\;\varepsilon\in(0,1),\\
1,&\gamma\geqslant 3/2.\end{cases}

(3.34)

We note that for
\tau>1/2
,
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
,

\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\tau}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\tau}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),

(3.35)

a special case of the well-known general fact (cf., e.g.,
[
183
, p. 41]
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}&f(Q)g(-i\nabla)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}\big(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big)\,\text{ for any
$f,g\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})$}\\
&\quad\text{with $\lim_{|x|\to\infty}f(x)=0=\lim_{|p|\to\infty}g(p)$.}\end{split}

(3.36)

To make the connection with the results collected in Section
2
, we identify
S_{0}
and
H_{0}
and
S
with

H=H_{0}+V
, and we factorize
V
according to

V=V_{1}^{*}V_{2},\quad V_{1}=V_{1}^{*}=\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\tau}I_{N},\quad V_{2}=\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{\tau}V,

(3.37)

with
V
satisfying the conditions in (
3.3
) for some fixed
\rho>1
, and hence, with

\tau\in(1/2,\rho)
,

\|V_{2}(\,\cdot\,)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}\leqslant C\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-(\rho-\tau)}.

(3.38)

In addition,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\tau}\big(H-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}\big)^{-1}\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\tau}=\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\tau}\big(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}\big)^{-1}\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\tau}\\
&\quad\times\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{\tau}V\,\big(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}\big)^{-1}\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\tau}\big]^{-1},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},\end{split}

(3.39)

to mention just a few analogs of the abstract facts collected in (
2.17
)–(
2.26
), which all
apply in this concrete setting of massless Dirac operators.

Thus, temporarily assuming
\rho>3/2
in (
3.3
), Theorem
2.5
applies to

S=H_{0}+V
with

\tau_{1}=\tau-(1/2)>1/2,\,\text{ necessitating }\,\tau>1,

(3.40)

and

\tau_{2}=(\rho-\tau)-(1/2)>0,\,\text{ requiring }\,\rho>3/2.

(3.41)

Actually, as shown in
[
185
, p. 98–99]
in the context of the Laplacian
h_{0}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
,

h_{0}=-\Delta,\quad\dom(h_{0})=H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})

(3.42)

it suffices to assume
just
\rho>1
in (
3.3
) (even though this cannot be inferred directly from abstract results,
the latter require
\rho>3/2
as outlined in (
3.40
), (
3.41
)) and

\tau\in(1/2,\rho-1/2)
. A closer examination of
[
185
, p. 98–99]
(see also

[
185
, p. 118]
) reveals that
there is nothing special about
h_{0}
and precisely the same results apply to

H_{0}=\alpha\cdot(-i\nabla)
as we discuss next.

Applying Theorems
2.4
–
2.6
, to the pair
(H,H_{0})
and to a union of
compact intervals exhausting
(-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)
, combined with the approach in

[
185
, p. 98–99, 118]
, thus yield the following result:

Theorem 3.4
.

Assume Hypothesis
3.1
and consider
H
as defined in (
3.4
). Then

\displaystyle\sigma_{ess}(H)=\sigma_{ac}(H)={\mathbb{R}},

(3.43)

\displaystyle\sigma_{sc}(H)=\emptyset,

(3.44)

\displaystyle\sigma_{s}(H)\cap({\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\})=\sigma_{p}(H)\cap({\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}),

(3.45)

with the only possible accumulation points of
\sigma_{p}(H)

being
0
and
\pm\infty
. If

{\mathcal{N}}_{0}:=\sigma_{p}(H)\cap({\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\})=\sigma_{d}(H)\cap({\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}),

(3.46)

then the operators

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(\lambda\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}},\;\overline{V_{1}(H_{0}-(\lambda\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\\
&\quad\text{$\big($resp.,
$\overline{V_{1}(H-(\lambda\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}$$\big)$}\end{split}

(3.47)

are Hölder continuous in
{\mathcal{B}}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})
-norm with respect to
\lambda
varying in compact subintervals of

{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}

(
resp.,
{\mathbb{R}}\backslash(\{0\}\cup{\mathcal{N}}_{0})
)
. In particular, with
{\mathcal{N}}_{\pm}
defined in analogy to
(
2.28
) by

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{N}}_{\pm}=\big\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}\,\big|\,&\,\text{there exists
$0\neq f\in{\mathcal{K}}$ s.t.}\\
&-f=\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(\lambda\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}f\big\},\end{split}

(3.48)

one obtains

{\mathcal{N}}_{+}={\mathcal{N}}_{-}:={\mathcal{N}}_{0},

(3.49)

and the
(
geometric
)
multiplicities of the eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}

of
H
and the eigenvalue
-1
of

\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(\lambda_{0}\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}
coincide and are finite.
Finally, the global wave operators

W_{\pm}(H,H_{0})=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}e^{itH}e^{-itH_{0}},

(3.50)

exist and are complete, that is,

\ker(W_{\pm}(H,H_{0}))=\{0\},\quad\ran(W_{\pm}(H,H_{0}))=E_{H,ac}{\mathcal{H}},

(3.51)

with
E_{H,ac}
the projection onto the absolutely continuous subspace of
H
.

Proof.

As discussed above, Theorems
2.5
and
2.6
apply to
S_{0}=H_{0}
and
S=H
and a union of closed intervals
\Lambda_{0}
exhausting
(-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)
under the additional assumption that
\rho>3/2
(and
\tau\in(1,\rho-1/2)
). Hence, Theorem
3.4
is proved subject to
\rho>3/2
.

To improve this to
\rho>1
(and
\tau\in(1/2,\rho-1/2)
) we now follow

[
185
, p. 98–99, 118]
. First, one notes that if
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
is an eigenvalue of
H
with a corresponding eigenvector
\psi\in[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
, then

\|({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}\psi)(\mu,\,\cdot\,)\|_{[L^{2}(S^{n-1})]^{N}}\leqslant C_{1}|\mu-\lambda|^{\rho-(3/2)},\quad\mu\in{\mathbb{R}},

(3.52)

for some
C_{1}\in(0,\infty)
.
In fact, by (
3.3
),
g:=-V\psi\in[L_{\rho}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
, and since
\langle Q\rangle^{-\rho}
is strongly
H_{0}
-continuous with exponent
\rho-(1/2)>(1/2)
by Proposition
3.3
, the function
\widetilde{g}:={\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}g
is Hölder continuous:

\|\widetilde{g}(\mu,\,\cdot\,)-\widetilde{g}(\lambda,\,\cdot\,)\|_{[L^{2}(S^{n-1})]^{N}}\leqslant C|\mu-\lambda|^{\rho-(1/2)},\quad\mu\in{\mathbb{R}},

(3.53)

for some constant
C\in(0,\infty)
, which is independent of
\lambda
and
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}
. In addition, since
g=H_{0}\psi-\lambda\psi
, the spectral representation in (
3.33
) yields

\widetilde{g}(\mu,\,\cdot\,)=(\mu-\lambda)({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}\psi)(\mu,\,\cdot\,),\quad\mu\in{\mathbb{R}},

(3.54)

which implies
\widetilde{g}(\lambda,\,\cdot\,)=0
. Therefore, (
3.53
) reduces to

\|\widetilde{g}(\mu,\,\cdot\,)\|_{[L^{2}(S^{n-1})]^{N}}\leqslant C|\mu-\lambda|^{\rho-(1/2)},\quad\mu\in{\mathbb{R}},

(3.55)

and then (
3.54
) yields (
3.52
) with
C_{1}=C
. In addition, one also notes that the equation

(H_{0}-\lambda I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})\psi=-V\psi
implies

g=-V(H_{0}-(\lambda\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}g,

(3.56)

since
\psi=(H_{0}-(\lambda\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}g
and
\psi=0
if
g=0
.

To prove that non-zero eigenvalues of
H
have finite multiplicity and may only accumulate at
0
and
\pm\infty
, one may follow the proof of
[
185
, Proposition 1.9.2]
essentially verbatim; one only needs to replace
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
by
{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
.

Next, one proves that for any
\tau\in(0,1/2]
and
p<2(1-2\tau)^{-1}
, and for any compact set

X\subset{\mathbb{R}}
,

\int_{X}d\lambda\,\|({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}f)(\lambda,\,\cdot\,)\|_{[L^{2}(S^{n-1})]^{N}}^{2}\leqslant C_{2}\|f\|_{[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}^{p},\quad f\in[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},

(3.57)

for some
C_{2}=C_{2}(\alpha,p,X)\in(0,\infty)
. To prove (
3.57
), one can follow, with minor modifications, the proof of
[
185
, Proposition 1.9.3]
. Indeed, for an arbitrary compact set

X\subset{\mathbb{R}}
, one introduces the family of spaces

L^{p}\big(X;d\lambda;[L^{2}(S^{n-1})]^{N}\big),\quad p\in[1,\infty)\cup\{\infty\},

(3.58)

and observes that by
[
185
, Theorem 1.1.4]
,

{\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}f\in L^{\infty}\big(X;d\lambda;[L^{2}(S^{n-1})]^{N}\big),\quad f\in[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},\;\tau>1/2.

(3.59)

The formula

\displaystyle\begin{split}({\mathcal{T}}(f_{1},f_{2}))(\lambda)=(({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}f_{1})(\lambda,\,\cdot\,),({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}f_{2})(\lambda,\,\cdot\,))_{[L^{2}(S^{n-1})]^{N}}&\\
\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in X$ and $(f_{1},f_{2})\in[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\times[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}$},&\end{split}

(3.60)

defines a bilinear map for each
q\geqslant 1
and
\tau\geqslant 0
:

{\mathcal{T}}:[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\times[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\to L^{q}(X,d\lambda).

(3.61)

The map
{\mathcal{T}}
is continuous for
\tau_{0}=0
,
q_{0}=1
and
\tau_{1}>1/2
,
q_{1}=\infty
, so by Calderón’s complex bilinear interpolation theorem (c.f., e.g.,
[
170
, §1.19.5]
,
[
37
]
), for any
s\in[0,1]
the map
{\mathcal{T}}
is continuous for

\tau=\tau(s)=s\tau_{0}+(1-s)\tau_{1},\quad q^{-1}=q(s)^{-1}=sq_{0}^{-1}+(1-s)q_{1}^{-1},

(3.62)

and

\|{\mathcal{T}}(f_{1},f_{2})\|_{L^{q}(X,d\lambda)}\leqslant C(\tau,X)\|f_{1}\|_{[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}\|f_{2}\|_{[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}},\quad f_{1},f_{2}\in[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}.

(3.63)

Taking
f_{1}=f_{2}=f\in[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
and
q=p/2
in (
3.63
) yields (
3.57
).

In analogy with
[
185
, Lemma 1.9.4]
, if
h\in[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
for some
\tau\in(1/2,1]
and
({\mathcal{F}}_{H_{0}}h)(\lambda,\,\cdot\,)=0
for some
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
, then

(H_{0}-(\lambda\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}h\in[L_{-\widetilde{\tau}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},\quad\widetilde{\tau}>1-\tau.

(3.64)

To prove (
3.64
), it suffices to show

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big|\big((H_{0}-(\lambda\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})N]})^{-1}h,g\big)_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}\big|\leqslant C_{3}\|h\|_{[L_{\tau}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}\|g\|_{[L_{\widetilde{\tau}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}},&\\
g\in[S({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},&\end{split}

(3.65)

for some
C_{3}=C_{3}(\lambda)\in(0,\infty)
. Using the spectral representation for
H_{0}
, one infers that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big((H_{0}-(\lambda\pm i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}h,g\big)_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}\\
