\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle x\rangle^{-(k+1-j)\rho(k+1)^{-1}}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{j-k-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),&\\
p>p\big((k+1-j)\rho(k+1)^{-1},k+1-j\big).&\end{split}

(7.29)

One distinguishes the two possible cases:
(i)

\rho(k+1)^{-1}<1
, or
(ii)

\rho(k+1)^{-1}\geqslant 1
.

In case
(i)
with
\rho(k+1)^{-1}<1
, one computes

\displaystyle p\big((k+1-j)\rho(k+1)^{-1},k+1-j\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{(k+1-j)\rho(k+1)^{-1}},

(7.30)

\displaystyle p\big(j\rho(k+1)^{-1},j\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{j\rho(k+1)^{-1}},

(7.31)

so that

\displaystyle p\big((k+1-j)\rho(k+1)^{-1},k+1-j\big)^{-1}+p\big(j\rho(k+1)^{-1},j\big)^{-1}=\frac{\rho}{n}>1.

(7.32)

Hence, the right-hand side of (
7.27
) belongs to
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
.

In case
(ii)
with
\rho(k+1)^{-1}\geqslant 1
, one computes

\displaystyle p\big((k+1-j)\rho(k+1)^{-1},k+1-j\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{k+1-j}

(7.33)

\displaystyle p\big(j\rho(k+1)^{-1},j\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{j},

(7.34)

so that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&p\big((k+1-j)\rho(k+1)^{-1},k+1-j\big)^{-1}+p\big(j\rho(k+1)^{-1},j\big)^{-1}\\
&\quad=\frac{k+1}{n}=\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}\geqslant 1+\frac{1}{n}>1.\end{split}

(7.35)

Hence, the right-hand side of (
7.27
) belongs to
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
.

In either case, the right-hand side of (
7.27
) belongs to
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
.
Since
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant k
, was arbitrary, it follows that every term in the summation on the right-hand side in (
7.26
) belongs to
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
, and then (
7.24
) follows from the vector space properties of the trace class.
∎

We conclude this section by recalling a well-known result:

Lemma 7.5
.

Suppose
p>n/\min(\tau,2\kappa)
,
p\geqslant 1
, with
\tau>0
,
\kappa>0
. Then

\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-\tau}(h_{0}+I_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})})^{-\kappa}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}\big(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big).

(7.36)

In particular, if
V
satisfies Hypothesis
7.1
and
\kappa>n/2
,

V\big(H_{0}^{2}+I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}\big)^{-\kappa}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big).

(7.37)

Proof.

While (
7.36
) is a special case of
[
185
, Proposition 3.1.5, Lemma 3.4.3]
(see also

[
82
]
,
[
158
, Ch. 4]
), (
7.37
) follows from combining (
3.10
) and (
7.36
).
∎

8.
The Spectral Shift Function: Abstract Facts

The significance of Theorem
7.4
is that the trace class condition (
7.24
) permits one to define a spectral shift function for the pair
(H,H_{0})
. To make this precise, we introduce the class of
functions
{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}({\mathbb{R}})
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, by

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}({\mathbb{R}}):=\big\{f\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})\,\big|\,f^{(\ell)}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}});\text{ there exists }\varepsilon>0\text{ and }f_{0}=f_{0}(f)\in{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{ such that }\big(d^{\ell}/d\lambda^{\ell}\big)\big[f(\lambda)-f_{0}\lambda^{-m}\big]\underset{|\lambda|\to\infty}{=}O\big(|\lambda|^{-\ell-m-\varepsilon}\big),\;\ell=0,1,2\big\}.

(8.1)

(It is implied that
f_{0}=f_{0}(f)
is the same as
\lambda\to\pm\infty
.) One observes that

C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathfrak{F}_{m}(\mathbb{R})
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

In
[
110
]
, M. Krein established the existence of a spectral shift function corresponding to any pair of resolvent comparable self-adjoint operators. Specifically, Krein proved that if
S_{0}
and
S
are self-adjoint and satisfy

\big[(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}-(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})

(8.2)

for some (and, hence, for all)
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
, then

[f(S)-f(S_{0})]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad f\in{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}}),

(8.3)

and there exists a real-valued spectral shift function

\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})\in L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}},(1+|\lambda|)^{-2}\,d\lambda\big)

(8.4)

so that

\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}(f(S)-f(S_{0}))=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})f^{\prime}(\lambda),\quad f\in{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}}).

(8.5)

One limitation to Krein’s theory is that the condition (
8.2
) generally does not hold for Schrödinger operators in dimensions
n\geqslant 4
. Similar difficulties are encountered for the polyharmonic operator (cf.
[
185
, §3.4]
) and the Dirac operator (cf.
[
185
, §3.5.3]
and Theorem
7.4
). In these cases, only the difference of higher powers of the resolvents belongs to the trace class (cf.
[
185
, Remark 3.3.3]
). Using the theory of double operator integrals, Yafaev
[
184
]
proved the existence of a spectral shift function under the weaker assumption that the difference of an odd power of the resolvents belongs to the trace class.

Theorem 8.1

(
[
184
, Theorem 2.2]
)
.

Let
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
m
odd, and suppose that
S_{0}
and
S
are self-adjoint operators in
{\mathcal{H}}
with

\big[(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}-(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

(8.6)

Then

[f(S)-f(S_{0})]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad f\in{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}({\mathbb{R}}),

(8.7)

and there exists a function

\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})\in L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\lambda|)^{-m-1}\,d\lambda\big)

(8.8)

such that the following trace formula holds,

\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}(f(S)-f(S_{0}))=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})f^{\prime}(\lambda),\quad f\in{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}({\mathbb{R}}).

(8.9)

In particular, one has

\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\big((S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}-(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}\big)=-m\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})d\lambda}{(\lambda-z)^{m+1}},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

(8.10)

Remark 8.2
.

The above theorem, together with Theorem
7.4
guarantees that for Dirac operators
H
and
H_{0}

in
[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
the spectral shift function
\xi(\,\cdot\,;H,H_{0})
exists. However, for the representation of the spectral shift function in terms of a regularized perturbation determinant it is desirable to take the regularized determinant
\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},p}((H-zI_{\mathcal{H}})(H_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1})
with
p
equal to
n+1
. Theorem
8.1
permits this in odd space dimensions
n
. In even space dimensions Theorem
8.1
does not guarantee the appropriate integrability of the spectral shift function
\xi(\,\cdot\,;H,H_{0})
and so one would be forced to consider a regularized determinant with
p=n+2
. To avoid this drawback, we prove that under a certain stronger condition (satisfied for Dirac operators
H
and
H_{0}
considered in Section
3
) an analogue of Theorem
8.1
holds for any
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

\diamond

Hypothesis 8.3
.

Let
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and assume that
S
and
S_{0}
are self-adjoint operators in
{\mathcal{H}}

with a common dense domain, such that

(S-S_{0})\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}),

(8.11)

and for some
0<\varepsilon<1/2
,

(S-S_{0})(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-(m/2)-\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.12)

Remark 8.4
.

(i)
Assuming Hypothesis
8.3
, it follows that

(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.13)

Since
(S-S_{0})\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
, it follows from the three line theorem that

(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/j}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad j\in{\mathbb{N}},\;1\leqslant j\leqslant m+1,

(8.14)

Furthermore, another application of the three line theorem implies

(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-j_{1}}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-j_{2}}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/(j_{1}+j_{2})}({\mathcal{H}})

(8.15)

for all
j_{1},j_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}},
with
1\leqslant j_{1}+j_{2}\leqslant m+1
.

(ii)
For the proof of Theorem
8.12
we will only need (
8.12
) and (
8.14
). We assumed boundedness of
S-S_{0}
only to get (
8.14
) as a consequence of (
8.12
). It is possible to go beyond this boundedness assumption, but we omit further details at this point.

(iii)
The inclusion (
7.37
) shows that assumption (
8.12
) holds with
m=n
for the pair of Dirac operators
(H,H_{0})
as long as
V
satisfies Hypothesis
7.1
.

\diamond

The following result appeared in
[
44
, Theorem 2.6]
.

Theorem 8.5
.

Assume Hypothesis
8.3
. For any
j=1,\dots,m
, one has

\left(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{-j}-\left(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{-j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/(j+1)}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.16)

Lemma 8.6
.

Assume Hypothesis
8.3
. For any
j=1,\dots,m
, and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
,
one has

\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big](S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.17)

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction on
j
. Let
j=1
. Using the resolvent identity twice one writes

\displaystyle\big[(S

\displaystyle-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big](S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+1}

\displaystyle=-(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+1}

\displaystyle=(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+1}

\displaystyle\quad-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+1}.

(8.18)

By (
8.15
) one obtains

(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),

(8.19)

and therefore the second term on the right-hand side of (
8
) is a trace-class operator.
By (
8.14
),

(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{m+1}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/m}({\mathcal{H}}),

(8.20)

guaranteeing that the first term on the right-hand side of (
8
) is also a trace-class operator. Thus, one concludes that

\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big](S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),

(8.21)

proving the first induction step.

Next, suppose that

\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big](S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})

(8.22)

for some
j=1,\dots m-1
. Writing

\displaystyle\big[(S

\displaystyle-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j-1}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j-1}\big](S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}

\displaystyle=\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big](S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}

\displaystyle\quad+(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big](S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}

(8.23)

\displaystyle\quad:=(I)+(II),

we will treat the terms
(I)
and
(II)
separately in the following.

For
(I)
on the right-hand side of (
8
) one gets

\displaystyle\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big](S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}

\displaystyle\quad=\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\times\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big](S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big](S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}

\displaystyle\quad=-\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big](S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\times(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}

(8.24)

\displaystyle\qquad+\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big](S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}.

By the induction hypothesis one concludes that

\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big](S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),

(8.25)

and therefore also

\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big](S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.26)

By Theorem
8.5
one obtains

\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/(j+1)}({\mathcal{H}}),

(8.27)

and by (
8.14
),

(S-S_{0})(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/(m-j)}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.28)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big](S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad\times(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/(j+1)}({\mathcal{H}})\cdot{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/(m-j)}({\mathcal{H}})\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),

(8.29)

\displaystyle\hskip 246.96971pt1\leqslant j\leqslant m-1.

Thus,
(I)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
.

To show that also
(II)
on the right-hand side of (
8
) is a trace-class operator, one writes

\displaystyle(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big](S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}

\displaystyle\quad=-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}

\displaystyle\quad=-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\times(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}

(8.30)

\displaystyle\qquad+(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}.

By (
8.14
) one infers

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j-1}(S-S_{0})\in{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/(j+1)}({\mathcal{H}}),\\
&(S-S_{0})(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/(m-j)}({\mathcal{H}}),\end{split}

(8.31)

and hence,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j}(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\\
&\quad\times(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).\end{split}

(8.32)

Furthermore, by (
8.15
),

(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad 1\leqslant j\leqslant m-1.

(8.33)

Thus, also
(II)
is a trace-class operator. Combining this with the fact that
(I)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
and referring to (
8
), one concludes that

\big[(S-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j-1}-(S_{0}-zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-j-1}\big](S_{0}+zI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-m+j+1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.34)

∎

From this point on we assume Hypothesis
8.3
for even
m=2k
for the remainder of this section. Introducing the function

\phi(t)=t\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{\frac{m-1}{2}}=t\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{k-(1/2)},\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}},

(8.35)

we aim at proving that

\big[(\phi(S)+iI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}-(\phi(S_{0})+iI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),

(8.36)

guaranteeing that the spectral shift function
\xi(\,\cdot\,;\phi(S),\psi(S_{0}))
is well-defined. Since

\phi^{\prime}(t)=\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{\frac{m-3}{2}}\big(1+mt^{2}\big)\geqslant 1>0,

(8.37)

it follows that
\phi
is a strictly monotone increasing function on
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Therefore, one can use the invariance principle for the spectral shift function (see
[
183
, Section 8.11]
)
to introduce
\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})
by setting

\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})=\xi(\phi(\lambda);\phi(S),\phi(S_{0}))\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}$.}

(8.38)

The choice of
\phi
and integrability properties of
\xi(\,\cdot\,;\phi(S),\phi(S_{0}))
will imply an appropriate integrability condition for
\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})
.

A crucial result in the proof of the inclusion (
8.36
) is the following result.
We recall that the Hölder space
C^{1,\alpha}([0,1])
,
0\leqslant\alpha\leqslant 1
, is the class of functions
f
on
[0,1]
such that

\|f\|_{C^{1,\alpha}([0,1])}=\|f\|_{C^{1}([0,1])}+\sup_{t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,1]}\frac{|f^{\prime}(t_{1})-f^{\prime}(t_{2})|}{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\alpha}}<\infty.

(8.39)

Theorem 8.7
.

[
134
, Theorem 4 and Corollary 2]

Suppose that
A
and
B
are self-adjoint operators on a Hilbert space, such that
(A-B)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
and

\sigma(A)\cup\sigma(B)\subset[0,1]
. For any function
f\in C^{1,\alpha}([0,1])
with
0<\alpha\leqslant 1
one
has

[f(A)-f(B)]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})

(8.40)

and

\|f(A)-f(B)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})}\leqslant C\|A-B\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})},

(8.41)

where the constant
C
is independent of
A
and
B
.

Assuming Hypothesis
8.3
with
m=2k
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we intend to use Theorem
8.7
for the operators
A=\big(S^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}
and
B=\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}.
In the following Lemma
8.8
we
will show that with this choice of operators
A,B
the condition
(A-B)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
of Theorem
8.7
is satisfied.

Lemma 8.8
.

Assume Hypothesis
8.3
with
m=2k
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then

\Big[\big(S^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}-\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}\Big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.42)

Proof.

One writes

\displaystyle\big(S^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}-\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}

\displaystyle\quad=(S+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}(S-iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}-(S_{0}+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}(S_{0}-iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}

\displaystyle\quad=\big[(S+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}-(S_{0}+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}\big]\big[(S-iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}-(S_{0}-iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}\big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\big[(S+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}-(S_{0}+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}\big](S_{0}-iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}

(8.43)

\displaystyle\qquad+(S_{0}+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}\big[(S-iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}-(S_{0}-iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}\big].

By Lemma
8.6
, the second and the third terms are trace-class operators.
By Theorem
8.5
one infers that

\big[(S+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}-(S_{0}+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-k}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/(k+1)}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.44)

Therefore, the first term on the right-hand side of (
8.43
) is a trace-class operator too.
∎

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and introduce the functions

h_{1}(t)=\frac{1}{t^{2}\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{2k-1}+1},\quad h_{2}(t)=\frac{\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{k}}{t^{2}\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{2k-1}+1},\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(8.45)

There exist
f_{1},f_{2}\in C^{1,\frac{1}{k}}([0,1])
such that

h_{1}(t)=f_{1}\Big(\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{-k}\Big),\quad h_{2}(t)=f_{2}\Big(\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{-k}\Big),\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(8.46)

Proof.

We set

f_{1}(u)=\frac{u^{2}}{1-u^{1/k}+u^{2}},\quad f_{2}(u)=\frac{u}{1-u^{1/k}+u^{2}},\quad u\in[0,1].

(8.47)

A direct verification shows that

h_{1}(t)=f_{1}\Big(\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{-k}\Big),\quad h_{2}(t)=f_{2}\Big(\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{-k}\Big),\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(8.48)

Since

1-u^{1/k}+u^{2}>0,\quad u\in[0,1],

(8.49)

f_{1},f_{2}\in C([0,1])
. By the fact
f_{1}(u)=uf_{2}(u),u\in[0,1]
, it suffices to show that
f_{2}\in C^{1,\frac{1}{k}}([0,1])
.
One verifies that

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{\big[1-u^{1/k}+u^{2}\big]-u\big[-\frac{1}{k}u^{1/k-1}+2u\big]}{\big[1-u^{1/k}+u^{2}\big]^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-[1-(1/k)]u^{1/k}-u^{2}}{\big[1-u^{1/k}+u^{2}\big]^{2}}.

(8.50)

Clearly
f_{j}\in C([0,1])
,
j=1,2
. Furthermore, since the map
u\mapsto u^{1/k}
is of Hölder class

C^{0,\frac{1}{k}}([0,1])
and the map
u\mapsto\big[1-u^{1/k}+u^{2}\big]^{-2}
is bounded on
[0,1]
, it follows that

f_{2}^{\prime}\in C^{0,\frac{1}{k}}([0,1])
, that is,
f_{2}\in C^{1,\frac{1}{k}}([0,1])
, as required.
∎

Lemma 8.10
.

Assume Hypothesis
8.3
with
m=2k
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
h_{2}
be as in Lemma
8.9
and introduce

g(t)=\frac{t}{\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{1/2}},\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(8.51)

Then,

[g(S)-g(S_{0})]h_{2}(S_{0})\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.52)

Proof.

Since

h_{2}(t)=\frac{\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{k}}{t^{2}\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{2k-1}+1}\underset{|t|\to\infty}{=}O\Big(\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{-k}\Big),

(8.53)

it suffices to show that

[g(S)-g(S_{0})]\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.54)

By
[
38
, Lemma 3.1]
,

g(S)-g(S_{0})=\frac{1}{\pi}\text{\rm Re}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda^{1/2}}\big[(S+i(\lambda+1)^{1/2}I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(S_{0}+i(\lambda+1)^{1/2}I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big]\bigg),

(8.55)

with a convergent Bochner integral in
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
. The substitution
\theta=(1+\lambda)^{1/2}
then yields

g(S)-g(S_{0})=\frac{1}{\pi}\text{\rm Re}\bigg(\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\theta d\theta}{(\theta^{2}-1)^{1/2}}\big[(S+i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(S_{0}+i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big]\bigg).

(8.56)

Therefore, it suffices to prove that

\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\theta d\theta}{(\theta^{2}-1)^{1/2}}\big[(S\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(S_{0}\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big]\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k},

(8.57)

are convergent integrals in
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
.

The resolvent identity implies

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\theta d\theta}{(\theta^{2}-1)^{1/2}}\big[(S\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(S_{0}\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big]\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}

\displaystyle\quad=-\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\theta d\theta}{(\theta^{2}-1)^{1/2}}(S\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}.

(8.58)

Let
0<\varepsilon<1/2
be as in Hypothesis
8.3
, that is,

(S-S_{0})\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k-\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.59)

One estimates

\displaystyle\Big\|(S\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}\Big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\big\|(S\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}\Big\|(S-S_{0})\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k-\varepsilon}\Big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})}\Big\|\big(S_{0}^{2}+\theta^{2}I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-1/2+\varepsilon}\Big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\theta^{-2+2\varepsilon}\Big\|(S-S_{0})\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k-\varepsilon}\Big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})},

(8.60)

implying,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\theta d\theta}{(\theta^{2}-1)^{1/2}}(S\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}\bigg\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{d\theta}{(\theta^{2}-1)^{1/2}\theta^{1-2\varepsilon}}\Big\|(S-S_{0})\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k-\varepsilon}\Big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})}.

(8.61)

Since
\varepsilon<1/2
, it follows that
\int_{1}^{\infty}d\theta(\theta^{2}-1)^{-1/2}\theta^{2\varepsilon-1}<\infty
and thus the integral

\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\theta d\theta}{(\theta^{2}-1)^{1/2}}(S\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}\pm i\theta I_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}

(8.62)

converges in
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
.
∎

Lemma 8.11
.

Assume Hypothesis
8.3
with
m=2k
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. For the function
\phi
introduced in (
8.35
) one concludes that

\big[(\phi(S)+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(\phi(S_{0})+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.63)

Proof.

One writes

\displaystyle[\phi(t)+i]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Big[t\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{k-\frac{1}{2}}+i\Big]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{t\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{k-\frac{1}{2}}}{t^{2}\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{2k-1}+1}-i\frac{1}{t^{2}\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{2k-1}+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{\big(t^{2}+1\big)^{1/2}}\,\frac{\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{k}}{t^{2}\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{2k-1}+1}-i\frac{1}{t^{2}\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{2k-1}+1},

(8.64)

that is,

[\phi(t)+i]^{-1}=g(t)h_{1}(t)-ih_{2}(t),\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}},

(8.65)

where
h_{1},h_{2}
are introduced in Lemma
8.9
and
g
in Lemma
8.10
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\big[(\phi(S)+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(\phi(S_{0})+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big]

\displaystyle\quad=g(S)h_{1}(S)-g(S_{0})h_{1}(S_{0})-i[h_{2}(S)-h_{2}(S_{0})]

\displaystyle\quad=[g(S)-g(S_{0})]h_{1}(S_{0})+g(S)[h_{1}(S)-h_{1}(S_{0})]-i[h_{2}(S)-h_{2}(S_{0})].

(8.66)

and by Lemma
8.10
one concludes that

[g(S)-g(S_{0})]h_{1}(S_{0})\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.67)

Thus, Lemma
8.9
implies

h_{j}(S)-h_{j}(S_{0})=f_{j}\Big(\big(S^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}\Big)-f_{j}\Big(\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}\Big),\quad j=1,2,

(8.68)

with
f_{j}\in C^{1,\frac{1}{k}}([0,1])
,
j=1,2
. Lemma
8.8
then yields

\Big[\big(S^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}-\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}\Big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(8.69)

Thus, by Theorem
8.7
one obtains

\Big[f_{j}\Big(\big(S^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}\Big)-f_{j}\Big(\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-k}\Big)\Big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad j=1,2,

(8.70)

and hence,

\big[(\phi(S)+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(\phi(S_{0})+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),

(8.71)

as required.
∎

The following theorem improves the integrability condition in (
8.8
) for even
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Theorem 8.12
.

Assume Hypothesis
8.3
with
m=2k
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. For any
f\in{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}({\mathbb{R}})
one has

[f(S)-f(S_{0})]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),

(8.72)

and there exists a function

\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})\in L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\lambda|)^{-m-1}\,d\lambda\big)

(8.73)

such that the following trace formula holds,

\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}(f(S)-f(S_{0}))=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})f^{\prime}(\lambda),\quad f\in{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}({\mathbb{R}}).

(8.74)

In particular, one has

\big[(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}-(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(8.75)

and

\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\big((S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}-(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}\big)=-m\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})d\lambda}{(\lambda-z)^{m+1}},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

(8.76)

Proof.

Let
\phi
be as in (
8.35
).
Then Lemma
8.11
implies that

\big[(\phi(S)+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}-(\phi(S_{0})+iI_{\mathcal{H}})^{-1}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),

(8.77)

and hence there exists the spectral shift function

\xi(\,\cdot\,;\phi(S),\phi(S_{0}))\in L^{1}\big([0,1];(1+|\lambda|)^{-2}d\lambda\big)

(8.78)

for the pair
(\phi(S),\phi(S_{0}))
.
Since

\phi^{\prime}(t)=\big(1+t^{2}\big)^{\frac{m-3}{2}}\big(1+mt^{2}\big)\geqslant 1>0,

(8.79)

it follows that
\phi
is strictly monotone increasing on
{\mathbb{R}}
. Hence, we introduce the spectral shift function

\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})
by setting

\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})=\xi(\phi(\lambda);\phi(S),\phi(S_{0}))\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}$.}

(8.80)

Since
\xi(\,\cdot\,;\phi(S),\phi(S_{0}))\in L^{1}\big([0,1];(1+|\lambda|)^{-2}d\lambda\big)
, the definition of
\phi
implies that

\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})\in L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\lambda|)^{-m-1}\,d\lambda\big).

(8.81)

Next, let
f\in{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}({\mathbb{R}})
. Then
f\circ\phi^{-1}\in{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}})
, and hence (
8.5
)
implies

\displaystyle\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\big(f(S)-f(S_{0})\big)

\displaystyle=\tr_{\mathcal{H}}\big((f\circ\phi^{-1})(\phi(S))-(f\circ\phi^{-1})(\phi(S_{0}))\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\xi(\mu;\phi(S),\phi(S_{0}))f^{\prime}(\phi^{-1}(\mu))\frac{d\mu}{\phi^{\prime}(\phi^{-1}(\mu))}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi(\phi(\lambda);\phi(S),\phi(S_{0}))f^{\prime}(\lambda)d\lambda

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})f^{\prime}(\lambda),

(8.82)

proving (
8.74
).

Since for any
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
the map
\lambda\mapsto(\lambda-z)^{-m}
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, belongs to the class
{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}({\mathbb{R}})
, the trace formula (
8.76
) is a particular case of formula (
8.74
).
∎

9.
Representing
\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})
in Terms of Regularized Fredholm Determinants

In this section we establish the representation of
\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})
in terms of regularized Fredholm determinants.

Hypothesis 9.1
.

Let
S_{0}
and
S
be self-adjoint operators in
{\mathcal{H}}
with
(S-S_{0})\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
.

(i)
If
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
is odd, assume (
8.6
), that is,

\big[(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}-(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

(9.1)

and

(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{(m+1)/j}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad j\in{\mathbb{N}},\;1\leqslant j\leqslant m+1.

(9.2)

(ii)
If
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
is even, assume the remaining conditions in Hypothesis
8.3
, that is,
for some
0<\varepsilon<1/2
,

(S-S_{0})\big(S_{0}^{2}+I_{\mathcal{H}}\big)^{-(m/2)-\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}).

(9.3)

By Remark
8.4

(i)
, (
9.2
) holds for odd and even
m
.

Remark 9.2
.

In the applications to multidimensional Dirac operators to be considered in the sequel, the number
m
in (
9.2
) is the dimension of the underlying Euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, that is,
m=n
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
n\geqslant 2
,
as detailed in Lemma
7.2
.
\diamond

By Theorem
8.5
, Hypothesis
9.1
implies

\big[(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}-(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(9.4)

for odd and even
m
.

By Theorem
8.12
the spectral shift function
\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})
exists and (
8.8
) and (
8.76
) hold. The main aim of the present section is to obtain an almost everywhere representation for
\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})
in terms of the regularized perturbation determinants of the operators
(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}
,
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
.

To set the stage, we begin by recalling some basic definitions and results pertaining to regularized determinants to be used in the sequel. For detailed discussions of regularized determinants, we refer to
[
158
, Chapters 3, 5, and 9]
and
[
183
, §1.7]
.

Let
\{h_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
denote an orthonormal basis for
{\mathcal{H}}
and suppose
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
. For each

N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
M_{N}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N\times N}
denote the matrix with entries

\delta_{j,k}+(h_{j},Ah_{k})_{{\mathcal{H}}},\quad 1\leqslant j,k\leqslant N.

(9.5)

The sequence
\{\Det_{{\mathbb{C}}^{N\times N}}(M_{N})\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
has a limit as
N\to\infty
, and its value does not depend on the orthonormal basis chosen. One defines the Fredholm determinant

\Det_{{\mathcal{H}}}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A):=\lim_{N\to\infty}\Det_{{\mathbb{C}}^{N\times N}}(M_{N}).

(9.6)

The Fredholm determinant is continuous with respect to
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})}
. That is, if
\{A_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|A_{n}-A\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})}=0
, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Det_{{\mathcal{H}}}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A_{n})=\Det_{{\mathcal{H}}}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A).

(9.7)

In fact, the Fredholm determinant is Fréchet differentiable with respect to
A
(cf., e.g.,
[
158
, Theorem 5.2]
).
Moreover, if
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
is an open set and
A:\Omega\to{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
is analytic, then the function
\Det_{{\mathcal{H}}}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A(\,\cdot\,))
is analytic in
\Omega
, and

\frac{d}{dz}\log\big(\Det_{{\mathcal{H}}}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A(z))\big)=\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\big((I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A(z))^{-1}A^{\prime}(z)\big).

(9.8)

The definition of the Fredholm determinant given in (
9.7
) is generally not meaningful if
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})
with
p\in{\mathbb{N}}\backslash\{1\}
. To give meaning to the determinant in this case, suitable modifications are needed. For
p\in{\mathbb{N}}\backslash\{1\}
, one introduces the function
R_{p}:{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
by

R_{p}(A)=(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A)e^{\sum_{j=1}^{p-1}(-1)^{j}j^{-1}A^{j}}-I_{{\mathcal{H}}},\quad A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\mathcal{H}}).

(9.9)

Then the regularized (or modified) Fredholm determinant is defined by

\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},p}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A)=\Det_{{\mathcal{H}}}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+R_{p}(A)),\quad A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\mathcal{H}}),\quad p\in{\mathbb{N}}\backslash\{1\}.

(9.10)

The Fredholm determinant
\det_{{\mathcal{H}},p}(\,\cdot\,)
retains many of the properties of the ordinary Fredholm determinant. For example,
\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},p}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A)
is continuous with respect to
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})
: if
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})
,
\{A_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})
, and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|A_{n}-A\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})}=0
, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},p}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A_{n})=\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},p}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A).

(9.11)

In addition, if
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
is open and
A:\Omega\to{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})
is analytic in
\Omega
, then the function
\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},p}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A(\,\cdot\,))
is analytic in
\Omega
and

\frac{d}{dz}\log\big(\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},p}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A(z))\big)=(-1)^{p-1}\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\big((I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A(z))^{-1}A^{p-1}(z)A^{\prime}(z)\big).

(9.12)

The importance of the regularized determinant stems from the fact that for
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})
, the operator
I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A
is boundedly invertible (i.e.,
-1\in\rho(A)
) if and only if
\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},p}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A)\neq 0
(cf., e.g.,
[
158
, Theorem 9.2]
). Equivalently,
-1\in\sigma(A)
if and only if
\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},p}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+A)=0
.

Finally, we note the cyclicity property of the regularized determinant: if
A,B\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
are such that
AB,BA\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})
, then

\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},p}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+AB)=\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},p}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+BA).

(9.13)

In particular, if
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})
and
B\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
, then (
9.13
) holds. Similarly, if
A,B\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
are such that
AB,BA\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
, then

\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}(AB)=\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}(BA).

(9.14)

With these preliminaries in hand, we start with introducing the regularized determinant associated with the (non-symmetrized) Birman–Schwinger-type operator

B(z):=(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

(9.15)

Lemma 9.3
.

Assume Hypothesis
9.1
. The map

B(z)=(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(9.16)

is a
{\mathcal{B}}_{m+1}({\mathcal{H}})
-valued analytic function.

Proof.

Let
z_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
be fixed. Then (see e.g.
[
182
, Theorem 5.14]
)

(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(S_{0}-z_{0}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-k-1}(z-z_{0})^{k},

(9.17)

where the series converges with respect to the
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
-norm for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
such that
|z-z_{0}|<\|(S_{0}-z_{0}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}^{-1}\leqslant|\text{\rm Im}(z_{0})|^{-1}
.
Therefore,

(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-z_{0}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-k-1}(z-z_{0})^{k}.

(9.18)

We claim that the latter series converges in the ball
\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\,|\,|z-z_{0}|<|\text{\rm Im}(z_{0})|\}
in the
{\mathcal{B}}_{m+1}({\mathcal{H}})
norm. By (
9.2
), the operator
B(z)=(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-z_{0}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{m+1}({\mathcal{H}})
, so that

\displaystyle\big\|(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-z_{0}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-k-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{m+1}({\mathcal{H}})}

(9.19)

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\big\|(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-z_{0}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{m+1}({\mathcal{H}})}\big\|(S_{0}-z_{0}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-k}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\big\|(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-z_{0}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{m+1}({\mathcal{H}})}|\text{\rm Im}(z_{0})|^{-k}.

This proves the convergence.
∎

Lemma 9.4
.

Assume Hypothesis
9.1
. The function

F_{S,S_{0}}(z):=\text{\rm ln}\big(\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},m+1}\big((S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big)\big),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(9.20)

is well-defined, in fact, analytic in
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

By the second resolvent identity,

(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}=I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+B(z),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(9.21)

where
B(\,\cdot\,)
is the
{\mathcal{B}}_{m+1}({\mathcal{H}})
-valued analytic function defined in (
9.16
). Hence, by the properties of regularized determinants the function

z\to\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},m+1}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+B(z))

(9.22)

is analytic in
{\mathbb{C}}_{\pm}
. Combining the Cauchy integral theorem and
[
127
, Theorem V.4.1]
, one infers that the function

z\to\text{\rm ln}\big(\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},m+1}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+B(z))\big)

(9.23)

is a well-defined analytic function in
{\mathbb{C}}_{\pm}
, provided that
\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},m+1}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+B(z))\neq 0
for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
. Thus, it remains to show, that
\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},m+1}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+B(z))\neq 0
for every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
. If
\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},m+1}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+B(z))=0
for some
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
, then
-1
is in the spectrum of
B(z).
By compactness of
B(\,\cdot\,)
,
-1
is an eigenvalue of
B(z),
and therefore,
I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+B(z)
has a nontrivial kernel. Since
S_{0}
is self-adjoint and hence
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
cannot be an eigenvalue of
S_{0}
,
z
is an eigenvalue for
S
, which, once more, cannot be the case since
S
is also self-adjoint.
∎

To correlate the function
\text{\rm ln}\big(\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},m+1}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+B(\,\cdot\,))\big)
with the spectral shift function for the pair
(S,S_{0})
, we need to introduce an auxiliary function.

Lemma 9.5
.

Assume Hypothesis
9.1
and let
B(\,\cdot\,)
be defined by (
9.16
). There exists an analytic function
G_{S,S_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)
in
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}G_{S,S_{0}}(z)=\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\Bigg(\frac{d^{m-1}}{dz^{m-1}}\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(-1)^{m-j}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}B(z)^{m-j}\Bigg),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

(9.24)

Proof.

It suffices to prove that each of the terms

\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\bigg(\frac{d^{m-1}}{dz^{m-1}}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}B(z)^{m-j}\bigg),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},\;j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0},\;0\leqslant j\leqslant m-1,

(9.25)

defines an analytic function. To analyze the operator under the trace in (
9.25
), we introduce multi-indices. Recalling
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}={\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
, for
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
an element
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\nu}
is called a multi-index which we express componentwise as

\underline{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{\nu})\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\nu},\quad k_{p}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0},\;1\leqslant p\leqslant\nu.

(9.26)

The order of the multi-index
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\nu}
is defined to be

|\underline{k}|:=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{\nu}.

(9.27)

For each fixed
j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
with
0\leqslant j\leqslant m-1
,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{m-1}}{dz^{m-1}}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}B(z)^{m-j}

(9.28)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{m-j+1}\\
|\underline{k}|=m-1\end{subarray}}c_{j,\underline{k}}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-(k_{1}+1)}\prod_{\ell=2}^{m-j+1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-I_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-(k_{\ell}+1)},

for an appropriate set of
z
-independent scalars

c_{j,\underline{k}}\in{\mathbb{R}},\quad\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{m-j+1},\;|\underline{k}|=m-1.

(9.29)

The assumption (
9.2
) and the analog of Hölder’s inequality for trace ideals (see
[
158
, Theorem 2.8]
) imply that each term in the sum in (
9.28
) is a trace class operator. In particular, (
9.28
) implies that the operator
\frac{d^{m-1}}{dz^{m-1}}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}B(z)^{m-j}
is a trace class operator. Repeating the argument in Lemma
9.3
and employing (
9.2
), one concludes that the map

{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}\ni z\to\frac{d^{m-1}}{dz^{m-1}}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}B(z)^{m-j},\quad 0\leqslant j\leqslant m-1,

(9.30)

is a
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
-valued analytic function.
∎

The following lemma is the main result, which allows to correlate the regularized determinant of the operator

I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}
and the spectral shift function
\xi(\,\cdot\,,S,S_{0})
(see Theorem
9.9
below).

Lemma 9.6
.

Assume Hypothesis
9.1
. If
F_{S,S_{0}}
and
G_{S,S_{0}}
denote the analytic functions in
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
introduced in (
9.20
) and (
9.24
), respectively, then there exist polynomials
P_{\pm,m-1}
of degree less than or equal to
m-1
such that

F_{S,S_{0}}(z)=(z-i)^{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})d\lambda}{(\lambda-i)^{m}}\frac{1}{\lambda-z}+G_{S,S_{0}}(z)+P_{\pm,m-1}(z),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\pm}.

(9.31)

Proof.

One recalls the
{\mathcal{B}}_{m+1}({\mathcal{H}})
-valued analytic function
B(\,\cdot\,)
defined in (
9.16
).
By the second resolvent identity,

\displaystyle\big(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+B(z)\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\big(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big)^{-1}

(9.32)

\displaystyle=\big((S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

In addition,

B^{\prime}(z)=B(z)(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

(9.33)

Applying (
9.12
), one obtains

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}F_{S,S_{0}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dz}\text{\rm ln}\big(\Det_{{\mathcal{H}},m+1}(I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+B(z))\big)

(9.34)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\big((S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}B(z)^{m}B(z)(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\big((S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}B(z)^{m+1}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big),

\displaystyle\hskip 224.7766ptz\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

For
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
,
(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
and
B(z)^{m+1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
(cf. (
9.2
)), so that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}F_{S,S_{0}}(z)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\big(B(z)^{m+1}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\big(B(z)^{m+1}(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\big((S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}B(z)^{m+1}\big),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

(9.35)

By the second resolvent identity,

\displaystyle(S-z)^{-1}B(z)

\displaystyle=(S-z)^{-1}(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-z)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(S_{0}-z)^{-1}-(S-z)^{-1},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(9.36)

and repeated application of (
9.36
) yields

\displaystyle(-1)^{m}(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}B(z)^{m+1}

(9.37)

\displaystyle\quad=(-1)^{m}\big((S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}-(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\big)B(z)^{m}

\displaystyle\quad=(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(-1)^{m-j}B(z)^{m-j}+(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}-(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1},

\displaystyle\hskip 267.45572ptz\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

Hence, combining (
9.35
) with (
9.37
), one obtains

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}F_{S,S_{0}}(z)

(9.38)

\displaystyle\quad=\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\bigg((S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}-(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}+(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(-1)^{m-j}B(z)^{m-j}\bigg),

\displaystyle\hskip 309.28131ptz\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

Differentiating (
9.38
)
m-1
times,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}F_{S,S_{0}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{m-1}}{dz^{m-1}}\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\bigg((S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}-(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}

(9.39)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt+(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(-1)^{m-j}B(z)^{m-j}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\bigg((m-1)!\Big((S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}-(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\frac{d^{m-1}}{dz^{m-1}}(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(-1)^{m-j}B(z)^{m-j}\bigg),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

By (
9.4
) and Lemma
9.5
, (
9.39
) may be recast as

\displaystyle\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}F_{S,S_{0}}(z)

\displaystyle=(m-1)!\tr_{{\mathcal{H}}}\Big((S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}-(S-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-m}\Big)

(9.40)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}G_{S,S_{0}}(z),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

and an application of Theorem
8.12
yields

\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}F_{S,S_{0}}(z)=m!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})\,d\lambda}{(\lambda-z)^{m+1}}+\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}G_{S,S_{0}}(z),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

(9.41)

Repeated application of the elementary identity

\frac{k!}{(\lambda-z)^{k+1}}=\frac{d}{dz}\Bigg(\frac{(k-1)!}{(\lambda-z)^{k}}-\frac{1}{(\lambda-i)^{k}}\Bigg),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}},\;k\in{\mathbb{N}},

(9.42)

yields

\displaystyle\frac{m!}{(\lambda-z)^{m+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\lambda-z}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{(z-i)^{j-1}}{(\lambda-i)^{j}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\lambda-z}-\frac{(z-i)^{m}-(\lambda-i)^{m}}{(z-\lambda)(\lambda-i)^{m}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}\Bigg(\frac{(z-i)^{m}}{(\lambda-z)(\lambda-i)^{m}}\Bigg),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(9.43)

Therefore, (
9.41
) and (
9.43
) imply

\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}F_{S,S_{0}}(z)=\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})d\lambda\,(z-i)^{m}}{(\lambda-i)^{m}(\lambda-z)}+\frac{d^{m}}{dz^{m}}G_{S,S_{0}}(z),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(9.44)

completing the proof.
∎

Remark 9.7
.

For bookkeeping purposes we have thus far worked with the non-symmetrized Birman–Schwinger-type operator

B(z):=(S-S_{0})(S_{0}-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}
,
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
and avoided a factorization of
S-S_{0}
(and similarly we exploited
V
without its factorization (
3.37
) in Section
7
). In the concrete case of massless Dirac operators
H_{0},H
we will eventually switch over to a symmetrized analog (cf. (
2.19
)–(
2.27
), (
10.146
)).

\diamond

The main result of this section provides a means for recovering the spectral shift function
\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})
almost everywhere in terms of the normal (or nontangential) boundary values of the functions
F_{S,S_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)
and
G_{S,S_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)
when the latter exist. Its proof relies on the following (special case of) Privalov’s theorem (see, e.g.,
[
183
, Theorem 1.2.5]
).

Theorem 9.8
.

Let
\theta\in L^{1}\big(\mathbb{R};(1+|\lambda|)^{-1}\,d\lambda\big)
. If

H(z):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\theta(\lambda)d\lambda}{\lambda-z},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(9.45)

then

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}H(\lambda\pm i\varepsilon)=\pm\pi i\theta(\lambda)+p.v.\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{\theta(\lambda^{\prime})d\lambda^{\prime}}{\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda}\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}$},

(9.46)

where
p.v.\,(\,\cdot\,)
abbreviates the principal value operation. In particular, one obtains the following special case of the Stieltjes inversion theorem,

\theta(\lambda)=(2\pi i)^{-1}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}[H(\lambda+i\varepsilon))-H(\lambda-i\varepsilon))]\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}$.}

(9.47)

Moreover, the normal limits in (
9.46
) and (
9.47
) can be replaced by nontangential limits.

Theorem 9.9
.

Assume Hypothesis
9.1
and let
F_{S,S_{0}}
and
G_{S,S_{0}}
denote analytic functions in
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying (
9.20
) and (
9.24
), respectively. If
F_{S,S_{0}}
and
G_{S,S_{0}}
have normal boundary values on
{\mathbb{R}}
, then for a.e.
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})=\pi^{-1}\text{\rm Im}(F_{S,S_{0}}(\lambda+i0))-\pi^{-1}\text{\rm Im}(G_{S,S_{0}}(\lambda+i0))+P_{m-1}(\lambda)\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}$},

(9.48)

where
P_{m-1}
is a polynomial of degree less than or equal to
m-1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.6
,

F_{S,S_{0}}(z)=(z-i)^{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})d\lambda}{(\lambda-z)(\lambda-i)^{m}}\,+G_{S,S_{0}}(z)+P_{\pm,m-1}(z),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\pm}.

(9.49)

If

H_{\pm}(z):=F_{S,S_{0}}(z)-G_{S,S_{0}}(z)-P_{\pm,m-1}(z),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\pm},

(9.50)

then (
9.49
) may be recast as

\frac{H_{\pm}(z)}{(z-i)^{m}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})d\lambda}{(\lambda-z)(\lambda-i)^{m}},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\pm},\;z\neq i.

(9.51)

By (
8.8
), the spectral shift function
\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})
satisfies

\xi(\,\cdot\,;S,S_{0})(\,\cdot\,-i)^{-m}\in L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\lambda|)^{-1}\,d\lambda\big).

(9.52)

An application of Theorem
9.8
then yields

\displaystyle\frac{\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})}{(\lambda-i)^{m}}=(2\pi i)^{-1}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\Bigg[\frac{H_{+}(\lambda+i\varepsilon)}{(\lambda+i\varepsilon-i)^{m}}-\frac{H_{-}(\lambda-i\varepsilon)}{(\lambda-i\varepsilon-i)^{m}}\Bigg]\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}$},

(9.53)

and hence,

\displaystyle\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})

\displaystyle=(2\pi i)^{-1}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}[H_{+}(\lambda-i\varepsilon)-H_{-}(\lambda+i\varepsilon)]\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}$}.

(9.54)

It follows from the definition of the functions
F_{S,S_{0}}
and
G_{S,S_{0}}
(cf. Lemmas
9.4
and
9.5
) that

F_{S,S_{0}}(\bar{z})=\overline{F_{S,S_{0}}(z)},\quad G_{S,S_{0}}(\bar{z})=\overline{G_{S,S_{0}}(z)},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

(9.55)

Thus, by (
9.54
),

\displaystyle\xi(\lambda;S,S_{0})

\displaystyle=(2\pi i)^{-1}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}[F_{S,S_{0}}(\lambda+i\varepsilon)-F_{S,S_{0}}(\lambda-i\varepsilon)]

\displaystyle\quad-(2\pi i)^{-1}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}[G_{S,S_{0}}(\lambda+i\varepsilon)+G_{S,S_{0}}(\lambda-i\varepsilon)]

\displaystyle\quad-(2\pi i)^{-1}[P_{+,m-1}(\lambda)-P_{-,m-1}(\lambda)]

\displaystyle=\pi^{-1}\text{\rm Im}(F_{S,S_{0}}(\lambda+i0))-\pi^{-1}\text{\rm Im}(G_{S,S_{0}}(\lambda+i0))+P_{m-1}(\lambda)

(9.56)

\displaystyle\hskip 200.59201pt\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}$},

where the polynomial
P_{m-1}:=(2\pi i)^{-1}[P_{-,m-1}-P_{+,m-1}]
has degree less than or equal to
m-1
(since

P_{\pm,m-1}
have degree less than or equal to
m-1
).
∎

10.
Analysis of
\text{\rm Im}(F_{H,H_{0}}(\lambda+i0))
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}

The principal purpose of this section is to analyze continuity properties of the function
\text{\rm Im}(F_{H,H_{0}}(\lambda+i0))
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

One recalls (see Lemma
9.4
) that

F_{H,H_{0}}(z)=\text{\rm ln}\big({\det}_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},n+1}\big(I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}\big)\big),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

(10.1)

Using a factorization
V=V_{1}^{*}V_{2}
(see Hypothesis
10.5
for the details of the factorization) and elementary properties of regularized determinants, the analysis of the function
F_{H,H_{0}}(z)
reduces to an analysis of

\text{\rm ln}\big({\det}_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},n+1}\big(I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{L_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}\big)\big),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

(10.2)

Theorem
6.16
then guarantees that the Birman–Schwinger-type operator

V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{L_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

(10.3)

extends to a continuous
{\mathcal{B}}_{n+1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
-valued function for
z
in the closed upper-half plane

\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}
, provided that
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are decaying sufficiently fast. In particular, the boundary values of the regularized Fredholm determinant,

{\det}_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},n+1}\big(I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(\lambda+i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big),

(10.4)

exist and are continuous for all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
. This means that the function
F_{H,H_{0}}(\lambda+i0)
has normal boundary values and is continuous at any point
\lambda
in
{\mathbb{R}}
such that

{\det}_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},n+1}\big(I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(\lambda+i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big)\neq 0.

(10.5)

By Theorem
3.4
, the latter holds for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
if and only if
\lambda
is not an eigenvalue of
H
. By
[
103
]
one can exclude nonzero eigenvalues by assuming (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) (see Theorem
4.1
). In particular, under these assumptions the function
\text{\rm Im}(F_{H,H_{0}}(\lambda+i0))
is continuous for

\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
. Thus, the only point where the behavior
\text{\rm Im}(F_{H,H_{0}}(\lambda+i0))
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, remains to be studied is the “threshold point”
\lambda=0
, and hence the majority of this section is devoted to an analysis of the latter.

We start with a series of well-known preliminary results which we state without proof closely following the general outline in the paper by Jensen and Nenciu
[
98
]
.

Lemma 10.1
.

(i)

(
cf.
[
98
]
)
. Let
A
be a densely defined closed operator and
P
a projection in
{\mathcal{H}}
. Suppose that
(A+P)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
and denote by
a:=P-P(A+P)^{-1}P
an operator in
P{\mathcal{H}}
. Then

A^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})\,\text{ if and only if }\,a^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}(P{\mathcal{H}}).

(10.6)

In particular, if
a^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}(P{\mathcal{H}})
then

A^{-1}=(A+P)^{-1}+(A+P)^{-1}Pa^{-1}P(A+P)^{-1}.

(10.7)

(ii)

(
cf.
[
75
]
,
[
107
, Sect. III.6.5]
)
. Let
A
be a densely defined closed operator in
{\mathcal{H}}
and

\lambda_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}
an isolated point in
\sigma(A)
with
P_{\lambda_{0}}
the Riesz projection in
{\mathcal{H}}

associated with
A
and
\lambda_{0}
. If the quasi-nilpotent operator associated with
A
and
\lambda_{0}
vanishes, that is,
D_{0}:=(A-\lambda_{0}I_{{\mathcal{H}}})P_{\lambda_{0}}=0
, then

(A-\lambda_{0}I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+P_{\lambda_{0}})^{-1}=P_{\lambda_{0}}+S_{\lambda_{0}}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}),

(10.8)

where

S_{\lambda_{0}}=\nlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\to\lambda_{0}\\
z\neq\lambda_{0}\end{subarray}}(A-zI_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{-1}[I_{{\mathcal{H}}}-P_{\lambda_{0}}]\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}).

(10.9)

(iii)

(
cf.
[
137
]
)
. Let
A
be a compact operator in
{\mathcal{H}}
and
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}
an isolated point in
\sigma(A)
with
P_{\lambda_{0}}
the Riesz projection in
{\mathcal{H}}

associated with
A
and
\lambda_{0}
. Then

(A-\lambda_{0}I_{{\mathcal{H}}}+P_{\lambda_{0}})^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}).

(10.10)

(iv)

(
cf.
[
98
]
)
. Suppose that
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
and
{\mathfrak{B}}
in
{\mathcal{H}}
is the block operator
matrix

{\mathfrak{B}}=\begin{pmatrix}b_{1,1}&b_{1,2}\\
b_{2,1}&b_{2,2}\end{pmatrix},

(10.11)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}&b_{j,j}\,\text{ are densely defined, closed operators in ${\mathcal{H}}_{j}$, $j=1,2$,}\\
&b_{1,2}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2},{\mathcal{H}}_{1}),\quad b_{2,1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1},{\mathcal{H}}_{2}).\end{split}

(10.12)

In addition, assume that
b_{2,2}^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2})
. Then

{\mathfrak{B}}^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})\,\text{ if and only if }\,\big[b_{1,1}-b_{1,2}\,b_{2,2}^{-1}\,b_{2,1}\big]^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1}).

(10.13)

In particular, abbreviating

b:=\big[b_{1,1}-b_{1,2}\,b_{2,2}^{-1}\,b_{2,1}\big],

(10.14)

if
b^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})
,
then

{\mathfrak{B}}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}b^{-1}&-b^{-1}\,b_{1,2}\,b_{2,2}^{-1}\\
-b_{2,2}^{-1}\,b_{2,1}\,b^{-1}&b_{2,2}^{-1}+b_{2,2}^{-1}\,b_{2,1}\,b^{-1}\,b_{1,2}\,b_{2,2}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

(10.15)

We emphasize that
b
in Lemma
10.1

(iv)
is also known as a Schur complement (see, e.g.,
[
169
, Sect. 1.6]
) and formula (
10.15
) is a variant of the so-called Feshbach formula (see, e.g.,
[
50
]
). In particular, Lemma
10.1

(iv)
is especially useful in the context of two-dimensional Schrödinger operators (cf.
[
98
]
) as well as two-dimensional massless Dirac operators (cf.
[
60
]
).

Lemma 10.2

(
[
98
]
)
.

Suppose that
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}
has zero as an accumulation point.

(i)
Let
A(\zeta)=A_{0}+\zeta A_{1}(\zeta)
,
\zeta\in\Omega
, be a family of
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
-valued operators,
with
A_{1}(\,\cdot\,)
uniformly bounded for
\zeta\in\Omega
sufficiently small. Suppose that
0
is an
isolated point in
\sigma(A_{0})
and denote by
P_{0}
the Riesz projection in
{\mathcal{H}}
associated with
A_{0}

and
0
. If
A_{0}P_{0}=0

(
i.e., the quasi-nilpotent operator associated with
A_{0}
and
0
vanishes
)
,
then for
\zeta\in\Omega
sufficiently small, the operator
B(\,\cdot\,)
in
P_{0}{\mathcal{H}}
, defined by

B(\zeta):=\zeta^{-1}\big\{P_{0}-P_{0}[A(\zeta)+P_{0}]^{-1}P_{0}\big\}=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}(-\zeta)^{j}P_{0}\big[A_{1}(\zeta)(A_{0}+P_{0})^{-1}\big]^{j+1}P_{0},

(10.16)

is uniformly bounded as
\zeta\to 0
. Moreover, for
\zeta\in\Omega
sufficiently small,

A(\zeta)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})\,\text{ if and only if }\,B(\zeta)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}(P_{0}{\mathcal{H}}).

(10.17)

In particular, if
B(\zeta)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}(P_{0}{\mathcal{H}})
for
\zeta\in\Omega
sufficiently small, then

A(\zeta)^{-1}=[A(\zeta)+P_{0}]^{-1}+\zeta^{-1}[A(\zeta)+P_{0}]^{-1}P_{0}B(\zeta)^{-1}P_{0}[A(\zeta)+P_{0}]^{-1}.

(10.18)

Remark 10.3
.

A combined application of Lemma
10.1

(iv)
and Lemma
10.2
can be realized in the following
scenario: Suppose

\displaystyle b_{1,1}(\zeta)=\zeta^{-1}[b_{0}+\beta(\zeta)],\,\text{ with $b_{0}^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})$ and
$\|\beta(\zeta)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta\to 0\\
\zeta\in\Omega\end{subarray}}{=}o(1)$},

\displaystyle b_{2,2}(\zeta)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2})\,\text{ is uniformly bounded for $\zeta\in\Omega$ sufficiently small,}

(10.19)

\displaystyle b_{1,2}(\zeta)\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2},{\mathcal{H}}_{1}),\quad b_{2,1}(\zeta)\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1},{\mathcal{H}}_{2})\,\text{ are uniformly bounded for $\zeta\in\Omega$}

sufficiently small.

Then

b_{1,1}(\zeta)^{-1}=\zeta b_{0}^{-1}\big[I_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}+\beta(\zeta)b_{0}^{-1}\big]^{-1},\quad\big\|b_{1,1}(\zeta)^{-1}\big\|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta\to 0\\
\zeta\in\Omega\end{subarray}}{=}O(\zeta),

(10.20)

and under these circumstances one then infers, with

b(\zeta):=\big[b_{1,1}(\zeta)-b_{1,2}(\zeta)\,b_{2,2}^{-1}(\zeta)\,b_{2,1}(\zeta)\big]

(10.21)

(cf. (
10.13
)), that for
\zeta\in\Omega
sufficiently small,

b(\zeta)^{-1}=b_{1,1}(\zeta)^{-1}\big[I_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}-b_{1,2}(\zeta)\,b_{2,2}(\zeta)^{-1}\,b_{2,1}(\zeta)\,b_{1,1}(\zeta)^{-1}\big]^{-1}.

(10.22)

\diamond

At this point one can summarize the strategy in deriving threshold expansions of resolvents described in Jensen and Nenciu
[
98
]
(see also Murata
[
121
]
), in fact, in our context, expansions of

\displaystyle\begin{split}&V_{2}(H-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}=I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}\\
&\quad-\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}\big]^{-1},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},\end{split}

(10.23)

in terms of the (symmetrized) Birman–Schwinger-type operator

V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}

(10.24)

(cf. Theorem
3.4
) around
z=0
as follows:

(\alpha)
One notes upon combining (
B.1
)–(
B.8
) and (
5.15
) that treating even dimensions
n
is considerably more involved than the case of odd dimensions
n
due to the presence of the logarithm
2
2

2

This is even more pronounced in the case of Schrödinger operators for
n=2
due to the logarithmic blowup of the Green’s function (
5
) as
z\to 0
. Actually, in the Schrödinger context even the one-dimensional case exhibits a
z^{-1/2}
singularity at
z=0
, rendering both cases more involved than
n\geqslant 3
. Since the Dirac Green’s matrix never exhibits a blowup as
z\to 0
in all dimensions
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
n\geqslant 2
(cf. (
5.16
)), this renders the massless Dirac situation technically a bit simpler than the case of one and two-dimensional Schrödinger operators (considered in great detail in
[
98
]
).
in (
B.4
). At any rate, formulas (
B.1
)–(
B.8
) and (
5.15
) permit one to expand the Birman–Schwinger-type operator
(
10.24
) around
z=0
assuming sufficient decay of
V_{1}^{*}(x),V_{2}(x)
as

|x|\to\infty
. This step is cumbersome, but poses no further difficulties. What might cause difficulties is an expansion of the left-hand side of (
10.23
), or, equivalently, an expansion of the inverse

\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}\big]^{-1}
on the right-hand side of
(
10.23
).

(\beta)
If this inverse exists boundedly in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
z=0
, that is, if

\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}\big]^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)

(10.25)

for
|z|
sufficiently small, then no difficulty arises and a geometric series argument yields the existence of such an expansion in norm (cf. Section
5
), given sufficient decay of
V_{1}^{*}(x),V_{2}(x)
as
|x|\to\infty
also in appropriate trace ideal norms (cf. the detailed treatment in Section
6
). This is actually the generic case where
H
has no zero-energy eigenvalue and no zero-energy resonance (the latter is defined as giving rise to an eigenvalue
-1
of the Birman–Schwinger-type operator (
10.24
) but with no associated

L^{2}
-eigenfunction in the domain of
H
). At this point all that remains is a computation of the expansion coefficients, but the latter is of limited urgency in our present context as we will primarily rely on the leading order in all expansions.

(\gamma)
If the inverse in (
10.25
) does not exist boundedly in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
z=0
, that is, if the compact operator
V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}
has an eigenvalue
-1
, the situation changes drastically. In this case
H
either has an eigenvalue
0
, or zero-energy resonances, or possibly both, a zero-energy eigenvalue and zero-energy resonances (all of them possibly degenerate) in the worst case scenario. In any of these (exceptional) situations the norm of

\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}\big]^{-1}

(10.26)

and hence that of

V_{2}(H-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}

(10.27)

will exhibit a singularity as
z\to 0
. Without going into details in this summary (see, however, Theorem
10.14
), we note that the blowup in the case of zero-energy eigenvalue(s) is of the order
z^{-1}
, and in the presence of zero-energy resonances (but no zero-energy eigenvalues) is of a less singular structure, for instance, like
z^{-1}[\text{\rm ln}(z)]^{-1}
,
z^{-1/2}
, or
\text{\rm ln}(z)
, etc., the details now depending crucially on the space dimension
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
n\geqslant 2
, and whether Schrödinger or Dirac operators (massive or massless) are considered.

But even though
\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}\big]
does not possess a bounded inverse as
z\to 0
, the operator

\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}+P_{0}\big],

(10.28)

where
P_{0}
is the (finite-dimensional) Riesz projection associated with the operator

I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(0+i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}},

(10.29)

and its eigenvalue
0
, the norm limit of

I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V_{2}(H_{0}-i\varepsilon I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}

(10.30)

as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, and its eigenvalue
0
, actually has a bounded inverse according to Lemma
10.1

(ii)
. (Assuming compactness of the operators

\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(0+i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}
as well as

V_{2}(H_{0}-i\varepsilon I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}
, one concludes that
\dim(\ran(P_{0}))<\infty
.) Lemma
10.2
then demonstrates the key reduction step where the inverse of
A(\zeta)

in
{\mathcal{H}}
is now reduced to the inverse of
B(\zeta)
in the finite-dimensional Hilbert space
P_{0}{\mathcal{H}}
.

(\delta)
At this point one iterates the procedure ending up localizing the singularity in subspaces of decreasing dimensions. With each step the singularity is increased. However, since

z\big[\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]

(10.31)

stays bounded for
z=i\varepsilon
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, the reduction process must stop after a finite number of steps, leading to invertibility of a reduced operator so that again a geometric series argument as in step
(\beta)
applies. This completes the process resulting in an expansion in appropriate variables involving
z
,
z^{1/2}
,
\text{\rm ln}(z)
, or
[c+\text{\rm ln}(z)]
for appropriate
c\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0\}
(again, depending on spatial dimension
n
and whether Schrödinger or Dirac operators are involved). We refer once more to
[
98
]
for the somewhat involved details (and the difficulties associated with expansions involving

\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\zeta^{k}[\text{\rm ln}(\zeta)]^{\ell}
which cannot be asymptotic in nature) in the case of Schrödinger operators and to
[
60
]
in the case of two-dimensional massless Dirac
operators. Much of the threshold analysis in
[
60
]
readily extends to dimensions
n\geqslant 3
as we will see later in this section.

Remark 10.4
.

In outlining steps
(\alpha)
–
(\delta)
above, we deliberately sidestepped verifying the condition
A_{0}P_{0}=0
necessary for Lemma
10.2
to hold. The condition is equivalent to the statement that the algebraic and geometric multiplicities of the eigenvalue
0
of
A_{0}
coincide. Since by (
5.16
),
G_{0}(0+i\,0;x,y)
is purely imaginary, but also involves the scalar product
\alpha\cdot(x-y)
, employing the polar decomposition for the self-adjoint
N\times N
matrix
V(\,\cdot\,)
(i.e.,
V(\,\cdot\,)=U_{V}(\,\cdot\,)|V(\,\cdot\,)|
) in the form (cf.
[
81
]
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&V(x)=|V(x)|^{1/2}U_{V}(x)|V(x)|^{1/2}=V_{1}(x)^{*}V_{2}(x)\,\text{ for a.e.~$x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}$,}\\
&V_{1}=V_{1}^{*}=|V|^{1/2},\quad V_{2}=U_{V}|V|^{1/2}=U_{V}V_{1},\quad U_{V}^{2}=I_{N},\end{split}

(10.32)

making the choice that

U_{V}
is unitary and self-adjoint

(10.33)

(the choice of
U_{V}
is nonunique if
V
has a kernel and we simply fix
U_{V}
to be the identity operator on
\ker(V)
), the matrix-valued integral kernel

|V(x)|^{1/2}(x)G_{0}(0+i\,0;x,y)|V(y)|^{1/2}

(10.34)

generates a self-adjoint operator. Hence, the elegant device used in
[
98
]
that reduces their analysis to a self-adjoint operator
A_{0}
in Lemma
10.2
, so that
A_{0}P_{0}=0
is automatically satisfied, applies also in the massless Dirac operator context. (Naturally, this approach of
[
98
]
also applies in the massive case, where
H_{0}(m)
,
m>0
, has the spectral gap
(-m,m)
.) In essence, Jensen and Nenciu
[
98
]
replace the operator

I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(10.35)

by its modification

U_{V}I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V_{1}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

(10.36)

and show that the formalism displayed in (
2.19
)–(
2.27
) instantly extends to the setup in (
10.36
).
In particular, the norm limit

U_{V}I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+\overline{V_{1}(H_{0}-(0+i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}

(10.37)

is now self-adjoint and hence the analog of the condition

A_{0}P_{0}=0

(10.38)

thus holds automatically.
Due to this fact we can, without loss of generality, safely disregard the distinction between (
10.35
) and (
10.36
) in much of the remainder of this manuscript.

Finally, by an abuse of notation, we also denote the Riesz projection associated with the self-adjoint operator (
10.37
) and its eigenvalue
0
by
P_{0}
. Assuming compactness of the operator

\overline{V_{1}(H_{0}-(0+i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}},

(10.39)

the fact that
\sigma(U_{V})\subseteq\{1,-1\}
implies that zero is an isolated point in the spectrum of the operator in (
10.37
) and hence

\dim(\ran(P_{0}))<\infty.

(10.40)

(In the concrete context of (
10.32
) one has in addition that
V_{1}=V_{1}^{*}
, but this simplification is not needed to conclude (
10.38
) and (
10.40
).)
\diamond

Applying the resolvent equation (
2.21
), (
2.22
) to the pair
H,H_{0}
results in

\displaystyle(H-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1}=(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1}-\big[V_{1}(H_{0}-{\overline{z}}I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1}\big]^{*}

\displaystyle\quad\times\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}\big]^{-1}V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1},

(10.41)

\displaystyle\hskip 271.72389ptz\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}},

To analyze the possible singularity of
(H-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1}
as
z\to 0
, we choose
arbitrary

\psi_{j}\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\,\text{ real-valued, }\,j=1,2,

(10.42)

and consider

\displaystyle\psi_{2}I_{N}(H-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1}\psi_{1}I_{N}=\psi_{2}I_{N}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1}\psi_{1}I_{N}

\displaystyle\quad-\big[V_{1}(H_{0}-{\overline{z}}I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1}\psi_{2}I_{N}\big]^{*}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}\big]^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\times V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1}\psi_{1}I_{N},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.

(10.43)

As long as

\big||V|^{1/2}_{\ell,m}(x)\big|=|V_{1,\ell,m}(x)|\leqslant C\langle x\rangle^{-1}\,\text{ for a.e.~$x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}$, $1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N$,}

(10.44)

Theorem
6.7

(iii)
implies that

V_{j}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1}\psi_{j^{\prime}}I_{N}\in{\mathcal{B}}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}},\;j,j^{\prime}\in\{1,2\},

(10.45)

since obviously

\psi_{2}I_{N}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1}\psi_{1}I_{N}\in{\mathcal{B}}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}},

(10.46)

(in fact, Theorem
6.13
implies trace ideal properties) one also has

\psi_{2}I_{N}(H-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1}\psi_{1}I_{N}\in{\mathcal{B}}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}.

(10.47)

Thus, since
\psi_{j}\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
,
j=1,2
, are arbitrary (apart from being real-valued for
simplicity), one thus concludes that

\displaystyle\psi_{2}I_{N}(H-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}]^{N}})^{-1}\psi_{1}I_{N}\in{\mathcal{B}}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\,\text{ for $|z|$ sufficiently small}

if and only if

(10.48)

\displaystyle\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)

for
|z|
sufficiently small.

Given the extensive treatment in
[
98
]
in the case of Schrödinger operators in dimensions
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
(especially, in the most difficult of cases
n=1,2
), and in
[
60
]
in the case of massless Dirac operators in dimension
n=2
, and given the fact that dimensions
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

n\geqslant 3
, subordinate in difficulty to the case
n=2
in the massless context, we now briefly discuss the threshold (i.e.,
z=0
) behavior of massless Dirac operator in dimensions
n\geqslant 2
.

We start by making the following assumptions on the matrix-valued potential
V
.

Hypothesis 10.5
.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
n\geqslant 2
, and
\varepsilon>0
. Assume the a.e. self-adjoint matrix-valued potential

V=\{V_{\ell,m}\}_{1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N}
satisfies for some fixed
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
C\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&V\in[L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N\times N},\\
&|V_{\ell,m}(x)|\leqslant C\langle x\rangle^{-2(1+\varepsilon)}\,\text{ for a.e.~$x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}$, $1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N$.}\end{split}

(10.49)

In accordance with the factorization based on the polar decomposition of
V
discussed in (
10.32
) we
suppose that

V=V_{1}^{*}V_{2}=|V|^{1/2}U_{V}|V|^{1/2},\,\text{ where }\,V_{1}=V_{1}^{*}=|V|^{1/2},\;V_{2}=U_{V}|V|^{1/2}.

(10.50)

We continue with the threshold, that is, the
z=0
behavior of
H
:

Definition 10.6
.

Assume Hypothesis
10.5
with
\varepsilon=0
in (
10.49
).

(i)
The point
0
is called a zero-energy eigenvalue of
H
if
H\psi=0
has a distributional solution
\psi
satisfying

\psi\in\dom(H)=[W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}

(10.51)

(
equivalently,
\ker(H)\supsetneqq\{0\}
)
.

(ii)
The point
0
is called a zero-energy
(
or threshold
)
resonance of
H
if

\ker\big(\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(0+i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]\big)\supsetneqq\{0\},

(10.52)

and if there exists
0\neq\phi\in\ker\big(\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(0+i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]\big)
such that
\psi
defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\psi(x)=-\big((H_{0}-(0+i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}\phi\big)(x)\\
&\hskip 24.18483pt=-i2^{-1}\pi^{-n/2}\Gamma(n/2)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}y\,|x-y|^{-n}[\alpha\cdot(x-y)]V_{1}(y)^{*}\phi(y)\end{split}

(10.53)

(
for a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
n\geqslant 2
)
is a distributional solution of
Hu=0
satisfying

\psi\notin[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}.

(10.54)

(iii)

0
is called a regular point for
H
if it is neither a zero-energy eigenvalue nor a zero-energy
resonance of
H
.

Additional properties of
\psi
are isolated in Theorem
10.7
.

While the point
0
being regular for
H
is the generic situation, zero-energy eigenvalues and/or resonances are exceptional cases.

For future purposes we recall the asymptotic Green’s matrix expansion as
z\to 0
in the following form,

\displaystyle G_{0}(z;x,y)

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}z\to 0\\
z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\backslash\{0\}\end{subarray}}{=}i2^{-1}\pi^{-n/2}\Gamma(n/2)\alpha\cdot\frac{(x-y)}{|x-y|^{n}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\;-\delta_{n,2}(2\pi)^{-1}z\text{\rm ln}(z)I_{N}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\;+\delta_{n,2}(2\pi)^{-1}[\gamma_{E-M}-i(\pi/2)+\text{\rm ln}(|x-y|/2))]zI_{N}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\;+[1-\delta_{n,2}](n-2)^{-1}2^{-1}\pi^{-n/2}\Gamma(n/2)|x-y|^{2-n}zI_{N}

(10.55)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\;+\delta_{n,2}O\big(z^{2}|x-y|\text{\rm ln}(z|x-y|)\big)+\delta_{n,3}O\big(z^{2}\big)+O\big(z^{2}|x-y|^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}z\to 0\\
z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\backslash\{0\}\end{subarray}}{=}R_{0,0}(x-y)+zR_{1,0}(x-y)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\;+z\big[-(2\pi)^{-1}\text{\rm ln}(z/2)-(2\pi)^{-1}\gamma_{E-M}+i4^{-1}\big]\delta_{n,2}R_{1,1}(x-y)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\;+\delta_{n,2}O\big(z^{2}|x-y|\text{\rm ln}(z|x-y|)\big)+\delta_{n,3}O\big(z^{2}\big)+O\big(z^{2}|x-y|^{2}\big),

(10.56)

where we introduced the following convenient abbreviations (for
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
x\neq y
):

\displaystyle R_{0,0}(x-y)

\displaystyle=G_{0}(0+i0;x,y)=i2^{-1}\pi^{-n/2}\Gamma(n/2)\,\alpha\cdot\frac{(x-y)}{|x-y|^{n}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(2\pi)^{-1}i\alpha\cdot\nabla_{x}\text{\rm ln}(|x-y|),&n=2,\\
-i\alpha\cdot\nabla_{x}g_{0}(0;x,y),&n\geqslant 3,\end{cases}

(10.57)

\displaystyle R_{1,0}(x-y)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-(2\pi)^{-1}\text{\rm ln}(|x-y|)I_{N},&n=2,\\
g_{0}(0;x,y)I_{N}=\frac{1}{(n-2)\omega_{n-1}}|x-y|^{2-n}I_{N},&n\geqslant 3,\end{cases}

(10.58)

\displaystyle\hskip 129.46011pt\omega_{n-1}=2\pi^{n/2}/\Gamma(n/2),

\displaystyle R_{1,1}(x-y)

\displaystyle=1,\quad n\geqslant 2.

(10.59)

Theorem 10.7
.

Assume Hypothesis
10.5
with
\varepsilon=0
in (
10.49
).

(i)
If
n=2
, there are precisely four possible cases:

Case
(I)
:
0
is regular for
H
.

Case
(II)
:
0
is a
(
possibly degenerate
3
3

3

We will recall in Lemma
10.12

(i)
that if
n=2
, the degeneracy in Case
(II)
is at most two.

)
resonance of
H
. In this case the resonance
functions
\psi
satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\psi\in[L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})]^{2},\quad q\in(2,\infty)\cup\{\infty\},\quad\nabla\psi\in[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})]^{2\times 2},\\
&\psi\notin[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})]^{2}.\end{split}

(10.60)

Case
(III)
:
0
is a
(
possibly degenerate
)
eigenvalue of
H
. In this case the corresponding
eigenfunctions
\psi\in\dom(H)=\big[W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\big]^{2}
of
H\psi=0
also satisfy

\psi\in[L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})]^{2},\quad q\in[2,\infty)\cup\{\infty\}.

(10.61)

Case
(IV)
: A possible mixture of Cases
(II)
and
(III)
.

(ii)
If
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
n\geqslant 3
, there are precisely two possible cases:

Case
(I)
:
0
is regular for
H
.

Case
(II)
:
0
is a
(
possibly degenerate
)
eigenvalue of
H
. In this case, the corresponding eigenfunctions
\psi\in\dom(H)=\big[W^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big]^{N}
of
H\psi=0
also satisfy

\psi\in\big[L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big]^{N},\quad q\in\begin{cases}(3/2,\infty)\cup\{\infty\},&n=3,\\
(4/3,4),&n=4,\\
(2n/(n+2),2n/(n-2)),&n\geqslant 5.\end{cases}

(10.62)

In particular, there are no zero-energy resonances of
H
in dimension
n\geqslant 3
.

(iii)
The point
0
is regular for
H
if and only if

\ker\big(\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(0+i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]\big)=\{0\}.

(10.63)

Proof.

Since
G_{0}(0+i0;x,y)
,
x\neq y
, exists for all
n\geqslant 2
(cf. (
5.16
)), the
Birman–Schwinger eigenvalue equation (cf. (
10.29
))

\big[I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}+\overline{V_{2}(H_{0}-(0+i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}}\big]\phi_{0}=0,\quad 0\neq\phi_{0}\in[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},

(10.64)

gives rise to a distributional zero-energy solution
\psi_{0}\in[L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
of
H\psi_{0}=0
in
terms of
\phi_{0}
of the form (for a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
n\geqslant 2
),

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(x)=-\big((H_{0}-(0+i0)I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-1}V_{1}^{*}\phi_{0}\big)(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 24.18483pt=-[R_{0,0}*(V_{1}^{*}\phi_{0})](x)

(10.65)

\displaystyle\hskip 24.18483pt=-i2^{-1}\pi^{-n/2}\Gamma(n/2)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}y\,|x-y|^{-n}[\alpha\cdot(x-y)]V_{1}(y)^{*}\phi_{0}(y),

(10.66)

\displaystyle\hskip 24.18483pt=-i2^{-1}\pi^{-n/2}\Gamma(n/2)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}y\,|x-y|^{-n}[\alpha\cdot(x-y)]V_{1}(y)^{*}V_{2}(y)\psi_{0}(y),

(10.67)

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(x)=(V_{2}\psi_{0})(x).

(10.68)

In particular, one concludes that
\psi_{0}\neq 0
. Thus, one estimates,
with
\|V_{1}(\,\cdot\,)\|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{N\times N}}\leqslant c\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-1}
and some constant
d_{n}\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\psi_{0}(x)\|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{N}}&\leqslant d_{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}y\,|x-y|^{1-n}\langle y\rangle^{-1}\|\phi_{0}(y)\|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{N}}\\
&=d_{n}{\mathcal{R}}_{1,n}\big(\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{-1}\|\phi_{0}(\,\cdot\,)\|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{N}}\big)(x),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.\end{split}

(10.69)

Invoking the Riesz potential
{\mathcal{R}}_{1,n}
(cf. Theorem
6.3
),
