\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\Big[|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}+|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{(1-n)/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 35.56593pt\vdots

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\langle x_{n-j-1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\Big[|x_{n-j-1}-x_{n-j}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}|x_{n-j-1}-x_{n-j}|^{(1-n)/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\langle x_{n-j}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\Big[1+|z|^{(n+1)/2}|x_{n-j}-x_{1}|^{(n+1)/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\widetilde{C}_{n,j,\delta}|z|^{-(n-j-1)\delta-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\cdots\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j}

(11.79)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\Big[|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}[1+|x_{1}|]^{(1-n)/2}[1+|x_{2}|]^{(1-n)/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 35.56593pt\vdots

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\langle x_{n-j-1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\Big[|x_{n-j-1}-x_{n-j}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}[1+|x_{n-j-1}|]^{(1-n)/2}[1+|x_{n-j}|]^{(1-n)/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\langle x_{n-j}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\Big[1+|z|^{(n+1)/2}[1+|x_{n-j}|]^{(n+1)/2}[1+|x_{1}|]^{(n+1)/2}\Big],

\displaystyle\hskip 286.80382ptz\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

where
C_{n,j,\delta},\widetilde{C}_{n,j,\delta}\in(0,\infty)
are suitable constants. We claim that for each bounded subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, the integrand under the iterated integral on the right-hand side in (
11.79
) is uniformly bounded with respect to
z\in\Omega
by an integrable function of the variables
x_{1},...,x_{n-j}
. Since
|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}
and
|z|^{(n+1)/2}
are locally bounded, to justify the claim, it suffices to establish convergence of the following integral:

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\cdots\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j}

(11.80)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\Big[|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}+[1+|x_{1}|]^{(1-n)/2}[1+|x_{2}|]^{(1-n)/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 35.56593pt\vdots

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\langle x_{n-j-1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\Big[|x_{n-j-1}-x_{n-j}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+[1+|x_{n-j-1}|]^{(1-n)/2}[1+|x_{n-j}|]^{(1-n)/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\langle x_{n-j}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\Big[1+[1+|x_{n-j}|]^{(n+1)/2}[1+|x_{1}|]^{(n+1)/2}\Big].

As with
Case 1
, it suffices to focus on the most singular term in (
11.80
) and thus disregard the terms originally multiplied by
|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}
or
|z|^{(n+1)/2}
in (
11.79
) (following the same line of reasoning used throughout (
11.14
)–(
11.16
)). With this simplification, the claim reduces to establishing convergence of the integral

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\cdots\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j}

(11.81)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}\times\cdots\times\langle x_{n-j-1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{n-j-1}-x_{n-j}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\langle x_{n-j}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}.

In analogy to
Case 1
, one successively estimates the integrals over the inner variables
x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-j-1}
in (
11.81
) as follows. Beginning with the integral with respect to
x_{2}
, an application of (
11.18
) with the choices

\alpha=\beta=1-\delta,\quad\gamma=n+1,

(11.82)

yields

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\,|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{2}-x_{3}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant c_{2,n}\Big[|x_{1}-x_{3}|^{2(1-\delta)-n}+1\Big].

(11.83)

The assumptions on
\alpha
and
\beta
in (
11.18
) are satisfied by the choices in (
11.82
). In fact,

\alpha=\beta=1-\delta\in(0,n]\quad\text{and}\quad(\alpha+\beta)-n=2(1-\delta)-n<n+1=\gamma.

(11.84)

Finally,
\min(n,2(1-\delta))=2(1-\delta)
and (
11.18
) results in (
11.83
). If
j=n-3
, then
x_{2}
is the only inner variable, and the integration over the inner variables is complete with (
11.83
). For
j\leqslant n-4
the process continues and there are
n-j-3
remaining inner integrals to estimate. Applying (
11.83
) in (
11.81
), the next inner integral is with respect to
x_{3}
:

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{3}\,|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{2}-x_{3}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\langle x_{3}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{3}-x_{4}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant c_{2,n,\delta}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{3}\,\Big[|x_{1}-x_{3}|^{2(1-\delta)-n}+1\Big]\langle x_{3}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{3}-x_{4}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad=c_{2,n,\delta}\bigg[\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{3}\,|x_{1}-x_{3}|^{2(1-\delta)-n}\langle x_{3}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{3}-x_{4}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{3}\,\langle x_{3}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{3}-x_{4}|^{1-\delta-n}\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad=:c_{2,n,\delta}[{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(x_{1},x_{4})+{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(x_{4})]

(11.85)

for some
c_{2,n,\delta}\in(0,\infty)
. An application of (
11.18
) with the choices

\alpha=2(1-\delta),\quad\beta=1-\delta,\quad\gamma=n+1,

(11.86)

yields

{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(x_{1},x_{4})\leqslant c_{3,n,\delta}^{\prime}\Big[|x_{1}-x_{4}|^{3(1-\delta)-n}+1\Big]

(11.87)

for some
c_{3,n,\delta}^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)
. With the choices in (
11.86
), it is clear that
\alpha,\beta\in(0,n]
and
(\alpha+\beta)-n=3(1-\delta)-n<n+1
since

\displaystyle n+1-[3(1-\delta)-n]=2(n-1)+3\delta>0,

(11.88)

so the assumptions on
\alpha
and
\beta
in (
11.18
) are satisfied. Finally,
\min(n,3(1-\delta))=3(1-\delta)
, and (
11.18
) results in (
11.87
). A second application of (
11.18
), this time with the choices

\alpha=n,\quad\beta=1-\delta,\quad\gamma=n+1

(11.89)

yields

{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(x_{4})\leqslant c_{3,n,\delta}^{\prime\prime},\quad x_{4}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(11.90)

for some
c_{3,n,\delta}^{\prime\prime}\in(0,\infty)
. As a result, (
11.85
), (
11.87
), and (
11.90
) imply

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{3}\,|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{2}-x_{3}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\langle x_{3}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{3}-x_{4}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant c_{3,n,\delta}\Big[|x_{1}-x_{4}|^{3(1-\delta)-n}+1\Big]

(11.91)

for some
c_{3,n,\delta}\in(0,\infty)
. Continuing systematically in this way, one obtains

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\cdots\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j-1}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\cdots\times|x_{n-j-2}-x_{n-j-1}|^{1-\delta-n}\langle x_{n-j-1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{n-j-1}-x_{n-j}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant c_{n-j-2,n,\delta}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j-1}\,\Big[|x_{1}-x_{n-j-1}|^{(n-j-2)(1-\delta)-n}+1\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 131.73639pt\times\langle x_{n-j-1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{n-j-1}-x_{n-j}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad=c_{n-j-2,n,\delta}\bigg[\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j-1}\,|x_{1}-x_{n-j-1}|^{(n-j-2)(1-\delta)-n}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j-1}\,\langle x_{n-j-1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{n-j-1}-x_{n-j}|^{1-\delta-n}\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad=:c_{n-j-2,n,\delta}[{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(x_{1},x_{n-j})+{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(x_{n-j})]

(11.92)

for some
c_{n-j-2,n,\delta}\in(0,\infty)
. Applying (
11.18
) with the choices

\alpha=(n-j-2)(1-\delta),\quad\beta=1-\delta,\quad\gamma=n+1,

(11.93)

yields

{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(x_{1},x_{n-j})\leqslant c_{n-j-1,n,\delta}^{\prime}\Big[|x_{1}-x_{n-j}|^{(n-j-1)(1-\delta)-n}+1\Big].

(11.94)

The assumptions on
\alpha
and
\beta
in (
11.18
) are satisfied by the choices in (
11.93
). In fact,

0<\alpha=(n-j-2)(1-\delta)\leqslant n-2\leqslant n\quad\text{and}\quad 0<\beta=1-\delta\leqslant n,

(11.95)

while

(\alpha+\beta)-n=(n-j-1)(1-\delta)-n\leqslant 0<n+1=\gamma.

(11.96)

Finally,
\min(n,(n-j-1)(1-\delta))=(n-j-1)(1-\delta)
and (
11.18
) results in (
11.94
). A second application of (
11.18
), this time with the choices

\alpha=n,\quad\beta=1-\delta,\quad\gamma=n+1,

(11.97)

yields

{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(x_{n-j})\leqslant c_{n-j-1,n,\delta}^{\prime\prime},\quad x_{n-j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(11.98)

for some
c_{n-j-1,n,\delta}^{\prime\prime}\in(0,\infty)
. Combining (
11.92
), (
11.94
), and (
11.98
), one obtains

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\cdots\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j-1}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\hskip 29.59067pt\times\cdots\times|x_{n-j-2}-x_{n-j-1}|^{1-\delta-n}\langle x_{n-j-1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{n-j-1}-x_{n-j}|^{1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant c_{n-j-1,n,\delta}\Big[|x_{1}-x_{n-j}|^{(n-j-1)(1-\delta)-n}+1\Big]

(11.99)

for some
c_{n-j-1,n,\delta}\in(0,\infty)
.

The estimate in (
11.99
) implies

(
11.81
)

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{n-j-1,n,\delta}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j}

(11.100)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\Big[|x_{1}-x_{n-j}|^{(n-j-1)(1-\delta)-n}+1\Big]\langle x_{n-j}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}

Focusing on the integral over
x_{n-j}
in (
11.100
),

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j}\,\Big[|x_{1}-x_{n-j}|^{(n-j-1)(1-\delta)-n}+1\Big]\langle x_{n-j}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j}\,|x_{1}-x_{n-j}|^{(n-j-1)(1-\delta)-n}\langle x_{n-j}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j}\,\langle x_{n-j}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\quad=:{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(x_{1})+{\mathcal{I}}_{2},

(11.101)

one infers that

{\mathcal{I}}_{2}=c_{n}<\infty.

(11.102)

An application of (
11.18
) with the choices

\alpha=(n-j-1)(1-\delta),\quad\beta=n,\quad\gamma=n+1

(11.103)

yields

{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(x_{1})\leqslant c_{n-j,n,\delta}^{\prime},\quad x_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

(11.104)

Thus, (
11.101
), (
11.102
), and (
11.104
) imply

\eqref{11.100}\leqslant c_{n-j,n,\delta},\quad x_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(11.105)

for some
c_{n-j,n,\delta}\in(0,\infty)
. As a result, (
11.100
) and (
11.105
) imply

\eqref{11.80}\leqslant c_{n-j-1,n,\delta}c_{n-j,n,\delta}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}<\infty.

(11.106)

Hence, this establishes the claim that for each bounded subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, the integrand under the iterated integral on the right-hand side in (
11.79
) is uniformly bounded with respect to
z\in\Omega
by an integrable function of the variables
x_{1},...,x_{n-j}
. As a consequence of this claim, (
11.79
) implies that for each
\delta\in(0,1)
and each bounded subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, the following estimate holds:

\eqref{11.78}\leqslant C_{n,j,\delta,\Omega}|z|^{-(n-j-1)\delta-1},\quad z\in\Omega,

(11.107)

for some
C_{n,j,\delta,\Omega}\in(0,\infty)
. In summary, for
Case 2
, one has that for any
\delta\in(0,1)
and any bounded subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\cdots\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j}\,V(x_{1})G_{0}(z;x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 44.10185pt\cdots\times V(x_{n-j-1})G_{0}(z;x_{n-j-1},x_{n-j})

\displaystyle\hskip 58.04343pt\times V(x_{n-j})\frac{1}{n!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial z^{n}}G_{0}(z;x_{n-j},x_{1})\bigg]\bigg\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant C_{n,j,\delta,\Omega}|z|^{-(n-j-1)\delta-1},\quad z\in\Omega.

(11.108)

In addition, since
|z|^{-(n-j-1)(1-\delta)-1}
is bounded in
\overline{\Omega}
if
0\notin\overline{\Omega}
, Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem implies that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\cdots\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{n-j}\,V(x_{1})G_{0}(z;x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83368pt\cdots\times V(x_{n-j-1})G_{0}(z;x_{n-j-1},x_{n-j})

(11.109)

\displaystyle\hskip 52.92213pt\times V(x_{n-j})\frac{1}{n!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial z^{n}}G_{0}(z;x_{n-j},x_{1})\bigg]

is analytic in
\Omega
and extends continuously to
\overline{\Omega}
if
0\notin\overline{\Omega}
. This settles
Case 2
.

Turning to
Case 3
, we assume that
j=n-2
. In this case,
\underline{k}=(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
with
k_{1}+k_{3}\neq n-1
. Let
\delta\in(0,1)
be fixed. Invoking the final estimate in (
11.35
), one obtains

\displaystyle\bigg\|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\,V(x_{1})\frac{1}{k_{2}!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{k_{2}}}{\partial z^{k_{2}}}G_{0}(z;x_{1},x_{2})\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\times V(x_{2})\frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{3}+1)!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{k_{1}+k_{3}+1}}{\partial z^{k_{1}+k_{3}+1}}G_{0}(z;x_{2},x_{1})\bigg]\bigg\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant C_{n,j,\delta}|z|^{-2\delta}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big\{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{k_{2}+1-\delta-n}\chi_{[0,1]}(|z||x_{1}-x_{2}|)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\quad+|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}\big[|x_{1}|^{(2k_{2}+1-n)/2}+|x_{2}|^{(2k_{2}+1-n)/2}\big]\chi_{[1,\infty)}(|z||x_{1}-x_{2}|)\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big\{|x_{2}-x_{1}|^{k_{1}+k_{3}+2-\delta-n}\chi_{[0,1]}(|z||x_{2}-x_{1}|)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\quad+|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}\big[|x_{2}|^{(2k_{1}+2k_{3}+3-n)/2}+|x_{1}|^{(2k_{1}+2k_{3}+3-n)/2}\big]

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83368pt\qquad\times\chi_{[1,\infty)}(|z||x_{2}-x_{1}|)\big\}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\widetilde{C}_{n,j,\delta}|z|^{-2\delta}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}

(11.110)

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83368pt\times\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big\{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{k_{2}+1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83368pt\quad+|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}\big[|x_{1}|^{(2k_{2}+1-n)/2}+|x_{2}|^{(2k_{2}+1-n)/2}\big]\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83368pt\times\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big\{|x_{2}-x_{1}|^{k_{1}+k_{3}+2-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83368pt\quad+|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}\big[|x_{2}|^{(2k_{1}+2k_{3}+3-n)/2}+|x_{1}|^{(2k_{1}+2k_{3}+3-n)/2}\big]\big\},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

where
C_{n,n-2,\delta},\widetilde{C}_{n,n-2,\delta}\in(0,\infty)
are suitable constants. We claim that for each bounded subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, the integrand under the iterated integral on the right-hand side in (
11.110
) is uniformly bounded with respect to
z\in\Omega
by an integrable function of the variables
x_{1},x_{2}
. Since
|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}
is locally bounded, to justify the claim, it suffices to establish convergence of the following integral:

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\,\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big\{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{k_{2}+1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\hskip 69.70915pt\qquad+\big[|x_{1}|^{(2k_{2}+1-n)/2}+|x_{2}|^{(2k_{2}+1-n)/2}\big]\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 69.70915pt\quad\times\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big\{|x_{2}-x_{1}|^{k_{1}+k_{3}+2-\delta-n}

(11.111)

\displaystyle\hskip 69.70915pt\qquad+\big[|x_{2}|^{(2k_{1}+2k_{3}+3-n)/2}+|x_{1}|^{(2k_{1}+2k_{3}+3-n)/2}\big]\big\}.

In turn, as in the argument for the proof of part
(i)
and Cases 1 and 2, it suffices to focus on the most singular term in (
11.111
) and thus disregard the terms originally multiplied by the factor
|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}
in (
11.110
) (following the same line of reasoning used throughout (
11.14
)–(
11.16
)). With this simplification, the claim reduces to establishing convergence of the integral:

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\,\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{k_{2}+1-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\hskip 81.09052pt\times\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{2}-x_{1}|^{k_{1}+k_{3}+2-\delta-n}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2(1-\delta)-n}\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}.

(11.112)

Applying (
11.18
) with
\alpha=2(1-\delta)
,
\beta=n
, and
\gamma=n+1
, one infers that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2(1-\delta)-n}\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\leqslant c_{2,n,\delta},\quad x_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(11.113)

for some
c_{2,n,\delta}\in(0,\infty)
. In turn, (
11.113
) implies

\eqref{11.111}\leqslant c_{2,n,\delta}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\,\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}<\infty.

(11.114)

Hence, this establishes the claim that for each bounded subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, the integrand under the iterated integral on the right-hand side in (
11.110
) is uniformly bounded with respect to
z\in\Omega
by an integrable function of the variables
x_{1},...,x_{2}
. As a consequence of this claim, (
11.110
) implies that for each
\delta\in(0,1)
and each bounded subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, the following estimate holds:

\eqref{11.109}\leqslant C_{n,n-2,\delta,\Omega}|z|^{-2\delta},\quad z\in\Omega,

(11.115)

for some
C_{n,n-2,\delta,\Omega}\in(0,\infty)
. In summary, for
Case 3
, one has that for any
\delta\in(0,1)
and any bounded subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\,V(x_{1})\frac{1}{k_{2}!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{k_{2}}}{\partial z^{k_{2}}}G_{0}(z;x_{1},x_{2})\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 45.52458pt\times V(x_{2})\frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{3}+1)!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{k_{1}+k_{3}+1}}{\partial z^{k_{1}+k_{3}+1}}G_{0}(z;x_{2},x_{1})\bigg]\bigg\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant C_{n,n-2,\delta,\Omega}|z|^{-2\delta},\quad z\in\Omega.

(11.116)

In addition, since
|z|^{-2\delta}
is bounded in
\overline{\Omega}
if
0\notin\overline{\Omega}
, Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem implies that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\,V(x_{1})\frac{1}{k_{2}!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{k_{2}}}{\partial z^{k_{2}}}G_{0}(z;x_{1},x_{2})\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83368pt\times V(x_{2})\frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{3}+1)!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{k_{1}+k_{3}+1}}{\partial z^{k_{1}+k_{3}+1}}G_{0}(z;x_{2},x_{1})\bigg]

(11.117)

is analytic in
\Omega
and extends continuously to
\overline{\Omega}
if
0\notin\overline{\Omega}
. This settles
Case 3
.

Turning to
Case 4
, we assume that
j=n-2
. In this case,
\underline{k}=(k_{1},0,k_{3})
with
k_{1}+k_{3}=n-1
. Let
\delta\in(0,1)
be fixed. Invoking the final estimate in (
11.35
), one obtains

\displaystyle\bigg\|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\,V(x_{1})\frac{1}{k_{2}!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{k_{2}}}{\partial z^{k_{2}}}G_{0}(z;x_{1},x_{2})\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\times V(x_{2})\frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{3}+1)!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{k_{1}+k_{3}+1}}{\partial z^{k_{1}+k_{3}+1}}G_{0}(z;x_{2},x_{1})\bigg]\bigg\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}

\displaystyle\quad=\bigg\|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\,V(x_{1})G_{0}(z;x_{1},x_{2})V(x_{2})\frac{1}{n!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial z^{n}}G_{0}(z;x_{2},x_{1})\bigg]\bigg\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant C_{n,j,\delta}|z|^{-\delta-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big\{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}\chi_{[0,1]}(|z||x_{1}-x_{2}|)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\quad+|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}\big[|x_{1}|^{(1-n)/2}+|x_{2}|^{(1-n)/2}\big]\chi_{[1,\infty)}(|z||x_{1}-x_{2}|)\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big\{\chi_{[0,1]}(|z||x_{2}-x_{1}|)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\quad+|z|^{(n+1)/2}\big[|x_{2}|^{(n+1)/2}+|x_{1}|^{(n+1)/2}\big]

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83368pt\qquad\times\chi_{[1,\infty)}(|z||x_{2}-x_{1}|)\big\}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\widetilde{C}_{n,j,\delta}|z|^{-\delta-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}

(11.118)

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83368pt\times\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big\{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}+|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}\big[|x_{1}|^{(1-n)/2}+|x_{2}|^{(1-n)/2}\big]\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83368pt\times\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big\{1+|z|^{(n+1)/2}\big[|x_{2}|^{(n+1)/2}+|x_{1}|^{(n+1)/2}\big]\big\},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

where
C_{n,n-2,\delta},\widetilde{C}_{n,n-2,\delta}\in(0,\infty)
are suitable constants. We claim that for each bounded subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, the integrand under the iterated integral on the right-hand side in (
11.118
) is uniformly bounded with respect to
z\in\Omega
by an integrable function of the variables
x_{1},x_{2}
. Since
|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}
and
|z|^{(n+1)/2}
are locally bounded, to justify the claim, it suffices to establish convergence of the following integral:

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83368pt\times\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big\{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}+|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}\big[|x_{1}|^{(1-n)/2}+|x_{2}|^{(1-n)/2}\big]\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 39.83368pt\times\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\big\{1+|z|^{(n+1)/2}\big[|x_{2}|^{(n+1)/2}+|x_{1}|^{(n+1)/2}\big]\big\}

(11.119)

In turn, as in the argument for the proof of part
(i)
and
Cases 1, 2
, and
3
, it suffices to focus on the most singular term in (
11.119
) and thus disregard the terms originally multiplied by the factor
|z|^{(n-1+2\delta)/2}
or
|z|^{(n+1)/2}
in (
11.118
) (following the same line of reasoning used throughout (
11.14
)–(
11.16
)). With this simplification, the claim reduces to establishing convergence of the integral:

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\,\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\delta-n}\langle x_{2}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}.

(11.120)

The integral in (
11.120
) is similar to the integral in (
11.112
). An argument entirely analogous to that used throughout (
11.112
)–(
11.114
) to show the integral in (
11.112
) is finite shows that the integral in (
11.120
) is finite. We omit further details at this point. In summary, for
Case 4
, one has that for any
\delta\in(0,1)
and any bounded subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\,V(x_{1})\frac{1}{k_{2}!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{k_{2}}}{\partial z^{k_{2}}}G_{0}(z;x_{1},x_{2})\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times V(x_{2})\frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{3}+1)!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{k_{1}+k_{3}+1}}{\partial z^{k_{1}+k_{3}+1}}G_{0}(z;x_{2},x_{1})\bigg]\bigg\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant C_{n,n-2,\delta,\Omega}|z|^{-\delta-1},\quad z\in\Omega,

(11.121)

for some
C_{n,n-2,\delta,\Omega}\in(0,\infty)
. In addition, since
|z|^{-\delta-1}
is bounded in
\overline{\Omega}
if
0\notin\overline{\Omega}
, Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem implies that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{2}\,V(x_{1})\frac{1}{k_{2}!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{k_{2}}}{\partial z^{k_{2}}}G_{0}(z;x_{1},x_{2})\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 79.6678pt\times V(x_{2})\frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{3}+1)!}\bigg[\frac{\partial^{k_{1}+k_{3}+1}}{\partial z^{k_{1}+k_{3}+1}}G_{0}(z;x_{2},x_{1})\bigg]

(11.122)

is analytic in
\Omega
and extends continuously to
\overline{\Omega}
if
0\notin\overline{\Omega}
. This settles
Case 4
.

In
Case 5
, we assume that
j=n-1
. In this case,
\underline{k}=(k_{1},k_{2})
with
k_{1}+k_{2}=n-1
. Invoking the final estimate in (
C.34
), one obtains

\displaystyle\bigg\|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\,V(x_{1})\bigg[\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial z^{n}}G_{0}(z;x_{1},x_{1})\bigg]\bigg\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{n}|z|^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\,\langle x_{1}\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{n}|z|^{-1},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

(11.123)

for some
C_{n},\widetilde{C}_{n}\in(0,\infty)
. In addition, since
|z|^{-1}
is bounded outside any neighborhood of
0
, Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem implies that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{n}x_{1}\,V(x_{1})\bigg[\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial z^{n}}G_{0}(z;x_{1},x_{1})\bigg]

(11.124)

is analytic in
\Omega
and extends continuously to
\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\backslash\{0\}
.

Now, looking at the bounds (
11.76
), (
11.108
), (
11.116
), (
11.121
), and (
11.123
), we identify the bound which is the most singular as
z\to 0
in
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. The bounds from (
11.76
) are (up to
z
-independent constant multiples)

|z|^{-(n-j)\delta},\quad\delta=\frac{n-j-2}{n-j-1},\,0\leqslant j\leqslant n-3.

(11.125)

The singularity in (
11.125
) is strongest when
(n-j)\delta
,
0\leqslant j\leqslant n-3
, is largest. Since the expression

(n-j)\delta=(n-j)-\frac{n-j}{n-j-1}

(11.126)

is decreasing with respect to the parameter
j
, its maximum value is attained for
j=0
:

\max_{0\leqslant j\leqslant n-3}(n-j)\delta=n-\frac{n}{n-1}.

(11.127)

Thus, the strongest singularity in (
11.125
) corresponds to
j=0
and is

|z|^{-[n-(n/(n-1))]}.

(11.128)

The bounds from (
11.108
) are (up to
z
-independent constant multiples)

|z|^{-(n-j-1)\delta-1},\quad\delta\in(0,1),\,0\leqslant j\leqslant n-3.

(11.129)

Choosing
\delta=\eta/(n-j-1)
,
\eta\in(0,1)
, the bound in (
11.129
) may be recast as

|z|^{-(1+\eta)},\quad\eta\in(0,1),\,0\leqslant j\leqslant n-3.

(11.130)

The bound from (
11.116
) is (up to a
z
-independent constant multiple)

|z|^{-2\delta},\quad\delta\in(0,1),\,j=n-2.

(11.131)

Choosing
\delta=\eta/2
,
\eta\in(0,1)
, the bound in (
11.131
) may be recast as

|z|^{-\eta},\quad\eta\in(0,1),\,j=n-2.

(11.132)

The bound from (
11.121
) is (up to a
z
-independent constant multiple)

|z|^{-(1+\delta)},\quad\delta\in(0,1),\,j=n-2.

(11.133)

The bound from (
11.123
) is (up to a
z
-independent constant multiple)

|z|^{-1},\quad j=n-1.

(11.134)

If
n\geqslant 4
, then the strongest singularity from (
11.125
), (
11.130
), (
11.132
), (
11.133
), and (
11.134
) is given by (
11.128
). Therefore, combining the results of
Case 1–Case 5
above with (
11.5
) and (
11.6
), one concludes that
\frac{d^{n}}{dz^{n}}G_{H,H_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)
is analytic in
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, continuous in
\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\backslash\{0\}
and

\bigg\|\frac{d^{n}}{dz^{n}}G_{H,H_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)\bigg\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}z\to 0,\\
z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\backslash\{0\}\end{subarray}}{=}O\big(|z|^{-[n-(n/(n-1))]}\big).

(11.135)

If
n=2
, then the strongest singularity in (
11.132
) and (
11.134
) is
|z|^{-(1+\delta)}
. Therefore, combining the results of
Case 4
and
Case 5
above with (
11.5
) and (
11.6
), one concludes that
\frac{d^{2}}{dz^{2}}G_{H,H_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)
is analytic in
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, continuous in
\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\backslash\{0\}
and for any
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\bigg\|\frac{d^{2}}{dz^{2}}G_{H,H_{0}}(\,\cdot\,)\bigg\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{2})}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}z\to 0,\\
z\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\backslash\{0\}\end{subarray}}{=}O\big(|z|^{-(1+\delta)}\big).

(11.136)

∎

12.
Analysis of
\xi(\,\cdot\,;H,H_{0})
and an Application to the Witten Index for a Class of
Non-Fredholm operators

Combining Hypotheses
10.16
and
11.1
we next make the following assumptions to describe continuity properties of the spectral shift function for the pair
(H,H_{0})
.

Hypothesis 12.1
.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and suppose that
V=\{V_{\ell,m}\}_{1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N}
satisfies for some constants

C\in(0,\infty)
and
\varepsilon>0
,

V\in[L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N\times N},\quad|V_{\ell,m}(x)|\leqslant C\langle x\rangle^{-n-1-\varepsilon}\,\text{ for a.e.~$x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}$, $1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N$.}

(12.1)

In addition, assume that
V(x)=\{V_{\ell,m}(x)\}_{1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N}
is self-adjoint for a.e.
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
In accordance with the factorization based on the polar decomposition of
V
discussed in (
10.32
) we
suppose that
V=V_{1}^{*}V_{2}=|V|^{1/2}U_{V}|V|^{1/2}
, where
V_{1}=V_{1}^{*}=|V|^{1/2}
,
V_{2}=U_{V}|V|^{1/2}
.

Finally, we assume that
V
satisfies (
4.4
) and (
4.5
)
5
5

5

The first condition in (
4.5
) is superseded by assumption (
12.1
).
.

Thus, combining Theorems
9.9
,
10.17
, and
11.2
yields the principal result of this
manuscript:

Theorem 12.2
.

Assume Hypothesis
12.1
. Then

\xi(\,\cdot\,;H,H_{0})\in C((-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)),

(12.2)

and the left and right limits at zero,

\xi(0_{\pm};H,H_{0})=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\xi(\pm\varepsilon;H,H_{0}),

(12.3)

exist. In particular, if
0
is a regular point for
H
according to Definition
10.6

(iii)
and Theorem

10.7

(iii)
, then

\xi(\,\cdot\,;H,H_{0})\in C({\mathbb{R}}).

(12.4)

In the remainder of this section we describe an application to the Witten index for a class of non-Fredholm operators applicable in the context of multi-dimensional, massless Dirac operators
H
. We develop some necessary preparations and the basic setup next.

We begin by isolating a bit of notation: Linear operators in the Hilbert space
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};dt;{\mathcal{H}})
, in short,
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}})
, will be denoted by calligraphic boldface symbols of the type
{\bm{T}}
, to distinguish them from operators
T
in
{\mathcal{H}}
. In particular, operators denoted by

{\bm{T}}
in the Hilbert space
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}})
represent operators associated with a
family of operators
\{T(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
in
{\mathcal{H}}
, defined by

\displaystyle({\bm{T}}f)(t)=T(t)f(t)\,\text{ for a.e.\ $t\in{\mathbb{R}}$,}

\displaystyle f\in\dom({\bm{T}})=\bigg\{g\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}})\,\bigg|\,g(t)\in\dom(T(t))\text{ for a.e.\ }t\in{\mathbb{R}};

(12.5)

\displaystyle\quad t\mapsto T(t)g(t)\text{ is (weakly) measurable;}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}dt\,\|T(t)g(t)\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}<\infty\bigg\}.

In the special case, where
\{T(t)\}
is a family of bounded operators on
{\mathcal{H}}
with

\sup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\|T(t)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}<\infty
, the associated operator
{\bm{T}}
is a bounded operator on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}})
with
\|{\bm{T}}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}}))}=\sup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\|T(t)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}
.

For brevity we will abbreviate
{\bm{I}}:=I_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{H}})}
in the following and note that in the concrete situation of
n
-dimensional, massless Dirac operators at hand,
{\mathcal{H}}=[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
.

Denoting

A_{-}=H_{0},\quad B_{+}=V,\quad A_{+}=A_{-}+B_{+}=H,

(12.6)

we introduce two families of operators in

[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}
by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&B(t)=b(t)B_{+},\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}},\\
&b^{(k)}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}};dt),\;k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0},\quad b^{\prime}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};dt),\\
&\lim_{t\to\infty}b(t)=1,\quad\lim_{t\to-\infty}b(t)=0,\\
&A(t)=A_{-}+B(t),\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.\end{split}

(12.7)

Next, following the general setups described in
[
38
]
,
[
41
]
–
[
44
]
,

[
77
]
,
[
136
]
we recall the definitions of

{\bm{A}}
,
{\bm{B}},{\bm{A}}^{\prime}={\bm{B}}^{\prime}
, given in terms of the families
A(t)
,
B(t)
, and
B^{\prime}(t)
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, as in (
12.5
). In addition,
{\bm{A}}_{-}
in
L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
represents
the self-adjoint (constant fiber) operator defined by

\displaystyle({\bm{A}}_{-}f)(t)=A_{-}f(t)\,\text{ for a.e.\ $t\in{\mathbb{R}}$,}

\displaystyle f\in\dom({\bm{A}}_{-})=\bigg\{g\in L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\,\bigg|\,g(t)\in\dom(A_{-})\text{ for a.e.\ }t\in{\mathbb{R}},

\displaystyle\quad t\mapsto A_{-}g(t)\text{ is (weakly) measurable,}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}dt\,\|A_{-}g(t)\|_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}^{2}<\infty\bigg\}.

(12.8)

Next, we introduce the operator
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
in
L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
by

{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}=\frac{d}{dt}+{\bm{A}},\quad\dom({\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}})=W^{1,2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\cap\dom({\bm{A}}_{-}),

(12.9)

where

{\bm{A}}={\bm{A}}_{-}+{\bm{B}},\quad\dom({\bm{A}})=\dom({\bm{A}}_{-}),

(12.10)

and

\|{\bm{B}}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}))}=\sup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\|B(t)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}<\infty.

(12.11)

Here the operator
d/dt
in
L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
is defined by

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{d}{dt}f\bigg)(t)=f^{\prime}(t)\,\text{ for a.e.\ $t\in{\mathbb{R}}$,}

\displaystyle\,f\in\dom(d/dt)=\big\{g\in L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\,\big|\,g\in AC_{\operatorname{loc}}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),

\displaystyle\hskip 200.59201ptg^{\prime}\in L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 65.44142pt=W^{1,2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big).

(12.12)

By
[
77
, Lemma 4.4]
(which extends to the present setting),

{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
is densely defined and closed in
L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
and the adjoint
operator
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}^{*}
of
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
is given by

{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}^{*}=-\frac{d}{dt}+{\bm{A}},\quad\dom({\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}^{*})=W^{1,2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\cap\dom({\bm{A}}_{-}).

(12.13)

This enables one to introduce the nonnegative, self-adjoint operators

{\bm{H}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, in
L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
by

{\bm{H}}_{1}={\bm{D}}_{{\bm{A}}}^{*}{\bm{D}}_{{\bm{A}}},\quad{\bm{H}}_{2}={\bm{D}}_{{\bm{A}}}{\bm{D}}_{{\bm{A}}}^{*}.

(12.14)

In order to effectively describe the domains of
{\bm{H}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, we will
decompose the latter as discussed below: To this end, one first observes that

\|{\bm{B}}^{\prime}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}))}=\sup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\|B^{\prime}(t)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}<\infty.

(12.15)

It is convenient to also introduce the operator
{\bm{H}}_{0}
in
L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
by

{\bm{H}}_{0}=-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+{\bm{A}}_{-}^{2},\quad\dom({\bm{H}}_{0})=W^{2,2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\cap\dom\big({\bm{A}}_{-}^{2}\big).

(12.16)

Then
{\bm{H}}_{0}
is self-adjoint by Theorem VIII.33 of
[
138
]
. Moreover, since
the operator
{\bm{B}}{\bm{A}}_{-}+{\bm{A}}_{-}{\bm{B}}
is
{\bm{H}}_{0}
-bounded with bound less than one,

[
107
, Theorem VI.4.3]
implies the following decomposition of the operators
{\bm{H}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
,

\displaystyle{\bm{H}}_{j}=-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+{\bm{A}}^{2}+(-1)^{j}{\bm{A}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 15.649pt={\bm{H}}_{0}+{\bm{B}}{\bm{A}}_{-}+{\bm{A}}_{-}{\bm{B}}+{\bm{B}}^{2}+(-1)^{j}{\bm{B}}^{\prime},

(12.17)

\displaystyle\,\dom({\bm{H}}_{j})=\dom({\bm{H}}_{0}),\quad j=1,2.

Next, we introduce an approximation procedure as follows: Consider the characteristic function for the interval
[-\ell,\ell]\subset{\mathbb{R}}
,

\chi_{\ell}(\nu)=\chi_{[-\ell,\ell]}(\nu),\quad\nu\in{\mathbb{R}},\;\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(12.18)

and hence

\slim_{\ell\to\infty}\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})=I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}.

(12.19)

Introducing

\displaystyle\begin{split}&A_{\ell}(t)=A_{-}+\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B(t)\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})=A_{-}+B_{\ell}(t),\\
&\dom(A_{\ell}(t))=\dom(A_{-}),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},\end{split}

(12.20)

\displaystyle A_{+,\ell}=A_{-}+\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B_{+}\chi_{\ell}(A_{-}),\quad\dom(A_{+,\ell})=\dom(A_{-}),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(12.21)

where

B_{\ell}(t)=\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B(t)\chi_{\ell}(A_{-}),\quad\dom(B_{\ell}(t))=[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N},\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},

(12.22)

one concludes that

\displaystyle A_{+,\ell}-A_{-}=\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B_{+}\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(12.23)

\displaystyle A_{\ell}^{\prime}(t)=B_{\ell}^{\prime}(t)=\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B^{\prime}(t)\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(12.24)

As a consequence of (
12.23
), which follows from

\displaystyle\|\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B_{+}\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\big\|\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B_{+}(A_{-}-iI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-n-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\big\|(A_{-}-iI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{n+1}\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}<\infty

(12.25)

(cf. (
7.4
)), the spectral shift functions

\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, exist and are uniquely determined by

\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};d\nu),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(12.26)

implying

\tr_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}(f(A_{+,\ell})-f(A_{-}))=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\nu\,\xi(\nu;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})f^{\prime}(\nu),\quad f\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}).

(12.27)

We also note the analogous decompositions,

\displaystyle{\bm{H}}_{j,\ell}=-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+{\bm{A}}_{\ell}^{2}+(-1)^{j}{\bm{A}}_{\ell}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 19.06332pt={\bm{H}}_{0}+{\bm{B}}_{\ell}{\bm{A}}_{-}+{\bm{A}}_{-}{\bm{B}}_{\ell}+{\bm{B}}_{\ell}^{2}+(-1)^{j}{\bm{B}}_{\ell}^{\prime},

(12.28)

\displaystyle\,\dom({\bm{H}}_{j,\ell})=\dom({\bm{H}}_{0})=W^{2,2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;j=1,2,

with

{\bm{B}}_{\ell}=\chi_{\ell}({\bm{A}}_{-}){\bm{B}}\chi_{\ell}({\bm{A}}_{-}),\quad{\bm{B}}_{\ell}^{\prime}=\chi_{\ell}({\bm{A}}_{-}){\bm{B}}^{\prime}\chi_{\ell}({\bm{A}}_{-}),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(12.29)

implying

\displaystyle{\bm{H}}_{2}-{\bm{H}}_{1}=2{\bm{B}}^{\prime},

(12.30)

\displaystyle{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell}-{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell}=2{\bm{B}}_{\ell}^{\prime}=2\chi_{\ell}({\bm{A}}_{-}){\bm{B}}^{\prime}\chi_{\ell}({\bm{A}}_{-}),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(12.31)

Next, we recall the fact that for
\varepsilon>0
,

L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{n};(1+|x|)^{(n/2)+\varepsilon}d^{n}x\big)\subset\ell^{1}(L^{2})({\mathbb{R}}^{n})

(12.32)

(see, e.g.,
[
158
, p. 38]
for the definition of the Birman–Solomyak space
\ell^{1}(L^{2})({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
) and, given
\alpha>n
,

(1+|\,\cdot\,|)^{-\alpha}\in L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{n};(1+|x|)^{(n/2)+\varepsilon}d^{n}x\big)

(12.33)

for
0<\varepsilon
sufficiently small (depending on
a
). This is of relevance here so that

[
44
, Subsection 7.2.1]
becomes applicable in our context.

We continue with the following basic result in
[
44
, Theorems 3.6, 7.1]
:

Theorem 12.3
.

In addition to Hypothesis
12.1
suppose that

V_{\ell,m}\in W^{4n,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}),\quad 1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N.

(12.34)

Then, abbreviating

q=\lceil n/2\rceil=\begin{cases}(n+1)/2,&$n$\text{ odd},\\
n/2,&$n$\text{ even},\end{cases}

(12.35)

one obtains

\displaystyle\begin{split}&[({\bm{H}}_{2}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}-({\bm{H}}_{1}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}\big],\,\big[({\bm{H}}_{2,\ell}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}-({\bm{H}}_{1,\ell}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}\big]\\
&\quad\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big(L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\big),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;r\in{\mathbb{N}},\;r\geqslant q,\;z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash[0,\infty),\end{split}

(12.36)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\big\|\big[({\bm{H}}_{2,\ell}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}-({\bm{H}}_{1,\ell}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}\big]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-[({\bm{H}}_{2}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}-({\bm{H}}_{1}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-q}\big]\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}))}=0,

(12.37)

\displaystyle\hskip 234.73523ptz\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash[0,\infty).

For the fact that
q=\lceil n/2\rceil
in (
12.36
) can be replaced by any
r\geqslant q
,
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
, see, for instance,
[
183
, p. 210]
; similarly, (
12.37
) extends to
r\geqslant q
,
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
, by
[
39
]
.

Relations (
12.36
) together with the fact that
{\bm{H}}_{j}\geqslant 0
,
{\bm{H}}_{j,\ell}\geqslant 0
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
j=1,2
, implies the existence and uniqueness of spectral shift functions

\xi(\,\cdot\,;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})
and

\xi(\,\cdot\,;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})
for the pair of operators
({\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})
and

({\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, respectively, employing the normalization

\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})=0,\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})=0,\quad\lambda<0,\;\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}

(12.38)

(cf.
[
183
, Sect. 8.9]
). Moreover,

\xi(\,\cdot\,;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})\in L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\lambda|)^{-q-1}d\lambda\big).

(12.39)

Since in analogy to (
12.25
),

\displaystyle\|A_{\ell}^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}=\|B_{\ell}^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}

\displaystyle\quad=\|\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,)\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\big\|\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})B_{+}(A_{-}-iI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-n-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}

(12.40)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\big\|(A_{-}-iI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{n+1}\chi_{\ell}(A_{-})\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}\,b^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,)\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};dt),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

employing
b^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,)\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};dt)
(cf. (
12.7
)), one obtains

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}dt\,\|A_{\ell}^{\prime}(t)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}<\infty,\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(12.41)

Given (
12.41
), the results in
[
136
]
(see also
[
77
]
)
actually imply that

[({\bm{H}}_{2,\ell}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-1}-({\bm{H}}_{1,\ell}-z\,{\bm{I}})^{-1}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big(L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)\big),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(12.42)

and

\xi(\,\cdot\,;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})\in L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\lambda|)^{-2}d\lambda\big),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(12.43)

In particular,

\displaystyle{\tr}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}(f({\bm{H}}_{2})-f({\bm{H}}_{1}))=\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})f^{\prime}(\lambda),

\displaystyle{\tr}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}(f({\bm{H}}_{2,\ell})-f({\bm{H}}_{1,\ell}))=\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})f^{\prime}(\lambda),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

\displaystyle\hskip 250.38434ptf\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}).

(12.44)

In addition, as derived in
[
136
]
(see also,
[
77
]
), (
12.26
), (
12.38
), and
(
12.41
) imply the approximate trace formula,

\int_{[0,\infty)}\frac{\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})\,d\lambda}{(\lambda-z)^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{\xi(\nu;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})\,d\nu}{(\nu^{2}-z)^{3/2}},\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash[0,\infty),

(12.45)

which in turn implies Pushnitski’s formula
[
136
]
,

\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\lambda^{1/2}}^{\lambda^{1/2}}\frac{\xi(\nu;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})\,d\nu}{(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{1/2}},&\text{for a.e.~$\lambda>0$,}\\
0,&\lambda<0,\end{cases}\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(12.46)

via a Stieltjes inversion argument (cf.
[
77
, Sect. 8]
).

As shown in
[
43
]
, (
12.37
) implies for
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\|[f({\bm{H}}_{2,\ell})-f({\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})]-[f({\bm{H}}_{2})-f({\bm{H}}_{1})]\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}))}=0,\end{split}

(12.47)

and hence

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})f^{\prime}(\lambda)=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}{\tr}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}(f({\bm{H}}_{2,\ell})-f({\bm{H}}_{1,\ell}))

\displaystyle\quad={\tr}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}};[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}(f({\bm{H}}_{2})-f({\bm{H}}_{1}))=\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})f^{\prime}(\lambda).

(12.48)

Abbreviating

q_{0}=2\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1=\begin{cases}n,&$n$\text{ odd},\\
n+1,&$n$\text{ even},\end{cases}

(12.49)

and assuming Hypothesis
7.1
, one recalls that Theorem
7.4
implies

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big[(A_{+}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-r_{0}}-(A_{-}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-r_{0}}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),&\\
r_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}},\;r_{0}\geqslant q_{0},\;z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}.&\end{split}

(12.50)

Since
q_{0}
is always odd,
[
184
, Theorem 2.2]
yields the existence of a spectral shift function

\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})
for the pair
(A_{+},A_{-})
satisfying

\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})\in L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\nu|)^{-q_{0}-1}d\nu\big)

(12.51)

and hence

\tr_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}}(f(A_{+})-f(A_{-}))=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\nu\,\xi(\nu;A_{+},A_{-})f^{\prime}(\nu),\quad f\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}).

(12.52)

While
\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})
in (
12.51
), (
12.52
) is not unique, we will select a unique candidate using Theorem
12.4
below.

The next result is essentially
[
39
, Theorem 4.7]
; due to its importance we reproduce the proof here. To prepare the stage, we temporarily go beyond the approximation
A_{+,\ell}
of
A_{+}
and now introduce the following path of self-adjoint operators
\{A_{+}(s)\}_{s\in[0,1]}
, in

\big[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big]^{N}
, where

\displaystyle A_{+}(s)=A_{-}+P_{s}B_{+}P_{s},\quad\dom(A_{+}(s))=\dom(A_{-}),\quad s\in[0,1],

(12.53)

\displaystyle P_{s}=\chi_{[-(1-s)^{-1},(1-s)^{-1}]}(A_{-}),\;s\in[0,1),\quad P_{1}=I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}},

(12.54)

in particular,

A_{+}(0)=A_{+,1}\text{ (cf.\ \eqref{12.23} with $\ell=1$) and }\,A_{+}(1)=A_{+}.

(12.55)

Theorem 12.4
.

Assume Hypothesis
12.1
and suppose that

V_{\ell,m}\in W^{4n,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}),\quad 1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N.

(12.56)

Then there exists a unique spectral shift function
\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})
such that

\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})=\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+}(1),A_{-})=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})\,\text{ in $L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\nu|)^{-q_{0}-1}d\nu\big)$.}

(12.57)

Moreover, assume that
g\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}};d\nu)
. Then

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\|\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})g-\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})g\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\nu|)^{-q_{0}-1}d\nu)}=0,

(12.58)

and hence,

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\nu\,\xi(\nu;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})h(\nu)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\nu\,\xi(\nu;A_{+},A_{-})h(\nu)

(12.59)

for all
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}};d\nu)
such that
\esssup_{\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}}|h(\nu)|(1+|\nu|)^{q_{0}+1}<\infty
.

Proof.

Since by (
12.23
),
\chi_{\ell}(A-)B_{+}\chi_{\ell}(A-)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big)
, also

A_{+}(s)-A_{-}=P_{s}B_{+}P_{s}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big),\quad s\in[0,1),

(12.60)

and hence there exists a uniques spectral shift function
\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+}(s),A_{-})
for the pair

(A_{+}(s),A_{-})
satisfying

\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+}(s),A_{-})\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};d\nu).

(12.61)

Moreover, in complete analogy to (
12.50
), the family

A_{+}(s)
depends continuously on
s\in[0,1]
with respect to the pseudometric

d_{q_{0},z}(A,A^{\prime})=\big\|(A-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-q_{0}}-(A^{\prime}-zI_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-q_{0}}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N})}

(12.62)

for
A,A^{\prime}
in the set of self-adjoint operators which satisfy
for all
\zeta\in i{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big[(A-\zeta I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-q_{0}}-(A_{-}-\zeta I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-q_{0}}\big],\\
&\big[(A^{\prime}-\zeta I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-q_{0}}-(A_{-}-\zeta I_{[L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}})^{-q_{0}}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\big([L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})]^{N}\big).\end{split}

(12.63)

Thus, the hypotheses of
[
39
, Theorem 4.7]
are satisfied and one concludes the existence of a unique spectral shift function
\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+}(s),A_{-})
for the pair
(A_{+}(s),A_{-})
depending continuously on

s\in[0,1]
in the space
L^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\nu|)^{-q_{0}-1}d\nu\big)
, satisfying

\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+}(0),A_{-})=\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+,1},A_{-})
. Taking
s=(\ell-1)/\ell
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})&=\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+}(1),A_{-})=\lim_{s\uparrow 1}\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+}(s),A_{-})\\
&=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})\,\text{ in $L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\nu|)^{-q_{0}-1}d\nu\big)$.}\end{split}

(12.64)

Hence an appropriate subsequence, again denoted by
\{\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})\}_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, converges
pointwise a.e. to
\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})
as
\ell\to\infty
. Since each

\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};d\nu)
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, is uniquely defined one obtains a unique
spectral shift function satisfying (
12.62
).

The facts (
12.58
) and (
12.59
) are now evident.
∎

In the following we will always employ
\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})
as determined by the limiting relation (
12.57
) as the spectral shift function for the pair
(A_{+},A_{-})
.

The next result is fundamental, it establishes (
12.46
) in the limit
\ell\to\infty
.

Theorem 12.5
.

Assume Hypothesis
12.1
and suppose that

V_{\ell,m}\in W^{4n,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}),\quad 1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N.

(12.65)

Then,

\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\lambda^{1/2}}^{\lambda^{1/2}}\frac{\xi(\nu;A_{+},A_{-})\,d\nu}{(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{1/2}}\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda>0$.}

(12.66)

Proof.

We start by multiplying the approximate relation (
12.46
) by the derivative
f^{\prime}
of a test function

f\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
, and integrate to get,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})f^{\prime}(\lambda)=\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})f^{\prime}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,f^{\prime}(\lambda)\int_{-\lambda^{1/2}}^{\lambda^{1/2}}\frac{\xi(\nu;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})\,d\nu}{(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\nu\,\xi(\nu;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})F^{\prime}(\nu),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(12.67)

where
F^{\prime}
is defined by

F^{\prime}(\nu)=\int_{\nu^{2}}^{\infty}d\lambda\,f^{\prime}(\lambda)(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{-1/2},\quad\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(12.68)

We claim that

F^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}),

(12.69)

rendering the manipulations leading to (
12.67
) well-defined. Clearly,
F^{\prime}\in C_{0}({\mathbb{R}})

since
f^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}})
. To show that
F^{\prime}\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
, it suffices to repeatedly integrate by parts and allude to the following representations of
F^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{\nu^{2}}^{\infty}d\lambda\,f^{\prime}(\lambda)(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\nu^{2}}^{\infty}d\lambda\,f^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{1/2}=2\frac{2}{3}\int_{\nu^{2}}^{\infty}d\lambda\,f^{\prime\prime\prime}(\lambda)(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{3/2}

\displaystyle\;\,\,\vdots

\displaystyle=c_{k}\int_{\nu^{2}}^{\infty}d\lambda\,f^{(k)}(\lambda)(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{k-(3/2)}\quad\nu\in{\mathbb{R}},\;k\in{\mathbb{N}},

(12.70)

for appropriate constants
c_{k}
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Thus, (
12.67
) yields the
following,

\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})f^{\prime}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\nu\,\xi(\nu;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})F^{\prime}(\nu),\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},

(12.71)

where
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
was arbitrary, and
F^{\prime}\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})

(depending on
f^{\prime}
) is given by (
12.68
) or equivalently, by any of the expressions in (
12.70
).

It remains to control the limits
\ell\to\infty
on either side of (
12.71
): By (
12.48
),
the left-hand side of (
12.71
) converges as
\ell\to\infty
,

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2,\ell},{\bm{H}}_{1,\ell})f^{\prime}(\lambda)=\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})f^{\prime}(\lambda).

(12.72)

For the right-hand side of (
12.71
) one applies Theorem
12.4
, especially, (
12.59
),
and concludes that

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\nu\,\xi(\nu;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})F^{\prime}(\nu)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\nu\,\xi(\nu;A_{+},A_{-})F^{\prime}(\nu),

(12.73)

since by (
12.62
)

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\|\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+,\ell},A_{-})-\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}};(1+|\nu|)^{-q_{0}-1}d\nu)}=0.

(12.74)

Combining (
12.71
)–(
12.73
) finally yields

\displaystyle\int_{[0,\infty)}d\lambda\,\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})f^{\prime}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\nu\,\xi(\nu;A_{+},A_{-})F^{\prime}(\nu)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}d\lambda\,f^{\prime}(\lambda)\int_{-\lambda^{1/2}}^{\lambda^{1/2}}\frac{\xi(\nu;A_{+},A_{-})\,d\nu}{(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{1/2}}\chi_{[0,\infty)}(\lambda),\quad f\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}).

(12.75)

An application of the Du Bois–Raymond Lemma (see, e.g.,
[
113
, Theorem 6.11]
), thus
implies for some constant
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\xi(\lambda;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\lambda^{1/2}}^{\lambda^{1/2}}\frac{\xi(\nu;A_{+},A_{-})\,d\nu}{(\lambda-\nu^{2})^{1/2}}\chi_{[0,\infty)}(\lambda)+c\,\text{ for a.e.~$\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}$.}

(12.76)

Due to our normalization (
12.38
),
c=0
, proving (
12.66
).
∎

Having established (
12.66
), we turn to the resolvent regularized Witten index of the densely defined and closed operator
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
. We refer to
[
31
]
,
[
38
]
,

[
41
]
–
[
44
]
,
[
77
]
,
[
80
]
,
[
136
]
and the references therein for a bit of history on this subject.

Since
\sigma(A_{\pm})={\mathbb{R}}
, in particular,

0\notin\rho(A_{+})\cap\rho(A_{-})
,

{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
is a non-Fredholm operator.

(12.77)

This follows from the criterion for Fredholm operators established in
[
43
, Theorem 2.6]
(which extends to the current setting by replacing the resolvent of
A_{\pm}
by appropriate powers of the resolvent in the proof).

In the following we will show that even though
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
is a non-Fredholm operator, its Witten
index is well-defined and expressible in terms of the spectral shift functions for the pair of operators

({\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})
and
(A_{+},A_{-})
.

To introduce an appropriately (resolvent regularized) Witten index of
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
, we consider a densely defined, closed operator
T
in the complex, separable Hilbert space
{\mathcal{K}}
and assume that for some

k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and all
\lambda<0

\big[(T^{*}T-\lambda I_{{\mathcal{K}}})^{-k}-(TT^{*}-\lambda I_{{\mathcal{K}}})^{-k}\big]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}({\mathcal{K}}).

(12.78)

Then the
k
th resolvent regularized Witten index of
T
is defined by

W_{k,r}(T)=\lim_{\lambda\uparrow 0}(-\lambda)^{k}\tr_{{\mathcal{K}}}\big((T^{*}T-\lambda I_{{\mathcal{K}}})^{-k}-(TT^{*}-\lambda I_{{\mathcal{K}}})^{-k}\big),

(12.79)

whenever the limit exists. The case
k=1
as well as the approach where resolvents are replaced by semigroups has been studied in great detail in
[
43
]
, the extension to
k\geqslant 2
was discussed in

[
44
]
.

It is well-known that the (regularized) Witten index is generally not an integer, in fact, it can take on any real value (cf.
[
31
]
,
[
80
]
). The intrinsic value of
W_{k,r}(T)

lies in its stability properties with respect to additive perturbations,
analogous to stability properties of the Fredholm index. Indeed, as long as one replaces the
familiar relative compactness assumption on an additive perturbation in connection with the
Fredholm index, by appropriate relative trace class conditions in connection with
the resolvent regularized Witten index, stability of the Witten index was proved in
[
31
]
(for
k=1
, see also
[
45
]
) and, in connection with the analogous semigroup regularized Witten index, in
[
80
]
(the semigroup approach then yielding stability for
W_{k,r}(\,\cdot\,)
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
).

The following result, the first of this kind applicable to non-Fredholm operators in a
partial differential operator setting involving multi-dimensional massless Dirac operators, then characterizes the Witten index of
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
in terms of spectral shift functions:

Theorem 12.6
.

Assume Hypothesis
12.1
and suppose that

V_{\ell,m}\in W^{4n,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}),\quad 1\leqslant\ell,m\leqslant N.

(12.80)

Then
0
is a right Lebesgue point of
\xi(\,\cdot\,;{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})
, denoted by

\xi_{L}(0_{+};{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})
, and

\xi_{L}(0_{+};{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})=[\xi(0_{+};A_{+},A_{-})+\xi(0_{-};A_{+},A_{-})]/2.

(12.81)

In addition, the resolvent regularized Witten index
W_{k,r}({\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}})
of
{\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}}
exists for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
k\geqslant q
and
equals

\displaystyle\begin{split}W_{k,r}({\bm{D}}_{\bm{A}})=\xi_{L}(0_{+};{\bm{H}}_{2},{\bm{H}}_{1})&=[\xi(0_{+};A_{+},A_{-})+\xi(0_{-};A_{+},A_{-})]/2\\
&=[\xi(0_{+};H,H_{0})+\xi(0_{-};H,H_{0})]/2.\end{split}

(12.82)

Proof.

The key new input for the proof is the existence of
0
as a left and right Lebesgue point of

\xi(\,\cdot\,;A_{+},A_{-})=\xi(\,\cdot\,;H,H_{0})
. This is established in Theorem
12.2
, in fact, more is proved since left and right limits of
\xi(\,\cdot\,;H,H_{0})
at
0
are shown to exist. For
q=1
, the remaining assertions are proved in
[
43
, Theorem 4.3]
, the extension to

q\geqslant 2
is discussed in
[
44
, Sects. 5.2, 7.1]
.
∎

The actual computation of the right-hand side of (
12.82
) in terms of the potential
V
is left for a future investigation.

Appendix A
Some Remarks on Block Matrix Operators

In this appendix we collect some useful (and well-known) material on linear operators in connection with pointwise domination, boundedness, compactness, and the Hilbert-Schmidt property.

Definition A.1
.

Let
(M;{\mathcal{M}};\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite, separable measure space,
\mu
a nonnegative, measure with

0<\mu(M)\leqslant\infty
, and consider the linear operators
A,B\in{\mathcal{B}}\big(L^{2}(M;d\mu)\big)
. Then
B
pointwise dominates
A

\text{if for all $f\in L^{2}(M;d\mu)$, $|(Af)(\,\cdot\,)|\leqslant(B|f|)(\,\cdot\,)$ $\mu$-a.e.~on $M$}.

(A.1)

For a linear block operator matrix
T=\{T_{j,k}\}_{1\leqslant j,k\leqslant N}
,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, in the Hilbert space

[L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}
(where
[L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}=L^{2}(M;d\mu;{\mathbb{C}}^{N})
), we recall that

T\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}\big([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}\big)
if and only if
T_{j,k}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}\big(L^{2}(M;d\mu)\big)
,

1\leqslant j,k\leqslant N
. Moreover, we recall that (cf. e.g.,
[
27
, Theorem 11.3.6]
)

\displaystyle\|T\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L^{2}(M;d\mu)^{N})}=\int_{M\times M}d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y)\,\|T(x,y)\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{M\times M}d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y)\,\sum_{j,k=1}^{N}|T_{j,k}(x,y)|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j,k=1}^{N}\int_{M\times M}d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y)\,|T_{j,k}(x,y)|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j,k=1}^{N}\|T_{j,k}\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L^{2}(M;d\mu))},

(A.2)

where, in obvious notation,
T(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
denotes the
N\times N
matrix-valued integral kernel of
T
in
[L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}
, and
T_{j,k}(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
represents the integral kernel of
T_{j,k}
in
L^{2}(M;d\mu)
, ,
1\leqslant j,k\leqslant N
.

In addition, employing the fact that for any
N\times N
matrix
D\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N\times N}
,

\|D\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}\leqslant\|D\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}\leqslant N^{1/2}\|D\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})},

(A.3)

one also obtains

\|T\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L^{2}(M;d\mu)^{N})}\leqslant N\int_{M\times M}d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y)\,\|T(x,y)\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{C}}^{N})}.

(A.4)

More generally, for
{\mathcal{H}}
a complex separable Hilbert space and
T=\{T_{j,k}\}_{1\leqslant j,k\leqslant N}
,

N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, a block operator matrix in
{\mathcal{H}}^{N}
, one confirms that

T\in{\mathcal{B}}\big({\mathcal{H}}^{N})

(
resp.,
T\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}\big({\mathcal{H}}^{N}\big)
,
p\in[1,\infty)\cup\{\infty\}
)

if and only if

(A.5)

\displaystyle\text{for each $1\leqslant j,k\leqslant N$, $T_{j,k}\in{\mathcal{B}}\big({\mathcal{H}}^{N})$ \big(resp., $T_{j,k}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{p}\big({\mathcal{H}}^{N}\big)$, $p\in[1,\infty)\cup\{\infty\}$\big)}.

In other words, for membership of
T
in
{\mathcal{B}}\big({\mathcal{H}}^{N})
or
{\mathcal{B}}_{p}\big({\mathcal{H}}^{N}\big)
,

p\in[1,\infty)\cup\{\infty\}
, it suffices to focus on each of its matrix elements

T_{j,k}
,
1\leqslant j,k\leqslant N
. (For necessity of the last line in (
A.5
) it suffices to multiply

T
from the left and right by
N\times N
diagonal matrices with
I_{{\mathcal{H}}}
on the
j
th and
k
th position, resp., to isolate
T_{j,k}
and appeal to the ideal property. For sufficiency, it suffices to
write
T
as a sum of
N^{2}
terms with
T_{j,k}
at the
j,k
th position and zeros otherwise.)

The next result is useful in connection with Sections
5
and
6
.

Theorem A.2
.

Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and suppose that
T_{1},T_{2}
are linear
N\times N
block operator matrices defined on
[L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}
, such that for each
1\leqslant j,k\leqslant N
,
T_{2,j,k}
pointwise dominates
T_{1,j,k}
. Then the following items
(i)
–
(iii)
hold:

(i)
If
T_{2}\in{\mathcal{B}}\big([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}\big)
then
T_{1}\in{\mathcal{B}}\big([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}\big)
and

\|T_{1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N})}\leqslant\|T_{2}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N})}.

(A.6)

(ii)
If
T_{2}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}\big([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}\big)
then
T_{1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}\big([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}\big)
and

\|T_{1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N})}\leqslant\|T_{2}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N})}.

(A.7)

(iii)
If
T_{2}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}\big([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}\big)
then
T_{1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}\big([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}\big)
and

\|T_{1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N})}\leqslant\|T_{2}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N})}.

(A.8)

Proof.

For item
(ii)
we refer to
[
51
]
and
[
129
]
(see also
[
112
]
) combined with (
A.5
) as we will not use it in this manuscript. While the proofs of items
(i)
and
(iii)
are obviously well-known, we briefly recall them here as we will be using these facts in Sections
5
and
6
. Starting with item
(i)
, we introduce the notation
f=(f_{1},\dots,f_{N})\in[L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}
and

|f|=(|f_{1}|,\dots,|f_{N}|)\in[L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}
and compute,

\displaystyle\|T_{1}f\|^{2}_{[L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\|(T_{1}f)_{j}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(M;d\mu)}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}((T_{1}f)_{j},(T_{1}f)_{j})_{L^{2}(M;d\mu)}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\bigg|\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{N}(T_{1,j,k}f_{k},T_{1,j,\ell}f_{\ell})_{L^{2}(M;d\mu)}\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{N}|(T_{1,j,k}f_{k},T_{1,j,\ell}f_{\ell})_{L^{2}(M;d\mu)}|

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{N}(|T_{1,j,k}f_{k}|,|T_{1,j,\ell}f_{\ell}|)_{L^{2}(M;d\mu)}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{N}(T_{2,j,k}|f_{k}|,T_{2,j,\ell}|f_{\ell}|)_{L^{2}(M;d\mu)}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j=1}^{N}((T_{2}|f|)_{j},(T_{2}|f|)_{j})_{L^{2}(M;d\mu)}=\|T_{2}|f|\|^{2}_{[L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\|T_{2}\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(M;d\mu)^{N})}\||f|\|^{2}_{[L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}}

\displaystyle\quad=\|T_{2}\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(M;d\mu)^{N})}\|f\|^{2}_{[L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N}},

(A.9)

implying item
(i)
. For item
(iii)
we recall from
[
158
, Theorem 2.13]
that

T_{1,j,k}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}\big(L^{2}(M;d\mu)\big)
,
1\leqslant j,k\leqslant N
, and

\|T_{1,j,k}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L^{2}(M;d\mu))}\leqslant\|T_{2,j,k}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L^{2}(M;d\mu))}
,
1\leqslant j,k\leqslant N
, and hence
by (
A.2
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|T_{1}\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N})}&=\sum_{j,k=1}^{N}\|T_{1,j,k}\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L^{2}(M;d\mu))}\leqslant\sum_{j,k=1}^{N}\|T_{2,j,k}\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L^{2}(M;d\mu))}\\
&=\|T_{2}\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}([L^{2}(M;d\mu)]^{N})}.\end{split}

(A.10)

∎

Remark A.3
.

We complete this appendix with the observation that the subordination assumption

|(Af)(\,\cdot\,)|\leqslant(B|f|)(\,\cdot\,)

\mu
-a.e. on
M
, if
A
and
B
are integral operators in

L^{2}(M;d\mu)
with integral kernels
A(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
and
B(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
, respectively,
is implied by the condition

|A(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)|\leqslant B(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)

\mu\otimes\mu
-a.e. on
M\times M
since

\displaystyle|(Af)(x)|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{M}d\mu(y)\,A(x,y)f(y))\bigg|\leqslant\int_{M}d\mu(y)\,|A(x,y)||f(y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{M}d\mu(y)\,B(x,y)|f(y)|=(B|f|)(x)|\,\text{ for a.e.~$x\in M$.}

(A.11)

In fact, the converse is true as well as shown next in a concrete situation.

\diamond

Lemma A.4
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
open,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, suppose
(\Omega,\Sigma,d\sigma)
represents the standard Lebesgue measure on
\Omega

(
i.e.,
d\sigma=d^{n}x
)
, and denote the Lebesgue measure of a set

S\in\Sigma
by
|S|
. Consider bounded, linear integral operators
A,B\in{\mathcal{B}}\big(L^{2}(\Omega;d^{n}x)\big)
, with integral kernels
A(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
and
B(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
, respectively. Then

B
pointwise dominates
A
, that is,

\displaystyle\quad|(Af)(\,\cdot\,)|\leqslant(B|f|)(\,\cdot\,)\,\text{ $\sigma$-a.e.~on $\Omega$ for all
$f\in L^{2}(\Omega;d^{n}x)$,}

(A.12)

if and only if

\displaystyle\quad|A(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)|\leqslant B(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)\,\text{ $\sigma\otimes\sigma$-a.e.~on
$\Omega\times\Omega$.}

(A.13)

Proof.

That (
A.13
) implies (
A.12
) has just been shown in (
A.11
).

To prove the converse, suppose (
A.12
) holds. Since the operators
A
and
B
are bounded operators on
L^{2}(\Omega;d^{n}x)
, it follows from Tonelli’s theorem that the integral kernels
A(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
and
B(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
are locally integrable functions on
\Omega\times\Omega
(with respect to
\sigma\otimes\sigma
).

Let
x,y\in\Omega
be arbitrary and let
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. For
\varepsilon>0
consider the open ball ball
B_{\varepsilon}(x)\subset\Omega
of radius
\varepsilon
with the center at
x\in\Omega
. The fact

f_{\varepsilon,x}=\chi_{B_{\varepsilon}(x)}(\cdot)\in L^{2}(\Omega;d^{n}x)
and assumption (
A.12
) imply that

\Big|\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(x)}d^{n}x^{\prime}\,A(x^{\prime},y)\Big|=\big|(Af_{\varepsilon,x})(y)\big|\leqslant(B|f_{\varepsilon,x}|)(y)\leqslant\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(x)}d^{n}x^{\prime}\,B(x^{\prime},y),

(A.14)

for
\sigma
-a.e.
y\in\Omega
. Since
\text{\rm Re}\big(e^{i\alpha}z\big)\leqslant|z|
for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, it follows that

\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(x)}d^{n}x^{\prime}\,\text{\rm Re}\big(e^{i\alpha}A(x^{\prime},y)\big)\leqslant\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(x)}d^{n}x^{\prime}\,B(x^{\prime},y),

(A.15)

for
\sigma
-a.e.
y\in\Omega
. Integrating inequality (
A.15
) over the ball
B_{\varepsilon}(y)
implies

\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(y)}d^{n}y^{\prime}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(x)}d^{n}x^{\prime}\,\text{\rm Re}\big(e^{i\alpha}A(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big)\leqslant\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(y)}d^{n}y^{\prime}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(x)}d^{n}x^{\prime}\,B(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}).

(A.16)

Since
A(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
and
B(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
are locally integrable functions, an application of Fubini’s theorem yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{|B_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)|^{2}}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(x)\times B_{\varepsilon}(y)}d(\sigma\otimes\sigma)(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\,\text{\rm Re}\big(e^{i\alpha}A(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big)\\
\leqslant\frac{1}{|B_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)|^{2}}\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(x)\times B_{\varepsilon}(y)}d(\sigma\otimes\sigma)(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\,B(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}).\end{split}

(A.17)

Moreover, since both
A(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
and
B(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
are locally integrable functions, it follows that almost every point
(x,y)\in\Omega\times\Omega
is a Lebesgue point for
B(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
and for
\text{\rm Re}\big(e^{i\alpha}A(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)\big)
. Hence, letting
\varepsilon\to 0
in inequality (
A.17
), one infers that for any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Q}}

\text{\rm Re}\big(e^{i\alpha}A(x,y)\big)\leqslant B(x,y),

(A.18)

for
\sigma\otimes\sigma
-a.e.
(x,y)\in\Omega\times\Omega
.
Taking the supremum over all
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q},
one obtains

|A(x,y)|\leqslant B(x,y),

(A.19)

for
\sigma\otimes\sigma
-a.e.
(x,y)\in\Omega\times\Omega
, proving (
A.13
).
∎

Appendix B
Asymptotic Results for Hankel Functions

In this appendix we collect asymptotic results for Hankel functions in the regions of large and small arguments. To set the stage, we recall some details on the analytic behavior of
H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\,\cdot\,)

(cf.
[
1
, p. 358–360]
):

\displaystyle H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\zeta)=J_{\nu}(\zeta)+iY_{\nu}(\zeta),\quad\nu\in{\mathbb{C}},\;\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0\},

(B.1)

\displaystyle J_{\nu}(\zeta)=(\zeta/2)^{\nu}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}[k!\Gamma(\nu+k+1)]^{-1}(-1)^{k}(\zeta/2)^{2k},

(B.2)

\displaystyle Y_{\nu}(\zeta)=[\sin(\nu\pi)]^{-1}[J_{\nu}(\zeta)\cos(\nu\pi)-J_{-\nu}(\zeta)],

(B.3)

\displaystyle Y_{n}(\zeta)=-\pi^{-1}(\zeta/2)^{-n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}[k!]^{-1}[(n-k-1)!](\zeta/2\big)^{2k}+2\pi^{-1}J_{n}(\zeta)\text{\rm ln}(\zeta/2)

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt-\pi^{-1}(\zeta/2)^{n}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}[\psi(k+1)+\psi(n+k+1)][k!(n+k)!]^{-1}(-1)^{k}(\zeta/2)^{2k},

\displaystyle\hskip 278.83708ptn\in{\mathbb{N}},

(B.4)

\displaystyle Y_{0}(\zeta)=\frac{2}{\pi}\{\text{\rm ln}(\zeta/2)+\gamma_{E-M}\}J_{0}(\zeta)-\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\ell}\bigg)\frac{(-4)^{-k}\zeta^{2k}}{(k!)^{2}},

(B.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&J_{-n}(\zeta)=(-1)^{n}J_{n}(\zeta),\quad Y_{-n}(\zeta)=(-1)^{n}Y_{n}(\zeta),\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}},\\
&H_{-\nu}^{(1)}(\zeta)=e^{i\nu\pi}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\zeta),\end{split}

(B.6)

where (cf.
[
1
, p. 256]
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\psi(\zeta)=\Gamma^{\prime}(\zeta)/\Gamma(\zeta),\\
&\psi(1)=-\gamma_{E-M},\quad\psi(\ell)=-\gamma_{E-M}+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell-1}k^{-1},\end{split}

(B.7)

and

\gamma_{E-M}:=\lim_{m\to\infty}\bigg(-\text{\rm ln}(m)+\sum_{k=1}^{m}k^{-1}\bigg)=0.5772156649\,.....

(B.8)

denotes the Euler–Mascheroni constant (cf.
[
1
, p. 255]
). We also recall the asymptotic behavior
(cf.
[
1
, p. 360]
,
[
98
, p. 723–724]
)

\displaystyle H_{0}^{(1)}(\zeta)\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta\to 0\\
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0\}\end{subarray}}{=}(2i/\pi)\text{\rm ln}(\zeta)+O\big(|\text{\rm ln}(\zeta)||\zeta|^{2}\big),

(B.9)

\displaystyle H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\zeta)\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta\to 0\\
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0\}\end{subarray}}{=}-(i/\pi)2^{\nu}\Gamma(\nu)\zeta^{-\nu}+\begin{cases}O\big(|\zeta|^{\min(\nu,-\nu+2)}\big),&\nu\notin{\mathbb{N}},\\
O\big(|\text{\rm ln}(\zeta)||\zeta|^{\nu}\big)+O\big(\zeta^{-\nu+2}\big),&\nu\in{\mathbb{N}},\end{cases}

(B.10)

\displaystyle\hskip 260.91171pt\text{\rm Re}(\nu)>0,

\displaystyle H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\zeta)\underset{\zeta\to\infty}{=}(2/\pi)^{1/2}\zeta^{-1/2}e^{i[\zeta-(\nu\pi/2)-(\pi/4)]},\quad\nu\geqslant 0,\;\text{\rm Im}(\zeta)\geqslant 0.

(B.11)

B.1.
Asymptotics of
H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\zeta)
as
|\zeta|\to\infty

Hypothesis B.1
.

Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
\text{\rm Re}(\nu+(1/2))>0
.

Assuming Hypothesis
B.1
, the Hankel function
H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\,\cdot\,)
permits the following representation (cf., e.g.,
[
87
, 8.421.9]
,
[
180
, 6.12(3)]
)

H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\zeta)=\bigg(\frac{2}{\pi\zeta}\bigg)^{1/2}\frac{e^{i[\zeta-(\pi/2)\nu-(\pi/4)]}}{\Gamma(\nu+(1/2))}\int_{0}^{\infty}du\,e^{-u}u^{\nu-(1/2)}\bigg(1+\frac{iu}{2\zeta}\bigg)^{\nu-(1/2)},

(B.12)

where
\text{\rm Re}(\nu+(1/2))>0
and
-\pi/2<\arg(\zeta)<3\pi/2
. We will derive the asymptotic behavior of
H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\zeta)
as
|\zeta|\to\infty
closely following the presentation given in
[
180
, Sect. 7.2]
.

The factor in parentheses in the integrand in (
B.12
) may be expanded for any
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
according to

\displaystyle\bigg(1+\frac{iu}{2\zeta}\bigg)^{\nu-(1/2)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{m}}{m!}\bigg(\frac{u}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{m}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{p}}{(p-1)!}\bigg(\frac{u}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{p}\int_{0}^{1}dt\,(1-t)^{p-1}\bigg(1-\frac{ut}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{\nu-p-(1/2)},

(B.13)

where we have employed the Pochhammer symbol,

(a)_{n}=\frac{\Gamma(a+n)}{\Gamma(a)},\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}},\;a\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0,-1,-2,-3,\ldots\}.

(B.14)

We shall assume for convenience that
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
is chosen sufficiently large to guarantee that

\text{\rm Re}(\nu-p-(1/2))\leqslant 0
, and we will comment on how to remove this restriction later. Next, fix
an angle
\delta\in(0,\pi/2)
which satisfies

|\arg(\zeta)-(\pi/2)|\leqslant\pi-\delta.

(B.15)

With
\delta
so chosen, one infers

\bigg|1-\frac{ut}{2i\zeta}\bigg|\geqslant\sin(\delta),\quad\bigg|\arg\bigg(1-\frac{ut}{2i\zeta}\bigg)\bigg|<\pi,

(B.16)

for all
t\in[0,1]
and all
u\in(0,\infty)
. In particular,

\bigg|\bigg(1-\frac{ut}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{\nu-p-(1/2)}\bigg|\leqslant e^{\pi|\text{\rm Im}(\nu)|}[\sin(\delta)]^{\text{\rm Re}(\nu-p-(1/2))}=:C_{\nu,p,\delta},

(B.17)

where
C_{\nu,p,\delta}
is independent of
\zeta
. Using the expansion (
B.13
) in (
B.12
), one obtains

\displaystyle H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{2}{\pi\zeta}\bigg)^{1/2}\frac{e^{i[\zeta-(\pi/2)\nu-(\pi/4)]}}{\Gamma(\nu+(1/2))}

\displaystyle\quad\times\int_{0}^{\infty}du\,e^{-u}u^{\nu-(1/2)}\Bigg[\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{m}}{m!}\bigg(\frac{u}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{m}

\displaystyle\hskip 54.06006pt+\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{p}}{(p-1)!}\bigg(\frac{u}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{p}\int_{0}^{1}dt\,(1-t)^{p-1}\bigg(1-\frac{ut}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{\nu-p-(1/2)}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{2}{\pi\zeta}\bigg)^{1/2}\frac{e^{i[\zeta-(\pi/2)\nu-(\pi/4)]}}{\Gamma(\nu+(1/2))}\Bigg[\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{m}\Gamma(\nu+m+(1/2))}{m!(2i\zeta)^{m}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{p}}{(p-1)!}\int_{0}^{\infty}du\,e^{-u}u^{\nu-(1/2)}\bigg(\frac{u}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{p}

\displaystyle\hskip 112.38829pt\times\int_{0}^{1}dt\,(1-t)^{p-1}\bigg(1-\frac{ut}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{\nu-p-(1/2)}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{2}{\pi\zeta}\bigg)^{1/2}e^{i[\zeta-(\pi/2)\nu-(\pi/4)]}\Bigg[\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{m}(\nu+(1/2))_{m}}{m!(2i\zeta)^{m}}+R_{\nu,p}^{(1)}(\zeta)\Bigg],

(B.18)

where

\displaystyle R_{\nu,p}^{(1)}(\zeta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{p}}{(p-1)!\Gamma(\nu+(1/2))}

(B.19)

\displaystyle\quad\times\int_{0}^{\infty}du\,e^{-u}u^{\nu-(1/2)}\bigg(\frac{u}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{p}\int_{0}^{1}dt\,(1-t)^{p-1}\bigg(1-\frac{ut}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{\nu-p-(1/2)}.

One observes that

\displaystyle|R_{\nu,p}^{(1)}(\zeta)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C_{\nu,p,\delta}}{(p-1)!}\bigg|\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{p}}{\Gamma(\nu+(1/2))(2i\zeta)^{p}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg[\int_{0}^{1}dt\,(1-t)^{p-1}\bigg]\bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-u}|u^{\nu+p-(1/2)}|\,du\bigg]

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{\nu,p,\delta}|\zeta|^{-p}.

(B.20)

As a consequence of (
B.18
), (
B.19
), and (
B.20
), one infers that for any fixed
\delta\in(0,\pi/2)
,

\displaystyle H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\zeta)\underset{|\zeta|\to\infty}{=}\bigg(\frac{2}{\pi\zeta}\bigg)^{1/2}e^{i[\zeta-(\pi/2)\nu-(\pi/4)]}\Bigg[\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{m}(\nu+(1/2))_{m}}{m!(2i\zeta)^{m}}

(B.21)

\displaystyle\hskip 64.01869pt+O(|\zeta|^{-p})\Bigg],\quad|\arg(\zeta)-(\pi/2)|\leqslant\pi-\delta.

To obtain similar expansions when
\text{\rm Re}(\nu-p-(1/2))>0
, one chooses
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
so large that
\text{\rm Re}(\nu-q-(1/2))<0
, which requires
p<q
. Then (
B.18
), (
B.19
), (
B.20
), and (
B.21
) hold with
p
replaced by
q
. In particular, by (
B.18
), for any fixed
\delta\in(0,\pi/2)
,

\displaystyle H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{2}{\pi\zeta}\bigg)^{1/2}e^{i[\zeta-(\pi/2)\nu-(\pi/4)]}\Bigg[\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{m}(\nu+(1/2))_{m}}{m!(2i\zeta)^{m}}+R_{\nu,q}^{(1)}(\zeta)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{2}{\pi\zeta}\bigg)^{1/2}e^{i[\zeta-(\pi/2)\nu-(\pi/4)]}\Bigg[\sum_{m=0}^{p-1}\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{m}(\nu+(1/2))_{m}}{m!(2i\zeta)^{m}}+\widetilde{R}_{\nu,q}^{(1)}(\zeta)\Bigg],

\displaystyle\hskip 173.56198pt|\arg(\zeta)-(\pi/2)|\leqslant\pi-\delta,

(B.22)

where

\widetilde{R}_{\nu,p,q}^{(1)}(\zeta)=\sum_{m=p}^{q-1}\frac{((1/2)-\nu)_{m}(\nu+(1/2))_{m}}{m!(2i\zeta)^{m}}+R_{\nu,q}^{(1)}(\zeta).

(B.23)

The following lemma provides sufficient conditions for the differentiability of an integral depending on a complex parameter.

Lemma B.2

(
[
118
]
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{M}},\mu)
be a measure space, let
G\subset{\mathbb{C}}
be an open set, and let
f:G\times X\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a function which satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

f(\zeta,\,\cdot\,)
is
{\mathcal{M}}
-measurable for every
\zeta\in G
,

(ii)

f(\,\cdot\,,x)
is holomorphic in
G
for every
x\in X
, and

(iii)

\int_{X}d\mu\,|f(\,\cdot\,,x)|
is locally bounded; that is, for every
\zeta_{0}\in G
, there exists
\varepsilon(\zeta_{0})>0
such that

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta\in G\\
|\zeta-\zeta_{0}|\leqslant\varepsilon(\zeta_{0})\end{subarray}}\int_{X}d\mu\,|f(\zeta,x)|<\infty.

(B.24)

Then
\int_{X}d\mu\,f(\,\cdot\,,x)
is holomorphic in
G
and

\frac{d^{n}}{d\zeta^{n}}\int_{X}d\mu\,f(\zeta,x)=\int_{X}d\mu\,\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial\zeta^{n}}f(\zeta,x)\quad\text{in $G$ for every $n\in{\mathbb{N}}$.}

(B.25)

Proposition B.3
.

Assume Hypothesis
B.1
. Let
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
u\in(0,\infty)
, and suppose
\text{\rm Re}(\nu-p-(1/2))\leqslant 0
. If

\Omega_{0}:=\{\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\,|\,|\arg(\zeta)-(\pi/2)|<\pi\},

(B.26)

then the function
a_{u,p,\nu}:\Omega_{0}\to{\mathbb{C}}
defined by

a_{u,p,\nu}(\zeta)=\int_{0}^{1}dt\,(1-t)^{p}\bigg(1-\frac{ut}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{\nu-p-(1/2)},\quad\zeta\in\Omega_{0},

(B.27)

is analytic in
\Omega_{0}
and

\frac{d^{n}}{d\zeta^{n}}a_{u,p,\nu}(\zeta)=\int_{0}^{1}dt\,(1-t)^{p}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial^{n}\zeta}\bigg(1-\frac{ut}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{\nu-p-(1/2)},\quad\zeta\in\Omega_{0}.

(B.28)

Proof.

Let
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
u\in(0,\infty)
,
\nu\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
\text{\rm Re}(\nu-p-(1/2))\leqslant 0
. It suffices to apply Lemma
B.2
to the function

f_{u,p,\nu}(\zeta,t)=(1-t)^{p}\bigg(1-\frac{ut}{2i\zeta}\bigg)^{\nu-p-(1/2)},\quad\zeta\in\Omega_{0},\;t\in(0,1).

(B.29)

Of course, (
B.29
) defines a function which is Lebesgue measurable for each
\zeta\in\Omega_{0}
and analytic in
\Omega_{0}
for every
t\in(0,1)
. Therefore, it remains to verify condition
(iii)
in Lemma
B.2
. To this end, let
\zeta_{0}\in\Omega_{0}
. Choose
\delta\in(0,\pi/2)
such that

\zeta_{0}\in\Omega_{\delta}:=\{\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\,|\,|\arg(\zeta)-(\pi/2)|<\pi-\delta\}.

(B.30)

By (
B.17
), one then infers

|a_{u,p,\nu}(\zeta)|=\bigg|\int_{0}^{1}dt\,f_{u,p,\nu}(\zeta,t)\bigg|\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}dt\,|f_{u,p,\nu}(\zeta,t)|\leqslant\frac{C_{\nu,p,\delta}}{p},\quad\zeta\in\Omega_{\delta}.

(B.31)

In particular, choosing
\varepsilon(\zeta_{0})\in(0,1)
so small that

\{\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\,|\,|\zeta-\zeta_{0}|<\varepsilon(\zeta_{0})\}\subset\Omega_{\delta},

(B.32)

one concludes

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta\in\Omega_{0}\\
